# Sūrah Fāṭir (Chapter 35) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Named After the First Word of God's Self-Description**

Sūrah Fāṭir is the thirty-fifth chapter of the Qur'an, consisting of forty-five verses. It takes its name from the very first descriptive word God applies to Himself in it: *Fāṭir al-samāwāt wa'l-arḍ*, "the Originator of the heavens and the earth." The name is not a later editorial label but grows out of the text itself, which is why the tradition also calls it *al-Malāʾikah*, "The Angels," after the phrase in the same opening verse: *jāʿil al-malāʾikati rusulan*, "Who appoints the angels as messengers." Both names are drawn from the same breath of the same verse, and both point to what the whole chapter is doing: naming God by His creative act, and naming the creatures — visible and invisible — that serve within it.

The sūrah is Makkan, and according to the received order of revelation it was sent down immediately after Sūrah 25, al-Furqān. Some authorities single out verses 27 and 32, and others verses 29 and 32, as belonging to the Madinan period; but the dominant judgment, and the one the sūrah's own argument confirms, is that Fāṭir is a Makkan discourse from beginning to end. That matters for how we read it. Makkan revelation rarely legislates. It does not lay down rules of inheritance or contracts or judicial procedure. It does something more fundamental first: it establishes who God is, who the human being is, and what happens after death. Fāṭir is a textbook of that method. Its concerns are the Oneness of God as sole Creator and Sustainer, the reality of resurrection, the enmity of Satan, the difference between the believer and the disbeliever, and the moral law by which every deed returns upon its doer.

**One of the Five Chapters That Open With Praise**

Five chapters of the Qur'an open with the words *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*, "Praise be to God": al-Fātiḥah (1:2), al-Anʿām (6:1), al-Kahf (18:1), Sabaʾ (34:1), and Fāṭir (35:1). The clustering is not accidental, and the commentators noticed it early. Each of these five sūrahs is a sūrah about God's relationship to the created order: al-Anʿām about the cattle and the produce and the argument from provision; al-Kahf about the Book itself and the stories that show God governing time; Sabaʾ about a kingdom given and taken away; and Fāṭir about the origins of the heavens, the earth, and the angels. Praise is the appropriate opening for all of them because praise is the appropriate response to creation. When a verse begins with *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*, it is telling the reader what the correct posture is before any argument begins: not that of an examiner auditing a claim, but of a beneficiary acknowledging a gift.

The relationship between Fāṭir and the sūrah before it is particularly close. Sūrah 34, Sabaʾ, is its twin in structure and theme: both open with *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*, both belong to the same group of Makkan chapters about God's exclusive lordship, both dwell on the contrast between those who believe and those who are given respite in arrogance, and both close on the note of God's encompassing knowledge. Read one after the other, they sound like two movements of the same composition.

**The Argument of the Chapter**

The sūrah's internal architecture is unusually clear, and it moves in a deliberate rhythm between two poles: the demonstration of God's power, and the contrast between the two kinds of human beings who respond to it.

It begins with an affirmation of God as sole Originator and Provider (vv. 1–3). Verse 1 names Him by creation and by the angels; verse 2 declares that mercy and its withholding are both in His hand alone; verse 3 asks the question the whole chapter will keep asking — *is there any creator other than God who provides for you from heaven and earth?* From there the sūrah turns to the human situation: verse 4 consoles the Prophet against denial, verses 5 and 6 expose the two deceivers — the life of this world and Satan — and verses 7 and 8 draw the first line between the two camps.

Then comes a sustained sequence of natural signs: the winds that raise the clouds and revive the dead land, offered as a proof of resurrection (v. 9); the ascent of the good word and the righteous deed (v. 10); the stages of human creation from dust to drop to pairs, and the recording of every life in a Book (v. 11); the two seas, sweet and salty, from which fish and ornaments are drawn (v. 12); the merging of night into day and the subservience of sun and moon (v. 13). Each sign is followed by the same devastating comparison: the idols own not so much as the husk of a date-stone (vv. 13–14).

The middle of the sūrah (vv. 15–18) states its metaphysical heart in the shortest possible compass: *you are the poor, and God is the Self-Sufficient*. From that single distinction everything else follows — God can remove you and replace you (vv. 16–17); no soul carries another's burden (v. 18). Verses 19–22 then produce what is perhaps the most poetic passage in the chapter: four pairs of unequals — the blind and the seeing, the darkness and the light, the shade and the scorching heat, the living and the dead — an unrhymed but perfectly balanced litany that commentators have long admired.

Verses 23–26 address the Prophet's function: he is only a warner, and every community has had one; those who deny him are following the pattern of those who denied before them, and the pattern ended in seizure. Verses 27–28 return to signs, this time the signs of colour: fruits of diverse colours, mountains streaked white and red and black, mankind and beasts and cattle of diverse colours — and out of that variety comes the sūrah's most famous single statement about knowledge: *only those among His servants who know fear God*.

The longest section (vv. 29–39) sets the two communities side by side at full length: those who recite the Book, pray, and spend, hoping for a commerce that will never perish; and those who disbelieve, for whom the Fire will neither be ended by death nor lightened. The three categories of the people who inherited the Book — those who wrong themselves, those who take a middle course, those who are foremost in good deeds — stand at the centre of this section (v. 32), followed by the Gardens of Eden (vv. 33–35) and their opposite (vv. 36–37). Verses 38–41 then return to God's knowledge, His vicegerency of mankind, and His maintenance of the heavens and the earth lest they fall apart.

The final passage (vv. 42–45) examines the anatomy of disbelief itself: a people who swore their most solemn oaths that they would be better guided than any community if only a warner came, and who, when he came, increased in nothing but aversion, arrogance, and plotting. The sūrah closes with the principle of respite: were God to take mankind to task for what they have earned, He would not leave a single creature upon the surface of the earth.

**The Two Deceptions and the Two Realities**

If one had to compress the chapter's teaching into a single pair of sentences, they would be these: the world is not what it appears to be, and you are not what you appear to be.

Verse 5 names the first deception plainly: *let not the life of this world delude you, nor let the Deluder delude you concerning God*. The Arabic word for the Deluder is *al-gharūr*, and it is one of Satan's functional titles. The word carries the sense of a thing that appears to be one thing and is another — a mirage, a beautifully lit stage set, an offer that reads as a gift and turns out to be a trap. What makes this verse remarkable is that it separates the two deceivers: the world, which deceives by its attractiveness, and Satan, who deceives by his interpretation of God. The world tempts you with what is in it; Satan tempts you with what he says about God's mercy, God's patience, God's indifference. The second deception is more dangerous because it uses the language of the first truth.

Verse 15 then names the reality beneath: *O mankind! You are the poor, and God is the Self-Sufficient*. The word for "poor" here is *fuqarāʾ*, the plural of *faqīr*, and the whole of Islamic spirituality built itself upon this verse. To be created is to be needy — needy of being itself, moment by moment, not merely at the start. The sūrah insists that this is not a defect to be overcome but the definition of the creature, and that God's Self-Sufficiency is not a threat to the creature but the ground on which the creature stands.

**A Sūrah of Contrasts**

A reader who moves through Fāṭir attentively notices that it thinks in pairs. Two seas. Night and day. Sun and moon. The blind and the seeing. Darkness and light. Shade and scorching heat. The living and the dead. The believer and the disbeliever. The Garden and the Fire. What is open and what is withheld. The three groups who inherit the Book and the one group that rejects it. Even God's names come in pairs at the ends of verses: *al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥakīm*, *al-Ghanī al-Ḥamīd*, *al-Ghafūr al-Shakūr*, *al-Ḥalīm al-Ghafūr*, *al-ʿAlīm al-Qadīr*, *al-Khabīr al-Baṣīr*.

This is not decoration. It is a method of teaching. Contrast is how the mind actually learns; we understand light by having known darkness, warmth by cold, safety by risk. The Qur'an uses the technique constantly, and Fāṭir concentrates it into a single chapter so densely that the chapter reads almost as a treatise on the discrimination (*furqān*) between realities that look alike on the surface. The appropriate response, which the sūrah also supplies, is the shiver of recognition described in Sūrah al-Zumar:

> *"God has sent down the best of speech, a Book consistent, repeating, whereat shivers the skins of those who fear their Lord; then their skins and their hearts soften to the remembrance of God."* (Qur'an 39:23)

**How This Commentary Proceeds**

What follows is a verse-by-verse expanded commentary. Every verse of the sūrah is treated as a complete and independent discourse, including those that the source commentary discusses in groups — verses 13 and 14, 16 and 17, 20 and 21 are here separated and given full individual treatment, as the method requires. Each section draws upon the Arabic language and its roots, cross-references to other Qur'anic passages quoted in full, authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections identified, the classical commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, al-Bayḍāwī, Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Tustarī and others), the reasoning behind each teaching, and its bearing upon ordinary human experience. Nothing has been compressed because of the chapter's length, and no verse has been shortened because a neighbouring verse seemed richer. The sūrah that declares *only those among His servants who know fear God* is read here at the pace that knowledge requires — from *"Praise be to God, Originator of the heavens and the earth"* through to *"truly God sees His servants."*

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:1

> **Praise be to God, Originator of the heavens and the earth, Who appoints the angels as messengers, of wings two, three, and four, increasing creation as He will. Truly God is Powerful over all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Chapter Begins Where Existence Begins**

The thirty-fifth chapter of the Qur'an opens with the same words that open five chapters in total, and those words are not a formulaic preamble. *Al-ḥamdu li-llāh* — "Praise be to God" — is a complete sentence in Arabic with a definite subject and a predicate, and the definite article on *al-ḥamd* makes it a statement about the whole genus of praise: all praise, whoever offers it, to whomever it is directed, ultimately belongs to God. The commentators explain that this is why the phrase is stronger than a mere verb. Had the verse said "we praise God," the praise would have been an act performed by a particular speaker at a particular moment, subject to the speaker's mood and limitations. By saying instead "praise belongs to God," the verse removes praise from the ownership of the one who praises and locates it as a permanent property of the Praised. Every genuine admiration anyone feels for anything beautiful, well-made, generous, or true is, whether the admirer knows it or not, an instalment of that praise being paid to its only rightful recipient.

That the chapter then immediately attaches this praise to the act of creation tells us why praise is due. *Al-ḥamdu li-llāh fāṭiri al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — "Praise be to God, Originator of the heavens and the earth." Praise, in other words, is not a mood; it is a response to a fact. The fact is that there is something rather than nothing, and that the something was made.

**Fāṭir: The One Who Cleaves Open**

The Divine Name *Fāṭir* comes from the root *f-ṭ-r*, whose basic sense in classical Arabic is to split, to cleave, to open by force, and hence to originate something by bringing it out of what did not previously contain it. The verb *faṭara* is used of splitting a thing lengthwise, of the first opening of a wound, and of a camel's tooth breaking through the gum for the first time — an image that carries the idea of something emerging that was never there before. When the Qur'an applies it to God, it means that He did not rearrange pre-existing matter; He initiated.

The Name occurs six times in the Qur'an, and always — remarkably — within the same larger phrase, "Originator of the heavens and the earth": here, and at 6:14, 12:101, 14:10, 39:46, and 42:11. In two of those places the phrase carries the weight of an argument:

> *"Say: Shall I take as protector other than God, the Originator of the heavens and the earth, Who feeds and is not fed?"* (Qur'an 6:14)

> *"Or has God, the Originator of the heavens and the earth, taken daughters and chosen sons for you?"* (Qur'an 42:11)

The logic in both is identical and it is the logic of Fāṭir itself: whoever originated the whole of existence cannot have a partner inside that existence, because a partner would have to be either created by Him — and then he is a creature, not a god — or uncreated alongside Him — and then He is not the Originator of everything. The Name is therefore not merely descriptive; it is argumentative. It closes a door.

The same root also appears in connection with the creation of human beings — at 11:51, 17:51, 20:72, 32:22, and 43:27 — and this is what ties *Fāṭir* to the famous word *fiṭrah* in Sūrah al-Rūm:

> *"So set thy face toward the religion, a man of pure faith — God's primordial nature (*fiṭrah*), upon which He originated mankind."* (Qur'an 30:30)

The connection is deeper than etymology. If God is *Fāṭir* of the human being, then the human being is not a blank slate onto which culture writes; he is a thing that was *cleaved open* with a grain already in it. The *fiṭrah* is that grain — the innate disposition to recognise the Creator, to distinguish good from evil, to feel the wrongness of injustice before any teacher explains it. Al-Ṭabarī and the exegetes after him read the verse of al-Rūm this way, and the Prophetic tradition that accompanies it is the one every Muslim parent knows:

> *"Every child is born upon the *fiṭrah*, and it is his parents who make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian."* (al-Bukhārī 1358; Muslim 2658)

Fāṭir, then, describes a God who does not merely start things but starts them *with a direction in them*.

**The Cleaving of Heaven and Earth**

Al-Rāzī, whose commentary on this sūrah is one of its most sustained treatments, develops the literal sense of "Cleaver" in an eschatological key. God cleaves open the heavens to make way for the descent of spirits into bodies at conception and birth, and He cleaves open the earth for the ascension of human spirits at the moment of resurrection. Read this way, *Fāṭir* is not only a Name of first creation but of the two great crossings — in and out. The Qur'an elsewhere uses the very same imagery of cleaving:

> *"On the Day when the heaven is cleft open with the clouds, and the angels are sent down in a great descent."* (Qur'an 25:25)

> *"And the earth is stretched out, and casts forth what is within it and turns empty."* (Qur'an 84:3–4)

This reading connects directly to the second half of the verse, because the angels are precisely the agents of those crossings — the ones who carry the command down and the soul up. It is why, in al-Rāzī's treatment, the mention of the angels is not an ornamental addition to a statement about creation but the mechanism of it.

**"Who Appoints the Angels as Messengers"**

The phrase *jāʿil al-malāʾikati rusulan* uses the active participle *jāʿil* — "the One who makes, appoints, constitutes" — rather than a past-tense verb. The grammatical choice is significant: this is not a historical event that happened once and finished, but a standing appointment. The angels are, continuously and presently, messengers.

*Rusul* is the plural of *rasūl*, "one sent with a message," the same word used for the human prophets. That the Qur'an applies it to angels establishes a single channel of communication between the unseen and the seen, in which the same office is discharged by beings of two entirely different orders. Ibn Kathīr emphasises the revelatory function: the angels are messengers because they carry revelation to the prophets. Al-Rāzī adds the cosmological function: they convey spirits between heaven and earth. Both are supported by the text elsewhere — Gabriel bringing the Qur'an down:

> *"Say: Whoever is an enemy to Gabriel — it is he who brings it down upon thy heart by God's Leave, confirming what came before it, a guidance and glad tidings for the believers."* (Qur'an 2:97)

and the angels of death taking the soul up:

> *"Say: The Angel of Death who is entrusted with you will take you; then unto your Lord you will be returned."* (Qur'an 32:11)

Belief in the angels is not a peripheral doctrine in Islam but the second article of faith, listed immediately after belief in God in the creedal formula of Sūrah al-Baqarah: *"The Messenger believes in what was sent down to him from his Lord, as do the believers; each believes in God, His angels, His Books, and His messengers"* (2:285). What Fāṭir contributes to that article is unique in the entire Qur'an: it is the only place that describes any aspect of the angels' physical form.

**"Of Wings Two, Three, and Four"**

*Ulī ajniḥatin mathnā wa-thulātha wa-rubāʿ* — "possessors of wings, two, three, and four." The words *mathnā*, *thulāth*, and *rubāʿ* belong to a rare Arabic grammatical category: the distributive or repetitive numerals (*ʿadad al-maʿdūl*), which express "two at a time, three at a time, four at a time" rather than a simple total. Grammarians note that these forms are structurally fixed and never take the definite article, which is exactly how they appear here. The effect is of enumeration by variety: some with pairs, some with trios, some with fours — a catalogue of differing forms, not an arithmetic sum.

Why does the Qur'an mention wings at all? Al-Zamakhsharī and those who follow him explain that wings (*ajniḥah*) indicate speed, ascent and descent, and the capacity to traverse distances that bodies cannot — the wing is the sign of a being whose movement is not limited by the medium. Al-Qurṭubī adds that the specification of numbers is a rhetorical device for demonstrating that the unseen order is itself structured, differentiated, and ranked, rather than a formless mass of spirits. A hierarchy of two, three, and four implies difference of capacity, of station, and of task.

The Prophet's own descriptions confirm that the numbers in this verse are a floor, not a ceiling. Al-Bukhārī records from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ saw Gabriel with six hundred wings."* (al-Bukhārī 3232; also 4856)

And Muslim records the vision in which the wings fill the horizon:

> *"I saw Gabriel, and he had six hundred wings, and from his wings there fell dust like the drops of pearl."* (Muslim 174, from Ibn Masʿūd)

Ibn Kathīr brings these reports to this verse precisely to establish the principle: the Qur'an says two, three, and four, and then adds *increasing creation as He will* — so no report of a greater number contradicts the verse; the verse has already authorised it.

Muslim also records the Prophet's statement about what the angels are made of, which helps explain how they can be simultaneously invisible and structured:

> *"The angels were created from light, the jinn were created from a smokeless flame of fire, and Adam was created from what has been described to you."* (Muslim 2996, from ʿĀʾishah)

**"Increasing Creation as He Will"**

*Yazīdu fī al-khalqi mā yashāʾ* admits two readings, and the commentators hold both. The first is narrow: God increases the number of the angels, or their wings, as He wills — the increase belongs to the sentence about them. The second is broad: God increases whomever and whatever He wills in creation, period — the sentence then becomes a general principle standing after a particular example. Al-Ṭabarī prefers the general reading because the phrase *fī al-khalq*, "in creation," is unrestricted, and an unrestricted phrase should not be narrowed without compelling evidence.

The general reading is the more powerful one, and the tradition has applied it to everything from physical stature to beauty to voice to intellect to hair. Ibn ʿAbbās and others are reported to have understood it as including beauty of face and voice; al-Zamakhsharī mentions it as a proof that excellence in created things is not distributed equally and that this inequality is not injustice but the signature of a Creator who gives as He wills. A person who reads the verse this way hears in it both a warning against envy and an instruction in gratitude: what you have been given in your appearance, your capacities, and your circumstances is a specific allocation from an unlimited Giver, and the Giver is not obliged to make the allocations equal.

**The Closing Name: Power Over All Things**

*Inna Allāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* — "Truly God is Powerful over all things." The verse began with origination and ends with capacity, and the two are the same truth seen from two angles. Having described the angels with numbers of wings that the human imagination cannot picture, and having declared that God increases creation as He wills, the verse closes by refusing to let the reader treat any of this as mythology. The point is not that angels have four wings; the point is that the One who made them is not constrained by anything, and that the reader's inability to picture the result is a fact about the reader, not about the Maker.

The practical effect of a chapter opening this way is worth stating plainly. Before Fāṭir argues against idols, before it warns against Satan, before it contrasts the believer and the disbeliever, it establishes scale. Everything that follows in the sūrah — the idols that own not the husk of a date-stone (v. 13), Satan who has no authority except invitation (v. 6), the human being who is poor and needy (v. 15) — is measured against the opening verse. The reader who has not felt the weight of *"Praise be to God, Originator of the heavens and the earth, Who appoints the angels as messengers"* will not feel the absurdity of worshipping a carved stone.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:2

> **Whatsoever mercy God opens unto mankind, none shall withhold. And whatsoever He withholds, none shall release thereafter. And He is the Mighty, the Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb "To Open": A Door That Only Has One Key**

The governing image of this verse is a door. The Arabic verb is *yaftaḥ*, from the root *f-t-ḥ*, "to open" — the same root as *fatḥ*, meaning both opening and victory and judicial decision, and the same root as *al-Fattāḥ*, one of God's Names:

> *"Say: Our Lord will bring us together, then He will judge between us with truth; and He is the Opener (*al-Fattāḥ*), the Knowing."* (Qur'an 34:26)

Mercy here is therefore not described as something poured out from an inexhaustible but open-handed source; it is described as something *released through a door*, and the verse's entire argument rests on the fact that there is only one hand on that door. *Mā yaftaḥ Allāh li-l-nās min raḥmatin fa-lā mumsika lahā* — "Whatsoever mercy God opens unto mankind, none shall withhold it."

Al-Ṭabarī connects this directly to the verses that speak of God's ownership of the treasuries:

> *"Unto Him belong the keys of the heavens and the earth. He outspreads and straitens provision for whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 42:12)

> *"And He it is Who has made you successors upon the earth... Say: Have you considered, if God should take away your hearing and your sight and set a seal upon your hearts — which god apart from God would bring it back to you?"* (Qur'an 6:46, in context of the same argument)

Ibn Kathīr reads the verse as a general principle with a single meaning: no one can hold back what God wills to give, and no one can release what He wills to withhold. He ties it to the almost identical statement in Sūrah Yūnus:

> *"And if God should touch thee with affliction, none can remove it save He; and if He desires some good for thee, none can hold back His Bounty."* (Qur'an 10:107)

The word *mumsik* in the verse is the active participle of *amsaka*, "to hold, to grip, to restrain, to hold back." It is the image of a hand closed around something. The verse says: if God opens, there is no closed hand anywhere in existence that can shut it again.

**Why Mercy Is Mentioned First**

The order of the two clauses is deliberate, and the commentators drew theological weight from it. God's opening of mercy is stated first; His withholding second. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī connect this to the famous ḥadīth qudsī in which the Prophet ﷺ reported God's own words:

> *"When God created the creation, He wrote in His Book — which is with Him above the Throne — 'Truly My Mercy prevails over My Wrath.'"* (al-Bukhārī 7404; Muslim 2751)

In another wording of the same report: *"My Mercy precedes My Wrath."* The precedence is not merely chronological; it is a statement about which of the two is God's primary mode of dealing with creation. Wrath is real, just, and promised — the sūrah itself will describe the Fire at length in verses 36 and 37 — but it is not the first thing. The first thing is the open door.

There is a further logical point in the ordering. The verse moves from what God does to what God refrains from doing, and by putting the positive act first it establishes that withholding is never the baseline. Every default in creation is gift. A reader who begins from this premise reads the rest of the verse differently: when a door stays shut, it is a specific decision within a general disposition of giving, not evidence of a hostile universe.

**"None Shall Release Thereafter" — Two Readings of One Phrase**

The second clause, *wa-mā yumsik fa-lā mursila lahā min baʿdih*, contains an ambiguity the exegetes exploited rather than resolved. *Min baʿdih* can mean "after it" — that is, after God's withholding — or "after Him." Al-Rāzī prefers the second: no one can dispense what God has chosen to withhold, because no one acts after Him in the sense of operating independently of His will. The first reading gives the same practical conclusion with a different emphasis: once God has withheld, no later intervention by anyone can force the release.

Either way, the verse removes the category of the independent second cause. There is no person, institution, angel, saint, charm, or effort in the universe that stands outside God's action and can override it. This is the theological core of *tawakkul* (reliance), and it is not, as is sometimes supposed, a counsel of passivity. The verse does not say "do nothing"; it says the outcome of what you do is not in your hands. Those are different propositions with different psychological effects. A person who believes the outcome is his to control becomes anxious; a person who believes the outcome belongs to God becomes responsible without being anxious. That distinction is one of the most practically useful in the whole of Islamic spirituality.

**The Verse in the Mouth of the Prophet Every Day After Prayer**

The strongest evidence that this verse is not an abstract metaphysical claim but a lived sentence is that the Prophet ﷺ turned its meaning into a daily supplication recited at the end of every obligatory prayer. Al-Mughīrah ibn Shuʿbah reported:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ used to say at the end of every obligatory prayer: 'O God, none can withhold what You have given, and none can give what You have withheld, and no possessor of wealth benefits from his wealth against You.'"* (al-Bukhārī 844; Muslim 593)

Compare the two texts clause by clause. *Whatsoever mercy God opens unto mankind, none shall withhold* corresponds to *"none can withhold what You have given."* *Whatsoever He withholds, none shall release thereafter* corresponds to *"none can give what You have withheld."* The verse of Fāṭir is being recited, in paraphrase, five times a day, by every Muslim who follows the Prophetic practice after prayer. The addition in the ḥadīth — *"no possessor of wealth benefits from his wealth against You"* — supplies the moral: since wealth itself is a mercy God has opened, it cannot be used as leverage against Him.

That the Companions and the generations after them chose these words at the moment when prayer ends is telling. Prayer concludes with a request for something. The supplication reminds the worshipper, at the very moment of asking, that the asking does not change who controls the answer — and that therefore the asking must be accompanied by acceptance of whatever comes.

**What Counts as "Mercy Opened"**

The exegetes enumerate what this mercy includes, and the list is broader than most readers expect. Al-Ṭabarī mentions guidance, provision, rain, health, and knowledge; al-Qurṭubī adds children, safety, and the ability to worship; Ibn Kathīr treats *raḥmah* here as covering both the religious and the worldly. The comprehensive reading is that *raḥmah* in this verse means every good that reaches a human being, in body, mind, circumstance, or soul — because every one of those goods requires an opening.

Consider what the verse implies about the most ordinary experiences of life. The rain that falls on a dry region is a mercy opened. The child born after years of waiting is a mercy opened. The understanding that arrives after years of confusion is a mercy opened. The ability to stand for prayer when the body is willing but weak is a mercy opened. The repentance that a person cannot manufacture on demand is itself a mercy opened — and this is why the verse is so often quoted in discussions of guidance: the heart that turns is turned.

**"And He is the Mighty, the Wise"**

The verse closes with two Names, and their pairing answers the two questions the verse raises. *Al-ʿAzīz*, the Mighty, the Invincible, the One who cannot be overcome: this answers the question "can anyone resist Him?" No. *Al-Ḥakīm*, the Wise, the One whose every act has a purpose and a right measure: this answers the harder question "is what He does just?"

The combination matters. Power without wisdom would mean that the closed door is arbitrary, and the human being could reasonably resent it. Wisdom without power would mean that the intention behind the closed door is good but ineffective, and the human being could reasonably doubt it. Together, *al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥakīm* say: what is withheld is withheld by One who cannot be overruled and who does nothing without a reason you may not presently see.

This is precisely the discipline the verse is training. The human being who has internalised *mā yaftaḥ Allāh* — "what God opens" — stops spending his energy on the two activities that consume most of the anxious mind: trying to force open a door God has shut, and trying to bolt a door God has opened. Neither is possible. What remains is the only thing that ever was possible — asking, working, and accepting.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:3

> **O mankind! Remember God's Blessing upon you. Is there a creator other than God who provides for you from Heaven and earth? There is no god but He. How, then, are you perverted?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Address That Skips Every Intermediate Identity**

The verse opens with *yā ayyuhā al-nās* — "O mankind!" Not "O believers," not "O Children of Israel," not "O Quraysh." The Makkan sūrahs use this address when the message concerns what is true of every human being simply by virtue of being human, and the choice is the first argument of the verse: whatever follows belongs to you because you exist.

Then comes the command: *udhkurū niʿmata Allāhi ʿalaykum* — "Remember God's Blessing upon you." The word *niʿmah* comes from the root *n-ʿ-m*, whose field of meaning includes softness, ease, comfort, and delight; *naʿīm* is the Qur'anic word for the bliss of Paradise, and *niʿmah* is its counterpart in this world — the ease and comfort of a life made livable. The command is in the imperative plural: remember, all of you. Not "learn," not "prove," not "believe" — *remember*.

That verb choice is theologically loaded. One does not command a person to remember something he has never known unless the thing is already before him. The blessing is not hidden information requiring research; it is a state of affairs in which the addressee is currently standing, and which he has failed to notice. The Qur'an's diagnosis of human ingratitude is not usually ignorance but inattention.

Six verses in the Qur'an carry this command in this form: 2:231, 5:7, 5:11, 5:20, 14:6, and this one. Five of the six recall a specific historical blessing given to a specific people; Fāṭir's is the only one addressed to mankind as a whole and grounded not in history but in existence.

**Two Blessings: Being Brought Into Being, and Being Kept**

Al-Rāzī organises his entire commentary on this verse around a distinction he returns to repeatedly. Blessings, he argues, fall into two categories: those of *ījād*, bringing into being, and those of *ibqāʾ*, perpetuation — keeping the thing in being once it exists. He reads the verse as containing both, in order. *"Is there a creator other than God"* addresses *ījād*. *"Who provides for you from heaven and earth"* addresses *ibqāʾ*.

The distinction is philosophically sharp, and it deserves to be stated carefully, because most people naturally assume the second is a continuation of the first. It is not. To be brought into existence is one thing; to continue existing for a further second is another, and nothing in the first guarantees the second. A thing that requires a cause to begin requires a cause to persist — otherwise its persistence would be a second, unexplained gift. The Qur'an makes this explicit elsewhere:

> *"God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Ever-Watchful. Slumber seizes Him not, nor sleep."* (Qur'an 2:255)

> *"O mankind! You are the poor, in need of God; and God is the Self-Sufficient, the Praised."* (Qur'an 35:15)

Verse 15 of this same sūrah, read alongside verse 3, completes al-Rāzī's point: the need is not merely the need of a beginning but the need of a moment-by-moment dependency. The word used there — *fuqarāʾ*, "the poor" — describes a condition, not an event.

**"Who Provides for You from Heaven and Earth"**

The provision (*rizq*) is specified as coming from two directions, and the specification is not decorative. From heaven comes the rain; from earth comes what the rain produces. Between them lies the whole apparatus of human survival, none of which any human being invented:

> *"And We send the winds as fertilisers, and We send down water from the sky, and We give you thereof to drink — and you are not its guardians."* (Qur'an 15:22)

> *"Is He who created the heavens and the earth not able to create the like of them? Yes indeed, and He is the All-Knowing Creator."* (Qur'an 36:81)

The phrase *"and you are not its guardians"* in 15:22 does the same work as Fāṭir's verse: the thing you depend on most absolutely is the thing you control least. You cannot store the rain, compel it, or delay it. You are downstream of a system you did not design and cannot operate.

This is why the verse's rhetorical question — *"Is there a creator other than God who provides for you from heaven and earth?"* — is not a request for information but a demand for arithmetic. The questioner already knows the answer is no, and so does the person asked. Idolaters of seventh-century Arabia did not claim their stones made rain. They claimed the stones mediated access to the One who did. The verse collapses the mediation by pointing out that there is nothing to mediate: the provision is already arriving, directly, from heaven and earth, to you.

> *"And if thou askest them who created them, they will surely say, 'God.' How then are they turned away?"* (Qur'an 43:87)

**"There Is No God but He" — The Bridge Between the Two Tawḥīds**

The verse then states the conclusion in the shortest possible form: *lā ilāha illā huwa*. Ibn Taymiyyah and the scholars who followed him made a point of the Qur'anic method visible here: revelation consistently argues from *tawḥīd al-rubūbiyyah* (God's exclusive lordship as Creator, Owner, and Provider) to *tawḥīd al-ulūhiyyah* (God's exclusive right to be worshipped). The first is something the idolaters already conceded — 43:87 proves it. The second is what they resisted. The Qur'an's argumentative strategy is to take the concession and show that it is inconsistent with the resistance.

The logic runs: if He alone created you, He alone owns you; if He alone provides for you, He alone can be relied upon; and the one whom you must rely upon absolutely, and to whom you owe absolutely, is the only one who may be worshipped absolutely. To direct worship toward a being that neither created nor provides is to misdirect the deepest impulse of the human being toward something that cannot receive it.

This is why the verse moves from a question about creation to a statement about worship without any transitional argument. To the Qur'an, no transition is needed. The fact is the argument.

**"How, Then, Are You Perverted?"**

The closing phrase, *fa-annā tuʾfakūn*, is one of the Qur'an's most compact accusations. The root *ʾ-f-k* carries the sense of being turned away, flipped over, inverted, and hence of falsehood — *ifk* is the word used for the slander against ʿĀʾishah in Sūrah al-Nūr, a story that was the exact opposite of the truth. To be *maʾfūk* is to be inverted: to have one's perception turned upside down so that the false looks true.

*Fa-annā* — "how then?" — is a question about manner, and it is genuinely bewildered in tone. It is the question one asks when a conclusion does not follow from the premises. The verse has just established: He created you, He provides for you, there is no other. And it says: given that, *how* are you turned the wrong way round? Not "why are you disobedient" — that would be a moral question about desire. "How are you inverted" is a question about cognition: something in your processing has been flipped.

The same phrase appears at 6:95, 10:34, and 40:62, always at the point where the Qur'an has finished laying out evidence and is addressing the failure to see it. Al-Ṭabarī explains *tuʾfakūn* as "turned away from the truth to falsehood." Al-Zamakhsharī notes the astonishment in the interrogative: the inversion is so complete that it provokes wonder even in the One describing it.

**Counting What Cannot Be Counted**

The practical instruction embedded in "remember God's Blessing upon you" is enumeration. The Qur'an repeatedly invites it:

> *"And if you should count the Blessing of God, you could not reckon it. Truly man is unjust, ungrateful."* (Qur'an 14:34)

> *"And if you would count God's Favor, you cannot number it. Truly God is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 16:18)

Both verses make the same observation from opposite ends: the blessing is uncountable, and the human being is ungrateful — and the second is a consequence of the first, because a person who never counts what he has never notices that he has anything. The Prophetic tradition supplies the social counterpart:

> *"He does not thank God who does not thank people."* (Abū Dāwūd 4811; al-Tirmidhī 1954, who graded it ṣaḥīḥ)

Gratitude in Islam is not a feeling but a practice of attention, and attention is trainable. The person who recites this verse and then looks at his hands, his food, the roof over him, the fact that he woke, is doing what the verse asked. The person who treats blessing as a theological abstraction is not.

**The Modern Form of the Inversion**

The inversion the verse describes has a contemporary shape worth naming. A civilisation that has solved enough of the problem of provision to make provision invisible is in a peculiarly dangerous position, because the primary evidence for the verse has been buried by the efficiency of its own delivery. When water comes from a tap, food from a shelf, and light from a switch, the chain between heaven and earth disappears from view — not because it has been broken, but because it has been hidden by intermediaries.

This is exactly the condition *fa-annā tuʾfakūn* describes: not a person who has examined the evidence and rejected it, but a person to whom the evidence has stopped appearing. The remedy the verse proposes is also exactly proportionate: *udhkurū* — remember. Attention, not argument, is the first step. And the argument, once attention returns, is very short: *Is there a creator other than God who provides for you from heaven and earth? There is no god but He.*

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:4

> **If they deny thee, messengers before thee were denied, and unto God are all matters returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Speaks to the Preacher Before It Speaks to the Congregation**

Most Qur'anic arguments in this sūrah are directed outward, at the idolater. This one is directed inward, at the messenger. *Fa-in yukadhdhibūka* — "If they deny thee" — uses the intensive form *kadhdhaba* from the root *k-dh-b*, "to lie." The doubled root conveys not a single act of calling someone a liar but a sustained, emphatic, repeated rejection: they do not merely disbelieve you, they insist on disbelieving you.

The verse then answers with a fact of history rather than a promise of victory: *faqad kudhdhibat rusulun min qablika* — "messengers before thee were denied." The passive construction is important. God does not say "you are being denied as I promised you would be"; He says the denial happened before, to others, repeatedly. Consolation is offered in the form of precedent.

**The Long Register of Denial**

That previous messengers were denied is one of the Qur'an's most frequently repeated statements, and the repetitions are not redundancy but accumulation. The theme appears in the narratives of the prophets themselves and is stated explicitly at 3:184, 6:10, 13:32, 21:41, 38:12, 40:5, 41:43, and 54:9. Sūrah al-Anʿām states it with the additional detail of what followed:

> *"And messengers before thee were denied, and they bore patiently their being denied and persecuted, until Our Help came to them. And there is none who can alter the Words of God. And there has already come to thee some tidings of the messengers."* (Qur'an 6:34)

Note the sequence in that verse: denial, then patience, then help. Not denial, then help. The intervening element — *ṣabr* — is where the messenger actually lives, and Fāṭir's shorter formulation assumes it.

Sūrah al-Muʾminūn adds the emotional register:

> *"And We did not send before thee any messenger except that We revealed to him: there is no god but I, so worship Me."* (Qur'an 21:25)

and a few verses earlier records the denial:

> *"And every party rejoiced in what it had."* (Qur'an 23:53)

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse of Fāṭir as an instruction in patience (*taṣbīr*) directed at the Prophet, and Ibn Kathīr develops the same reading: the denial of previous messengers consoles him and encourages him to bear the denial of the disbelievers patiently, since — from the Qur'an's perspective — every prophetic mission ends in victory.

**Victory Is Stated as a Law, Not a Hope**

The claim that all missions are ultimately victorious is not a vague optimism in the Qur'an; it is stated as a decree:

> *"It is He Who sent His Messenger with the Guidance and the Religion of Truth, that He may cause it to prevail over all religion, though the associators dislike it."* (Qur'an 9:33)

> *"And Our Word has already gone forth to Our servants the messengers: truly they shall be the ones helped, and truly Our hosts shall be the victors."* (Qur'an 37:171–173)

> *"God has written: 'I shall surely prevail, I and My messengers.' Truly God is Strong, Mighty."* (Qur'an 58:21)

These verses establish something that the immediate experience of a persecuted preacher in Makkah could not have confirmed: the mission wins even when the messenger suffers, and sometimes especially then. This is one of the hardest ideas in revelation to hold, because human beings instinctively measure truth by success in the short term. The Qur'an refuses that measure repeatedly. The generation that denied Noah was drowned; the generation that denied Lot was destroyed; the generation that denied Muhammad ﷺ was defeated — but in each case the messenger's vindication came after a long period in which the evidence pointed the other way.

**"And Unto God Are All Matters Returned"**

The second half of the verse is the hinge that keeps the first half from becoming mere consolation. *Wa-ilā Allāh turjaʿ al-umūr* — "and unto God are all matters returned." The verb is passive, and *al-umūr* is comprehensive: affairs, matters, cases, decisions, grievances, scores. Everything returns.

Ibn Kathīr explains the function of this clause in context: it means that those who denied God's messengers will be requited in full for what they did. The verse is therefore not only a comfort but a warning, and it holds both at once. To the messenger it says: your grievance has been filed with a court that will sit. To the denier it says: your denial has been recorded by the same court.

The same phrase appears at 2:210, 3:109, 8:44, 22:76, and 57:5, each time at the point where human justice has become impossible or irrelevant and the matter must be handed over. There is a psychological precision in this. A person who has been wronged and cannot obtain redress has two options: he can consume himself in resentment, or he can transfer the case. The verse authorises the second. It is not telling the wronged to forget the wrong; it is telling him that the wrong is not his to execute.

**What the Verse Tells the Prophet About Himself**

There is a third layer, one that the commentators touch and that deserves emphasis. The verse implicitly corrects a mistake that preachers of every age make: the assumption that the response of the audience measures the quality of the message or the messenger. If they deny you, the verse says, that does not mean you have failed — because messengers before you, who cannot have failed, were denied.

The Qur'an makes this point elsewhere in terms even more direct:

> *"Thou guidest not whom thou lovest; rather, God guides whomsoever He will. And He knows best those who are rightly guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

> *"And if it were thy Lord's Will, all who are on the earth would have believed together. Wouldst thou then compel mankind until they become believers?"* (Qur'an 10:99)

Read with those verses, Fāṭir's verse 4 is a boundary line around the messenger's responsibility. His task is delivery; the outcome belongs to God. This is why verse 23 of the same sūrah will say, without qualification: *Thou art naught but a warner.*

**Khabbāb's Complaint and the Answer Given to It**

The tradition records how the earliest Muslims actually experienced this verse. Khabbāb ibn al-Aratt, a blacksmith who was tortured by the Quraysh — pressed against burning coals until the fat of his back extinguished them — came to the Prophet ﷺ while he was reclining in the shade of the Kaʿbah and said:

> *"Will you not seek help for us? Will you not pray for us?"* The Prophet ﷺ said: *"There was a man among those before you who was dug into the ground, and a saw was brought and placed on his head, and he was split in two — and that did not turn him from his religion. And his flesh and sinews were combed with iron combs — and that did not turn him from his religion. By God, this matter will be completed until a rider travels from Ṣanʿāʾ to Ḥaḍramawt fearing nothing but God and the wolf for his sheep. But you are a people who are hasty."* (al-Bukhārī 3612; also 6958)

The answer is not "your suffering will stop soon." It is: others suffered worse and did not turn; the mission will complete; and you are hurrying. Every element of Fāṭir 35:4 is present in that reply — precedent, patience, and the return of affairs to God — with the addition of the diagnosis at the end. The suffering are hasty. The verse is patient.

**Denial as a Predictable Feature of the World**

Understood at its widest, the verse establishes that rejection of truth is not an anomaly requiring explanation but a regular feature of human history requiring only recognition. When a person expects the truth to be welcomed because it is true, he has misunderstood both the truth and the human being. Truth places demands; demands meet resistance; resistance expresses itself as denial. The prophets, who carried the clearest truths in the clearest language, met the clearest denial.

There is a liberation in this. The person who understands verse 4 stops treating every rejection as information about his own worth, and stops treating every acceptance as proof of his own correctness. Both judgments are removed from his jurisdiction: *and unto God are all matters returned.*

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:5

> **O mankind! Surely God's Promise is true. So let not the life of this world delude you, nor let the Deluder delude you concerning God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Deceivers, Named Separately**

The verse addresses all humanity again — *yā ayyuhā al-nās* — and issues two prohibitions in a single breath, and the fact that there are two is the whole point. *Fa-lā taghurrannakum al-ḥayātu al-dunyā* — "let not the life of this world delude you." *Wa-lā yaghurrannakum bi-llāhi al-gharūr* — "and let not the Deluder delude you concerning God."

The first deceiver is a circumstance: *al-ḥayāt al-dunyā*, "the lower life," from the root *d-n-w*, nearness and lowness — the life that is near at hand and therefore low in rank. The second deceiver is a person: *al-gharūr*, the one who deceives, the intensive form of the root *gh-r-r*. The verse is careful not to merge them, because the two deceptions operate by different mechanisms and require different defenses.

The root *gh-r-r* is rich. *Gharar* is a term of Islamic commercial law meaning excessive uncertainty or risk in a contract — the sale of birds in the sky or fish in the water, where the buyer cannot know what he will receive. *Ghurūr* is the state of being so deceived. And *gharūr* is the agent: the one who makes a thing appear other than it is. The Qur'an gives Satan this title because his method is not primarily force but misrepresentation. He does not make you do what you know to be bad; he makes the bad appear good, and — in the second half of this verse — he makes God appear other than God is.

**"God's Promise Is True"**

Before the two prohibitions comes the grounding statement: *inna waʿda Allāhi ḥaqq*. The word *waʿd* is the promise of good; its counterpart *waʿīd* is the promise of punishment. Al-Ṭabarī reads the reference here narrowly, as the promise of Divine Punishment mentioned in the preceding verse — the requital of those who denied the messengers. Ibn Kathīr and al-Jīlānī-style readings broaden it to include everything pertaining to the Hereafter: resurrection, reckoning, reward, and punishment. The broader reading is more consistent with the verse's universal address.

The Qur'an states the same truth in several places, and the phrase *waʿd Allāh* is treated as categorically different from human promise:

> *"God — there is no god but He. Surely He will gather you together unto the Day of Resurrection, in which there is no doubt. And who is truer in speech than God?"* (Qur'an 4:87)

> *"O mankind! Fear your Lord, and fear a Day when no father shall avail his son, nor shall the son avail his father in any way. Truly the Promise of God is true; so let not the life of this world delude you, nor let the Deluder delude you concerning God."* (Qur'an 31:33)

The parallel with 31:33 is nearly verbatim — the same address, the same declaration, and both prohibitions in the same order. When the Qur'an repeats a formulation in two Makkan sūrahs with this precision, the tradition reads it as a settled teaching rather than an incidental remark.

**How the World Deceives**

The deception of *al-ḥayāt al-dunyā* works by presence. The world is here: visible, tactile, immediate, responsive. The Hereafter is not here: invisible, unresponsive, delayed. A thing that is present has a rhetorical advantage over a thing that is absent, regardless of their comparative reality — and the verse is precisely a correction of that advantage. The world does not need to lie in order to deceive; it only needs to be loud while the truth is quiet.

The Qur'an describes the mechanism with an image that has rarely been surpassed:

> *"Know that the life of this world is but play, diversion, adornment, boasting among you, and rivalry in increase of wealth and children — like a rain whose vegetation pleases the disbelievers; then it withers and you see it yellowing, then it becomes chaff."* (Qur'an 57:20)

Note what the image does. The vegetation genuinely pleases. It is not fake growth; it is real growth with a short term. The deception lies not in the beauty but in the assumption that the beauty will continue. Chaff is what real vegetation becomes when its time is up.

The practical form of this deception in ordinary life is the postponement that feels like planning. "When I have enough money, then I will give." "When the children are older, then I will pray." "When things settle down, then I will repent." Each statement is individually reasonable and collectively fatal, because the condition attached is one that the world is designed never to satisfy. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported in the commentary tradition to have said of such people that a folk was deluded by the postponement of hope: while he says "tomorrow, tomorrow," his term arrives.

**How the Deluder Deceives "Concerning God"**

The second prohibition adds a preposition that changes everything: *bi-llāh* — "concerning God," or literally "by means of God." Satan's most effective temptation is not to invite you to something ugly but to reinterpret God.

The forms this deception takes are stable across centuries. He suggests that God's mercy is so vast that it renders obedience unnecessary. That God's forgiveness is guaranteed and can therefore be drawn upon in advance. That God is too great to care about small matters — and since almost all of human life consists of small matters, the suggestion quietly empties religion of content. That God understands your circumstances better than the revelation does, and that therefore your circumstances override it.

Each of these uses a true attribute — mercy, forgiveness, transcendence, knowledge — as the instrument of a false conclusion. That is why the preposition *bi-llāh* is so exact: the deception is perpetrated *with* God, not merely *about* Him. The Qur'an shows the outcome of this deception on the Day of Judgment, in the words of the hypocrites to the believers:

> *"Were we not with you?" They shall say: "Yes indeed, but you deluded yourselves, and you watched and waited, and you doubted, and wishes deluded you, until the Command of God came; and the Deluder deluded you concerning God."* (Qur'an 57:14)

The same phrase, in the same sense. And Sūrah al-Nisāʾ states the general principle:

> *"He promises them and arouses desires in them; and Satan promises them nothing but delusion."* (Qur'an 4:120)

**Why the Verse Puts God's Promise First**

The order of the verse is instructive. Before warning against the two deceptions, it establishes a truth: *God's Promise is true*. The defense against deception is not vigilance in the negative sense — suspicion, anxiety, constant scanning for traps — but a prior positive certainty. A person who knows with confidence what has been promised is not moved by what is offered.

This is a general principle of moral psychology that the verse applies to religion. Temptation succeeds almost never by arguing that the tempting thing is better than what one already has; it succeeds by making the person forget what he already has. The first act of the deceiver is amnesia. That is why the verse begins with an act of remembering.

**The Analogy of the Exam**

The classical commentators often illustrate this verse with an image from ordinary life, and it remains the clearest available. A student is told by the teacher himself, in writing, that there will be a final examination, that it will count, and that the results will be published. Two things then happen. The pleasures of the term — company, entertainment, comfort — absorb his attention until the examination becomes abstract. And a companion keeps telling him that the teacher is too kind to fail anyone, that the grading will be lenient, that everyone passes in the end. The first is the deception of the world; the second is the deception of the Deluder concerning the teacher. Neither involves any claim that the examination does not exist; both make it possible to live as though it does not.

The verse's remedy is stated in its first clause. The examination is real, and the one who announced it is the one being misquoted.

**A Sūrah That Will Return to This Twice**

Fāṭir does not leave this warning at the door. It returns to the deception of the world in verse 42, where a people who swore they would be more rightly guided than any community turn away when the warner actually comes, and to the deception concerning God in verses 36 and 37, where the inhabitants of the Fire ask to be sent back and are answered with the reminder that they were given long life and a warner. The verse 5 warning is the sūrah's thesis; the rest of the chapter is its demonstration.

> *"And when death comes to one of them, he says: 'My Lord, send me back, that I may do righteousness in what I have left behind.' Nay! It is but a word that he speaks. And behind them is a barrier until the Day they are resurrected."* (Qur'an 23:99–100)

The person who takes verse 5 seriously is the person who does not have to say verse 23:99.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:6

> **Truly Satan is an enemy unto you; so take him as an enemy. He only calls upon his party that they may be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Fact, Then a Command, Then the Motive**

The verse has three parts and they are arranged as an argument, not as a list. First, a declaration of fact: *inna al-shayṭāna lakum ʿaduwwun* — "Truly Satan is an enemy unto you." Second, a command derived from that fact: *fa-ttakhidhūhu ʿaduwwan* — "so take him as an enemy." Third, the evidence for the fact: *innamā yadʿū ḥizbahu li-yakūnū min aṣḥābi al-saʿīr* — "He only calls upon his party that they may be among the inhabitants of the Blaze."

The middle clause is the one that carries the weight, and it is easily misread. *Fattakhadhahu ʿaduwwan* uses the verb *ittakhadha*, "to take, to adopt, to treat as." God is not informing the human being that Satan hates him — that has already been stated. He is commanding him to adopt a stance toward the information. This is a command about the disposition of the believer, not about the disposition of Satan, which is fixed and needs no instruction.

Al-Jīlānī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Zamakhsharī all read the imperative this way: it is an injunction to obey God rather than Satan and to act upon the knowledge of the enmity. Al-Rāzī adds the dimension of righteous deeds. Knowing an enemy exists and treating him as an enemy are two different achievements, and the verse demands the second.

**The Word ʿAduww and What Enmity Requires**

*ʿAduww* comes from the root *ʿ-d-w*, whose core sense is to transgress a limit, to exceed, to go beyond — and hence to aggress. The enemy is the one who crosses the boundary against you. Its opposite in Qur'anic usage is not merely the friend but the *walī*, the one who is near and protective.

To take someone as an enemy means, in practice, four things: you do not trust his advice; you do not accept his framing of your situation; you expect his apparent kindness to carry a concealed cost; and you remain alert in his presence rather than relaxed. The verse requires all four. What makes this difficult is that Satan never presents himself as an enemy — he presents himself as a well-wisher, and the Qur'an says so explicitly in the story of the Garden:

> *"And he swore to them: 'Truly I am for you both among the sincere advisors.'"* (Qur'an 7:21)

The first human beings were undone by an oath of good intentions. Every subsequent temptation repeats the technique.

**An Enemy Unlike Any Human Enemy**

There is a difference between this enmity and human enmity that the commentators stress, and it makes the situation worse rather than better. A human enemy usually wants something *from* you — your property, your position, your submission, your fear. His hostility has an object, and that object can sometimes be surrendered or negotiated. If he obtains it, he may stop.

Satan wants nothing from you except your ruin. He cannot be appeased, bribed, out-negotiated, or satisfied. His purpose is not acquisition but destruction, and — critically — his destruction of you benefits him in no material way. He is, in the language of the tradition, one who acts against his own interest in the act of harming you. The Qur'an records his own declaration of this:

> *"Because Thou hast caused me to err, I shall surely lie in wait for them on Thy straight path. Then I shall come upon them from before them and from behind them, and from their right and from their left, and Thou wilt not find most of them thankful."* (Qur'an 7:16–17)

Four directions of attack, and one stated objective that is not gain but ingratitude — the destruction of thankfulness. That is a portrait of an enemy whose only product is a worse version of his victim.

**Al-Rāzī on the Strategy of an Unescapable Enemy**

Al-Rāzī's commentary on this verse is one of the most practical passages in his work, and it deserves quotation in full because it converts the verse into method:

> *"Whosoever knows that he has an enemy from whom he has no escape, and is certain of that, then focuses upon opposing him, and forbears in fighting him and being patient, shall triumph. Likewise for Satan: the human being cannot escape him, for he is with him. And he ceases not to follow him until he focuses upon him and routs him — for routing Satan comes through a human being's determination, and the path is being firm in intent and trusting in servitude to God."* (al-Rāzī, *Mafātīḥ al-Ghayb*, on 35:6)

The argument has a structure worth following. There are enemies you can flee; there are enemies you can defeat; and there are enemies you can only defeat by ceasing to flee. Satan is in the third category, because his proximity is guaranteed. The only available strategy is confrontation — and confrontation, in this context, means determination (*ʿazm*) and reliance (*tawakkul*), not willpower in the modern sense of gritting one's teeth.

This is why the Qur'an never instructs the believer to fight Satan directly. It instructs him to seek refuge:

> *"And when thou recitest the Qur'an, We place between thee and those who believe not in the Hereafter a hidden barrier. And if thou invitest them to guidance, they will not follow guidance ever."* (Qur'an 17:45–46, in context)

> *"And say: My Lord, I seek refuge in Thee from the whisperings of the devils, and I seek refuge in Thee, my Lord, lest they come near me."* (Qur'an 23:97–98)

The commanded response is not combat but shelter. That is itself a lesson about the enemy: he is not to be argued with, engaged, or negotiated; he is to be escaped from, by moving toward someone stronger.

**"He Only Calls Upon His Party"**

*Ḥizb* means party, faction, group — a body of people bound by allegiance. The verse says Satan calls (*yadʿū*) his party, using the same verb the Qur'an uses for the call of God and the call of the prophets. There is, in other words, a *daʿwah* of Satan parallel to the *daʿwah* of God, and every human being is hearing one or the other or both.

Sūrah al-Mujādilah identifies the party and its outcome:

> *"Satan has overcome them and made them forget the remembrance of God. Those are the party of Satan. Surely the party of Satan are the losers."* (Qur'an 58:19)

Al-Suhrawardī and other commentators of the spiritual tradition, cited in the source, read *ḥizbuhu* as the people of caprice, innovation, and error — those who obey him and listen to those who call to such things. The three terms are worth distinguishing: *hawā* (caprice) is desire treated as evidence; *bidʿah* (innovation) is a religious practice invented without warrant; *ḍalāl* (error) is the resulting state of being lost. The order is causal. Desire reinterpreted as insight produces invented religion, and invented religion produces loss.

**The Destination: Inhabitants of the Blaze**

*Aṣḥāb al-saʿīr* — "the inhabitants of the Blaze." *Saʿīr* comes from the root *s-ʿ-r*, meaning to kindle, to blaze up, to be inflamed; it is used of a fire that has been deliberately stoked. The word *aṣḥāb*, "companions" or "inhabitants," is the Qur'an's ordinary term for permanent residents of a place — *aṣḥāb al-jannah* and *aṣḥāb al-nār*.

The verse therefore states Satan's objective in the plainest terms: not to make you unhappy, not to make you sinful for its own sake, but to make you a permanent resident of a fire that has been lit. And the horror of the verse is that he achieves this by invitation. *Yadʿū* — he calls. He does not drag. The human being walks.

**He Disowns Them at the End**

The Qur'an completes the portrait by showing what the caller does once his party arrives. On the Day of Judgment, after the matter is decided, Satan delivers a speech:

> *"And Satan will say when the matter is decided: 'Truly God promised you a promise of truth, and I promised you and failed you. And I had no authority over you except that I called you and you responded to me. So blame me not, but blame yourselves. I cannot save you, nor can you save me. Truly I disbelieved in your ascribing me as partner aforetime.' Truly the wrongdoers shall have a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 14:22)

And the same disavowal appears in Sūrah al-Ḥashr:

> *"Like Satan, when he said to man: 'Disbelieve.' Then when he disbelieved, he said: 'Truly I am quit of thee. Truly I fear God, the Lord of the worlds.'"* (Qur'an 59:16)

Two details in 14:22 are devastating. First: *"I promised you and failed you"* — the promises were never real; only God's were. Second: *"I had no authority over you except that I called you and you responded to me"* — the human being is returned his responsibility. The enemy's power consisted entirely in the victim's agreement.

This is why "take him as an enemy" is a command rather than a consolation. The verse does not say Satan is powerful; it says he is hostile, and it puts the response in your hands. His call is his; your response is yours.

**The Practical Form of the Enmity**

The Prophetic tradition describes Satan's proximity in an image that made the enmity concrete for the Companions:

> *"Satan flows through the son of Adam as the blood flows."* (al-Bukhārī 2038; Muslim 2175, from Ṣafiyyah bint Ḥuyayy)

Not outside, not approaching — inside the circulation. And Muslim records the limits of his achievement:

> *"Satan has despaired of being worshipped by the people of prayer in the Arabian Peninsula, but not of sowing discord among them."* (Muslim 2812, from Jābir)

Discord, not apostasy, was his remaining field — which explains why the classical scholars treated social strife among believers as one of his primary works rather than an unfortunate accident.

The believer who takes Satan as an enemy does not spend his day in a state of fear. He does something simpler: he stops treating the suggestion that arrives in his mind as his own thought. Every temptation presents itself as an idea you had. The verse's command is to refuse that presentation — to identify the voice before answering it, which is what seeking refuge is for.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:7

> **Those who disbelieve, theirs shall be a severe punishment. And those who believe and perform righteous deeds, theirs shall be forgiveness and a great reward.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hinge Between the Two Parties**

Verse 6 ended by naming Satan's party and its destination. Verse 7 draws the consequence in two strokes, and its brevity is not thinness but compression: this is the sūrah's moral ledger reduced to a single line. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Bayḍāwī read it exactly this way — the verse juxtaposes those who follow Satan and are thus of his party with those who take him as an enemy and follow God.

The construction in Arabic is a topic-comment sentence in both halves, and the fronting matters. *Alladhīna kafarū lahum ʿadhābun shadīd* — literally, "those who disbelieved: theirs is a severe punishment." The pronoun *lahum*, "theirs," is placed before the noun, which in Arabic grammar produces a sense of possession and exclusivity. The punishment is not merely coming to them; it is theirs, allocated, as a property is allocated. The identical construction is used in the second half: *wa-alladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti lahum maghfiratun wa-ajrun kabīr* — "theirs is forgiveness and a great reward."

The symmetry is exact in form and deliberately unequal in content.

**The Asymmetry Between the Two Sides**

Disbelief receives one thing: severe punishment. Faith and righteous deeds receive two: forgiveness, and a great reward. The imbalance runs through the whole Qur'an and it always runs in the same direction, because it is an expression of a stated principle:

> *"Whoever brings a good deed shall have ten times its like, and whoever brings an evil deed shall be recompensed only with its like, and they shall not be wronged."* (Qur'an 6:160)

The good deed is multiplied at minimum tenfold and at maximum seven hundredfold:

> *"The likeness of those who spend their wealth in the way of God is as the likeness of a grain that grows seven ears, in every ear a hundred grains. And God multiplies for whomsoever He will. And God is All-Embracing, All-Knowing."* (Qur'an 2:261)

The evil deed is not multiplied at all. Verse 7 of Fāṭir is a small instance of this large arithmetic: the side of belief gets two gifts where the side of disbelief gets one penalty.

**Why Forgiveness Comes Before Reward**

The order of the two blessings on the believing side is significant, and the commentators noted it. Forgiveness (*maghfirah*) is mentioned first, reward (*ajr kabīr*) second. The root *gh-f-r* means to cover, to shield, to conceal from harm — the same root as *mighfar*, a helmet, which covers and protects the head. Forgiveness is therefore not simply cancellation of a debt; it is covering, a protection placed over the person.

The reason it precedes reward is logical rather than merely stylistic. Every human being who performs righteous deeds performs them imperfectly. If the sequence were reward first, the question would arise: what about the defects in the deeds that qualify for the reward? By placing forgiveness first, the verse removes the obstacle before presenting the gift. The classical juristic maxim expressing this is *dafʿ al-maḍarrah muqaddam ʿalā jalb al-manfaʿah* — the repelling of harm takes precedence over the acquisition of benefit. The verse performs the maxim.

There is also a pastoral effect. A believer reading this verse learns that the first thing awaiting his imperfect worship is not an audit of its quality but a covering of its defects. This is one of the Qur'an's central consolations, and it appears at the end of the longest chapter in exactly the same order:

> *"...pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy upon us."* (Qur'an 2:286)

**"And Perform Righteous Deeds": The Inseparable Pair**

Faith is almost never mentioned alone in the Qur'an when the subject is outcome. The formula *āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — "they believed and performed righteous deeds" — occurs more than fifty times. Its repetition is a doctrinal position: belief that produces nothing is not the belief the Qur'an is describing.

*Ṣāliḥ* comes from the root *ṣ-l-ḥ*, whose meaning is soundness, fitness, being in right working order — the opposite of *fasād*, corruption. A *ṣāliḥ* deed is not merely a permitted deed; it is a deed that is sound, that fits, that puts something right. The word carries an engineering sense: the deed works, it repairs, it functions.

This pairing answers a question that has troubled religious people in every century: does belief save, or does conduct save? The Qur'an refuses the disjunction. It presents belief as the root and conduct as the fruit, and it declines to accept either as sufficient alone. Sūrah al-ʿAṣr states the same structure in four lines:

> *"By time! Truly mankind is in loss, except those who believe, and perform righteous deeds, and enjoin one another to truth, and enjoin one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

**"A Great Reward"**

The reward is described by one adjective: *kabīr*, great. The Qur'an elsewhere specifies the reward in sensory detail — Gardens, rivers, silk, bracelets of gold — and this sūrah will do so in verses 33 to 35. Here it leaves the magnitude unqualified, and the unqualified adjective does more work than a list would. Great relative to what? Relative to the deed, which was small; relative to the time, which was short; relative to the effort, which was finite. The disproportion is the point.

Consider the arithmetic a believer performs: a lifetime of prayer, perhaps sixty or seventy years of standing, bowing, and prostrating, punctuated by failure and distraction; a portion of wealth given, always less than one intended; a handful of harms avoided and a handful of kindnesses done. And against this: *maghfiratun wa-ajrun kabīr*. The Prophetic tradition makes the disproportion explicit and refuses to let it become a claim of merit:

> *"None of you will be saved by his deeds." They said: "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: "Not even me, unless God envelops me with mercy from Him."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)

The reward is great because the Giver is generous, not because the giving was earned. Verse 30 of this same sūrah will state it plainly: *"that He may pay them their rewards in full and increase them from His Bounty."*

**The Function of the Verse in the Sūrah's Movement**

Structurally, verse 7 is the first of several statements in Fāṭir that set the two outcomes side by side without argument — as settled facts to be recorded rather than propositions to be demonstrated. Verses 19 to 22 will do the same through images: the blind and the seeing, the darkness and the light, the shade and the scorching heat, the living and the dead. Verses 29 to 37 will do it at length, through the commerce that never perishes and the Fire that is neither ended nor lightened.

Verse 7 is the summary statement placed early so that everything after it is read in its light. A person who has accepted it reads the natural signs of verses 9 to 13 not as decoration but as evidence for a case already filed; a person who has not accepted it reads them as scenery. The verse is where the sūrah asks the reader to declare which of the two he is.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:8

> **And what of one, the evil of whose deeds has been made to seem fair to him, such that he thinks it beautiful? Truly God leads astray whomsoever He will and guides whomsoever He will; so let not thy soul be expended in regrets over them. Truly God knows that which they do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Deliberately Does Not Finish**

The verse begins with a grammatical irregularity that no translation can fully carry. *A-fa-man zuyyina lahu sūʾu ʿamalihi fa-raʾāhu ḥasanan* — "And what of one, the evil of whose deed has been made to seem fair to him, such that he sees it as good?" There is no completion. The conditional particle *fa-man* introduces a subject and then the sentence moves on without a predicate. Arabic grammarians call this an elided apodosis, and the elision is intentional: the reader is left to supply the comparison himself.

The implied completion, which the following clause supplies in substance, is something like: *is he like one whom God has guided?* The verse refuses to answer because the answer is obvious, and an obvious answer stated by the reader is far more persuasive than the same answer stated by the text.

**"Made to Seem Fair": The Passive of Aesthetic Corruption**

The verb *zuyyina* is passive, from the root *z-y-n*, adornment, beautification, ornament. The same root gives *zīnah*, the adornment a person wears or displays:

> *"Adornment has been made fair to mankind: the love of women, sons, heaped-up treasures of gold and silver, horses of mark, cattle, and tilth. That is the enjoyment of the life of this world, and God — with Him is the fairest return."* (Qur'an 3:14)

The passive voice raises a question the exegetes answered directly: made fair *by whom*? The Qur'an attributes the beautification to three agents in different places — to Satan (*wa-zayyana lahum al-shayṭānu mā kānū yaʿmalūn*, 6:43; 16:63; 27:24; 29:38), to God as the ultimate agent of all events (6:108; 10:12), and to the human being's own desire operating without an external prompter. The commentators reconcile these by treating Satan as the one who presents, desire as the one that accepts, and God as the One who permits and, when the acceptance is deliberate and repeated, confirms.

The source's reading — that the one whose deeds have been beautified is the one who has failed to take Satan as an enemy, in direct continuation of verse 6 — is the contextual reading, and it explains the placement of this verse immediately after the command to treat Satan as an enemy. Refusing to see the enemy is what makes the enemy's presentation convincing.

**The Inversion of Taste**
