Al-Zamakhsharī makes the sharpest observation available on this verse: deeds seeming fair and being led astray are one and the same event, because it is through following caprice that a person comes to see the ugly as beautiful and the beautiful as ugly. The corruption is not in the eyes but in the standard.

This is a distinction with real consequences. An ordinary sinner knows he is sinning; he feels the friction between what he does and what he believes. His conscience is intact and his behaviour is deviant, and that very friction is his remaining protection — guilt is a signal that the moral sense still functions. The person described in this verse has lost the friction. He does not feel bad about the evil; he feels good about it. He has not merely violated a standard, he has replaced it.

Al-Rāzī adds the epistemic parallel: few who commit evil deeds are aware that they are evil, just as few who are ignorant are aware of their ignorance. Both cases involve a defect that includes blindness to itself. This is why correction is so difficult — the ordinary route to correcting an error is to point it out, and pointing out an error to someone whose error is the standard by which he judges errors accomplishes nothing.

The Qur'an describes the end-state of this process in a verse that has become proverbial:

> *"Say: Shall We inform you of those who are the greatest losers in respect of their deeds — those whose effort is lost in the life of this world, while they think that they are doing well?"* (Qur'an 18:103–104)

*Wa-hum yaḥsabūna annahum yuḥsinūnu ṣunʿā* — "while they think they are doing well." The greatest loss in the Qur'an is not failure; it is failure experienced as success.

**"God Leads Astray Whomsoever He Will and Guides Whomsoever He Will"**

This is one of the verses that has generated more theological literature than almost any other, and it must be handled with the seriousness the tradition gave it. Read in isolation, it appears to say that guidance and misguidance are allocated by Divine Will without reference to the human being's choice. The classical schools disagreed about how to reconcile this with the many verses that make guidance contingent on the person's own turning.

The reconciliation the majority of commentators adopt is to read the verse together with the verses that specify *whom* God leaves astray:

> *"Thus does God leave astray whomsoever He will, and guides whomsoever He will. And He is the Mighty, the Wise."* (Qur'an 74:31)

> *"And God guides not the wrongdoing people."* (Qur'an 3:86)

> *"And God guides not the transgressing people."* (Qur'an 5:108)

> *"Thus God leaves astray the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 40:74)

> *"And whosoever believes in God, He guides his heart."* (Qur'an 64:11)

The picture that emerges is of a Will that is not arbitrary but responsive: God misguides those who have chosen misguidance, and guides those who have turned toward Him. The Divine Will and the human choice are not competing causes operating on the same plane; the first is creative and permissive, the second is acquisitive. The human being acquires (*kasb*) the direction; God creates the movement.

There is a further observation that Ibn Taymiyyah and the exegetes of the Salaf pressed: verses of this kind are almost never addressed to the person worried about whether he has been misguided. They are addressed to the Prophet, in the context of consolation. A person who asks sincerely, "am I among those God has left astray?" has already answered the question — the question itself is evidence of a heart that has not been sealed. The sealed heart does not inquire.

**"Let Not Thy Soul Be Expended in Regrets Over Them"**

The verse turns from theology to pastoral care with a sentence of extraordinary tenderness. *Fa-lā tadhhah nafsaka ʿalayhim ḥasarāt* — literally, "do not let your soul go, over them, in regrets." The word *ḥasarah* comes from the root *ḥ-s-r*, meaning to be exhausted, worn out, spent — the condition of an animal that has been driven until it can go no further. *Al-ḥasrah* in the Qur'an is the regret that consumes:

> *"And warn them of the Day of Regret, when the matter will be decided."* (Qur'an 19:39)

God tells the Prophet not to spend his soul away in that consuming grief over people who enjoy their own error. The same instruction appears elsewhere in language even more vivid:

> *"Haply thou wouldst kill thyself with grief that they do not believe."* (Qur'an 18:6)

> *"And thy Lord creates and chooses what He will — not theirs is the choice."* (Qur'an 28:68)

The commentators list more than a dozen verses of this kind (3:176; 5:41, 68; 6:33; 10:65; 16:127; 26:3; 27:70; 31:23; 36:76). Their accumulation is itself the lesson: the burden of other people's rejection was real for the Prophet ﷺ, heavy enough to require repeated divine intervention. He was not indifferent to their fate; he was devastated by it. The verses do not tell him to stop caring. They tell him to stop spending his soul on a matter that has been removed from his jurisdiction.

**The Instruction for Everyone Who Cares About Someone Who Will Not Listen**

This is the verse's widest application, and it belongs to parents, teachers, spouses, friends, and preachers more than to prophets. The person who loves someone in error experiences a specific torment: he can see the damage clearly, he can name it precisely, and he cannot stop it. Every attempt produces resistance; every argument produces a counter-argument; the loved one's deeds have been beautified to him, and he sees them as good.

Verse 8 gives three instructions, and the order matters. First: recognise the mechanism. The person is not merely mistaken; his sense of beauty has been inverted, and argument addressed to beauty will not land. Second: accept the jurisdictional limit. Guidance is not yours to produce. Third: protect your own soul from consumption. Not withdraw, not cease to care, not abandon — but do not spend yourself to exhaustion on an outcome you do not control.

The final clause supplies the reason this is possible: *inna Allāha ʿalīmun bi-mā yaṣnaʿūn* — "Truly God knows that which they do." Nothing is being missed. The record is complete, the Agent is aware, and the case is in hands more capable than yours. The grief that comes from feeling responsible for another's soul is relieved not by convincing oneself the soul does not matter but by recognising who owns it.

**Knowing as the Final Word**

That the verse ends with knowledge rather than with power is characteristic. God does not say here "I will punish them" or "I am able to change them." He says *He knows what they do*. For a person in the state described by the first clause — convinced his evil is beautiful — knowledge is the more terrifying attribute, because it means there is no private sphere in which the beautification can be maintained. He may persuade his companions, his family, himself. He cannot persuade the One who knows.

And for the person grieving over him, the same attribute is the comfort: God sees what you see, and more. You are not the only witness.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:9

> **And God is He Who sends the winds; then they cause clouds to rise. Then We drive them to a land that is dead, and thereby revive the earth after its death. Thus shall be the Resurrection!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Plural of the Wind Is Mercy**

The verse opens with a linguistic detail that the classical exegetes treated as a deliberate pattern rather than an accident of style. The word here is *al-riyāḥ* — the winds, plural. The source's observation, drawn from a careful count of the Qur'an, is that the revivifying dimension of wind appears ten times and in the plural, and each time it carries an aspect of God's Mercy; whereas *al-rīḥ* in the singular is predominantly the wind of punishment.

The contrast can be seen by placing two verses side by side:

> *"And He it is Who sends the winds (*al-riyāḥ*) as glad tidings ahead of His Mercy, until, when they have carried a heavy-laden cloud, We drive it to a land that is dead."* (Qur'an 7:57)

> *"And in ʿĀd, when We sent against them the withering wind (*al-rīḥ al-ʿaqīm*). It left nothing it came upon but made it as decayed."* (Qur'an 51:41–42)

The plural wind builds, carries, distributes, and gives. The singular wind strips. Al-Qurṭubī and others draw the general principle: where God describes the wind as His instrument of life, He pluralises it; where He describes it as His instrument of destruction, He makes it one — a single, undifferentiated, irresistible force.

Sūrah al-Naml puts the same mercy in the form of a rhetorical question:

> *"Is He not better — Who guides you in the darknesses of the land and the sea, and Who sends the winds as glad tidings before His Mercy? Is there a god with God? Exalted is God above what they associate!"* (Qur'an 27:63)

**A Shift in Person: From "He" to "We"**

Readers of the Arabic notice something that translations often flatten. The verse begins in the third person — *wa-Allāhu alladhī arsala al-riyāḥ*, "God is He Who sends the winds" — and then shifts mid-sentence to the first person plural: *fa-tuthīru saḥāban fa-suqnāhu*, "then they stir up a cloud, and *We* drive it."

The shift is a feature of Qur'anic style known as *iltifāt*, the turning of speech from one grammatical person to another. Rhetoricians from the earliest generations noted that *iltifāt* prevents the listener's attention from settling, and that it typically marks a point of particular divine engagement. Here the effect is precise: the sending of the winds is described as a general principle of God's governance, but the driving of the cloud to a specific dead land is described as an act God performs — "We drove it." The universal becomes particular at the moment the mercy arrives.

**"To a Land That Is Dead"**

*Biladin mayt* — "a land that is dead." The Qur'an uses death as a literal description of soil without water, and life as a literal description of soil with it. This is not primarily metaphor in the Qur'an's own usage; it is a statement of fact that then carries metaphorical weight. Dead land is land in which nothing grows: cracked, colourless, unproductive, and — critically — indistinguishable in appearance from land that will never grow anything again.

The revival is then described with a verb of restoration: *fa-aḥyaynā bihi al-arḍa baʿda mawtihā* — "and thereby We revived the earth after its death." The phrase *baʿda mawtihā* — after its death — establishes that the sequence is death first, life second. This order is essential to the argument of the verse, because the analogy with resurrection depends on it. A thing that is revived after death demonstrates something a thing that merely continues does not: it demonstrates that the agent can act upon a state that appears final.

The Qur'an elsewhere supplies the physical detail of the process:

> *"Hast thou not seen that God drives the clouds, then composes them, then makes them a heap, and thou seest the rain emerging from within them? And He sends down from the sky mountains of hail, and strikes with it whomsoever He will and turns it away from whomsoever He will; the flash of His lightning all but takes away the sight."* (Qur'an 24:43)

> *"And God sends down water from the sky, and revives the earth after its death. Truly in that is a sign for a people who listen."* (Qur'an 16:65)

**"Thus Shall Be the Resurrection"**

The verse ends with four words that convert a meteorological observation into a doctrinal proof: *ka-dhālika al-nushūr*. *Nushūr* comes from the root *n-sh-r*, to spread out, to unfold, to publish, to bring the dead to life — the same root as *nashara* in the description of the scrolls of the Day being spread open:

> *"And when the scrolls are unrolled (*nushirat*)."* (Qur'an 81:10)

*Ka-dhālika* — "thus," "in like manner." The claim is an identity of operation: the same power, exercised in the same way, upon a subject in the same condition.

This argument from the revived earth is one of the Qur'an's most repeated forms of evidence for resurrection. It appears at 7:57; 16:65; 22:5; 25:48–49; 29:63; 30:19, 24, 50; 36:33; 41:39; 43:11; 45:5; and 50:9–11. Sūrah Qāf states it most fully:

> *"And We send down from the sky blessed water, and We bring forth thereby gardens and grain harvested, and tall palm trees with layered spadix, as provision for the servants — and We revive thereby a dead land. Thus is the emergence (*al-khurūj*)."* (Qur'an 50:9–11)

And Sūrah al-Rūm gives the argument its sharpest logical form:

> *"And among His signs is that He creates you from dust, then behold! you are human beings spreading abroad... And He it is Who originates creation, then He repeats it, and it is easier for Him."* (Qur'an 30:20, 27)

**Why the Analogy Works — and Its Limits**

The logic deserves to be set out, because it is often described vaguely when it is in fact quite tight. The sceptic about resurrection objects: a body has decayed, its parts have scattered, its form is gone; how can it be reassembled? The verse answers by pointing to a case the sceptic already accepts. A field has died: its moisture is gone, its colour is gone, its seeds are buried and apparently inert. Then water arrives and the field is alive — not merely repaired, but productive in a way it was not, bringing forth plants whose material came from elements that were scattered, buried, and invisible.

The inference is not that the two cases are identical in mechanism. It is that the *difficulty* the sceptic identifies — the impossibility of organising scattered, buried, lifeless material into living form — is a difficulty that is visibly overcome every spring. Whoever does it to a field can do it to a body. The Qur'an states the conclusion in the plainest terms:

> *"And he strikes a likeness for Us and forgets his own creation. He says: 'Who shall give life to bones when they are rotten?' Say: 'He shall give them life Who originated them the first time, and He is Knower of every creation.'"* (Qur'an 36:78–79)

The tradition reinforces the same comparison with an image the Prophet ﷺ used:

> *"Mankind will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection... and the earth will be changed, as God says: 'On the Day when the earth is changed into another earth, and the heavens likewise.'"* (Muslim 2792, in the long ḥadīth of the Gathering)

and, more directly to the point:

> *"The Hour will not be established until the land of the Arabs again becomes meadows and rivers."* (Muslim 157, from Abū Hurayrah)

The revival of the Arabian desert is presented as a sign preceding the End — the same demonstration performed on the same scale.

**Ibn Kathīr on the Placement of the Verse**

Ibn Kathīr notes that this verse is one of many in which the revival of the dead earth serves as a metaphor for resurrection, and he draws the practical conclusion: the argument is available to every human being in every climate, because every human being has seen a dry season and a wet one. No specialist knowledge is required. The evidence for the greatest unseen event in Islamic belief is placed in the most universally observable phenomenon in nature.

This is characteristic of Qur'anic method. Its proofs are not esoteric; they are agricultural. It asks the reader to look at rain, at seeds, at the alternation of night and day, at his own origin in a drop — and it treats the reader's failure to draw the obvious conclusion as a moral failure rather than an intellectual one.

**The Land That Is Dead in a Human Life**

The commentators of the spiritual tradition, Ibn ʿAjībah among them, read the verse's imagery inwardly without displacing its literal sense, and the inward reading has considerable power. There are seasons in which a human soul is a dead land: no desire for prayer, no pleasure in recitation, no capacity for tears, no interest in goodness. The person in that state does not experience it as loss; he experiences it as flatness, and flatness is the most deceptive of spiritual conditions because it does not hurt.

The verse describes what happens to such a land. It is not revived by its own effort — a dead field cannot irrigate itself. It is revived by something sent to it from elsewhere: *fa-suqnāhu ilā baladin maytin*, "We drove it to a dead land." The water comes; the driving is God's; the land's only contribution is to be there and to receive.

This is the structure of every genuine spiritual recovery in the Islamic tradition. The person does not manufacture the return; he exposes himself to the means by which it is sent — the recitation, the company of the good, the prayer performed even without feeling, the act of charity performed even without warmth. And the verse promises, by its very analogy, that the dead land does come back. Not that it can revive itself; that water arrives.

> *"Know that God revives the earth after its death. We have made clear for you the signs, that haply you may understand."* (Qur'an 57:17)

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:10

> **Whosoever desires might—to God belongs might altogether. Unto Him ascends the good word, and He uplifts the righteous deed. As for those who plot evil deeds, theirs shall be a severe punishment, and their plotting shall come to ruin.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Desire for Glory Is Legitimate — the Source Is Not Optional**

The verse begins by taking a desire seriously rather than condemning it. *Man kāna yurīdu al-ʿizzata* — "whosoever desires might." *ʿIzzah* comes from the root *ʿ-z-z*, whose basic sense is hardness, strength, and invincibility — the ground that is *ʿazāz* is hard earth that cannot be sunk into. From this comes honour, glory, prestige, the quality of being unconquerable and unhumiliated. It is one of the things human beings most want and are most willing to damage themselves for.

The verse does not say the desire is base. It says where the thing desired actually resides: *fa-li-llāhi al-ʿizzatu jamīʿan* — "to God belongs might altogether." The particle *fa-* creates a consequence: whoever desires glory, then — to God belongs all glory. The unspoken completion is obvious: let him seek it from its owner.

The Qur'an makes the same point in two other places, both addressed to specific errors of strategy:

> *"Those who take disbelievers as protectors instead of believers — do they seek might through them? Truly might belongs altogether to God."* (Qur'an 4:139)

> *"And let not their speech grieve thee. Truly might belongs altogether to God. He is the Hearing, the Knowing."* (Qur'an 10:65)

In the first, the error is seeking honour by alliance with those who do not have it. In the second, it is being grieved by the insults of those who cannot diminish it. Both verses end the same way, and Fāṭir's verse completes the third case: the person who seeks glory through his own scheming. Al-Qurṭubī adds a further reading — that whoever sincerely desires true honour, seeking it with humility and poverty before God, will find it with God and it will be unveiled to him.

Ibn Kathīr reads the verse as an instruction in obedience: since all such qualities come from God, seek them by obeying Him rather than from idols or from other men.

**The Ascent: Words and Deeds Going Up**

*Ilayhi yaṣʿadu al-kalimu al-ṭayyib* — "unto Him ascends the good word." *Yaṣʿadu* is from the root *ṣ-ʿ-d*, to ascend, climb, rise. The verse presents a vertical cosmology of moral action: certain human utterances travel upward and reach God.

*Kalim* is the plural of *kalimah*, a word or utterance, and *ṭayyib* — from the root *ṭ-y-b*, pure, wholesome, good in essence and pleasant in effect — is the Qur'an's standard adjective for what is intrinsically sound. The commentators identify the *kalim ṭayyib* variously as the remembrance and invocation of God, the recitation of the Qur'an, supplication, and the declaration of God's Oneness. All four are supported; the declaration *lā ilāha illa Allāh* is the most frequently named. The Qur'an gives the phrase its own parable two chapters later in the muṣḥaf:

> *"Hast thou not considered how God strikes a parable — a good word, like a good tree, its root firm and its branches in the sky, yielding its fruit every season by the Leave of its Lord?"* (Qur'an 14:24–25)

The parable supplies what Fāṭir's verse asserts: the good word has a root, a height, and a yield. It is not a sound that dissipates.

**Which Lifts Which: The Grammatical Ambiguity and Its Richness**

The second half of the clause is *wa-l-ʿamalu al-ṣāliḥu yarfaʿuh* — "and the righteous deed, He lifts it" or "it lifts it." The pronoun and the verb admit several readings, and the exegetes preserved all of them because each carries a distinct truth. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Qurṭubī set out the options carefully.

The first reading: *He* uplifts the righteous deed — God raises both the word and the deed to Himself. This is the plain reading and the one most translators follow.

The second: the righteous deed uplifts *the word*. The deed carries the word up; speech unaccompanied by action does not rise. This reading is supported by the saying transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and quoted by Ibn Kathīr:

> *"The good word is the remembrance of God, which is taken up to God, and the righteous deed is the performance of the obligatory duties. Whosoever remembers God while performing an obligatory duty — his deed carries his remembrance and takes it up to God. Whosoever remembers God and does not perform the obligatory duties — his words will be rejected, as will his deed."* (as cited by Ibn Kathīr on 35:10)

The third reading: the word uplifts *the deed*. No deed is accepted unless a sincere declaration of faith precedes or accompanies it — so the word is what gives the deed its eligibility to rise. Al-Qurṭubī states this as the necessary condition: without faith, the good deed has no carrier.

Al-Rāzī adds a qualification that resolves the tension: the good word is itself a good deed. Recitation, remembrance, and supplication are actions of the tongue and are therefore within the category of *ʿamal ṣāliḥ*. The two are not rivals; they are nested.

Al-Tustarī's reading is the one that identifies the operative factor: God *"uplifts it"* means He makes it reach Him because of the presence of sincerity toward God within it. Sincerity (*ikhlāṣ*) is the lift. The deed does not rise by its magnitude but by its purity.

The Prophetic tradition states the same principle in a sentence the commentators quote beside this verse:

> *"Truly God is Pure and accepts nothing but the pure."* (Muslim 1015)

**The Deeds That Do Not Ascend**

The verse's final clause exists because the first clause implies a question: if the good word ascends, what happens to the other kind? The answer is given through the fate of those who plot.

*Wa-alladhīna yamkurūna al-sayyiʾāt* — "and those who plot evil deeds." *Makr*, from the root *m-k-r*, is scheming, planning in secret, contrivance aimed at an end the target does not see. Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ṭabarī all identify the referent as people of duplicity and hypocrisy — those who perform "righteous deeds" in order to deceive others into thinking they obey God. Their deeds look like the ascending kind and are not.

The Qur'an describes the same phenomenon with an image of total nullification:

> *"O you who believe! Do not nullify your charities with reminder and injury, like one who spends his wealth to be seen by mankind and believes not in God and the Last Day. His likeness is as the likeness of a smooth rock upon which is dust; then heavy rain falls upon it and leaves it bare. They have no power over anything of what they have earned."* (Qur'an 2:264)

> *"And We shall turn to whatever deeds they did, and We shall make them scattered dust."* (Qur'an 25:23)

The image in 2:264 is precise: the rock *looks* like soil from a distance, and the dust upon it looks like fertility. When the rain comes — the test — nothing grows, because there was no depth. The deed performed to be seen is dust on stone.

**"Their Plotting Shall Come to Ruin"**

*Wa-makru ulāʾika huwa yabūr* — "and the plotting of those, it comes to ruin." The verb *yabūr* is from the root *b-w-r*, meaning to perish, to lie waste, to be destroyed; *bawār* is the state of ruin, and *bayr* the land that is left uncultivated and useless. The Qur'an uses the same word for the destination of the tyrants:

> *"Did thou not consider those who exchanged the Blessing of God for disbelief, and made their people alight in the Abode of Ruin (*dār al-bawār*) — Hell, in which they roast? An evil settlement!"* (Qur'an 14:28–29)

The verse's pronouncement is not that the plot will fail in the sense of being detected. It is that the plot will *become waste* — that the contrivance itself will turn into the instrument of ruin. The Qur'an states this law elsewhere with the sharpest formulation:

> *"And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 3:54)

> *"That they may plot therein — yet the evil plot besets none but its authors."* (Qur'an 35:43)

Verse 43 of this same sūrah returns to exactly this principle, which shows that verse 10 is not a parenthesis but the first statement of a theme Fāṭir will close on. The plotting of the disbelievers is treated throughout the sūrah as a self-executing mechanism: the plot closes in on the plotter.

**The Modern Economy of ʿIzzah**

The verse's opening clause has an unusually direct application to contemporary life, because the desire for *ʿizzah* — for standing, recognition, invulnerability to humiliation — is now mediated by systems built specifically to sell it. Metrics of followers, views, likes, and influence are, functionally, instruments for the distribution of honour, and they are controlled by entities that are neither God nor accountable to Him.

The verse's claim is narrow and therefore devastating: all *ʿizzah* belongs to God. Not some of it. Not the religious kind while the social kind belongs to the platforms. *Jamīʿan* — altogether. A person who seeks honour from a system that allocates it arbitrarily, revokes it without notice, and rewards behaviour he would be ashamed of in private, is doing what 4:139 describes: seeking might through those who do not have it.

The alternative the verse offers is the ascent. What rises is not what is seen — visibility is precisely what does not ascend. What rises is the good word and the righteous deed, and the condition of their rising is sincerity, which is invisible by definition. The two systems are not merely different; they are inverted. The one that makes you visible makes the deed worthless; the one that keeps the deed hidden makes it reach God.

There is a practical test embedded in this. Ask of any act of goodness: would I still do it if no one ever knew? The answer reveals whether the deed is ascending or being displayed. And the verse's consolation is that the ascending kind is never lost, even when the audience never appears — because *unto Him ascends the good word*, and He is an audience of one, which is the only audience that counts.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:11

> **God created you from dust, then from a drop, then He made you pairs; and no female bears or brings forth save with His Knowledge. And none who grows old grows old, nor has aught lessened of his life, but that it is in a Book. Truly that is easy for God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Dust to Drop: The Two Humiliations That Are Also Two Proofs**

The verse compresses the whole human biography into three steps, and each step is a deliberate lowering. *Wa-Allāhu khalaqakum min turāb* — "God created you from dust." *Turāb* is dry soil, the loose surface earth, the material of which there is an unlimited quantity and for which no one pays. Then: *thumma min nuṭfah* — "then from a drop." *Nuṭfah* literally means a small amount of liquid, a drop that drips; the Qur'an describes it elsewhere as *māʾ mahīn*, "a base fluid" (32:8; 77:20), and as *nuṭfatun tumnā*, "a drop emitted" (53:46).

The juxtaposition of the two materials is what does the work. Dust and a drop are the two least impressive substances available in human experience — one trodden underfoot, one spilt and disregarded. The verse begins human biography there, and it does so for a purpose that Sūrah al-Ḥajj states outright:

> *"O mankind! If you are in doubt concerning the Resurrection, then [know] We created you from dust, then from a drop, then from a clinging clot, then from a lump of flesh, formed and unformed, that We may make clear to you."* (Qur'an 22:5)

The argument is this: a being who has already watched dust become a speaking, reasoning person cannot seriously maintain that a body returning to dust cannot be reconstituted. The harder event has already been witnessed by every human being alive, in himself.

**"Then He Made You Pairs"**

*Thumma jaʿalakum azwājā* — "then He made you pairs." The word *zawj* means one of a matched set, and its application here is to the pairing of male and female. The Qur'an states the same fact in several places and draws different conclusions from it depending on context:

> *"O mankind! Fear your Lord, Who created you from a single soul, and created from it its mate, and scattered from the two of them many men and women."* (Qur'an 4:1)

> *"And God made for you mates from your own selves, and made for you from your mates sons and grandsons, and provided you with good things."* (Qur'an 16:72)

> *"And of His signs is that He created for you mates from yourselves, that you may find rest in them, and He placed between you affection and mercy. Truly in that are signs for a people who reflect."* (Qur'an 30:21)

Here, as in 16:72 and 42:11, the emphasis is on procreation — the pairing is presented as the mechanism by which the species continues. In 30:21 the emphasis is psychological and spiritual: rest (*sakana*), affection (*mawaddah*), and mercy. Fāṭir's verse sits in the first group, but the transition to the next clause shows why: the pairing exists so that a female may carry, and the carrying happens under God's Knowledge.

**"No Female Bears or Brings Forth Save with His Knowledge"**

*Wa-mā taḥmilu min unthā wa-lā taḍaʿu illā bi-ʿilmihi* — "and no female carries [a child] nor lays it down [in birth] except with His Knowledge." The two verbs cover the whole of pregnancy and delivery: *taḥmilu*, she carries the burden; *taḍaʿu*, she puts it down. The image is of carrying something heavy and then setting it down, which is exactly what gestation and birth are.

The preposition *bi-* in *bi-ʿilmihi* is the crucial word. It does not say "except by His permission" or "except by His power," though both are implied; it says "except with His Knowledge." The choice locates God's presence in the pregnancy at the level of awareness — He is not merely the One who allowed it, He is the One who knows it in every particular. Sūrah al-Raʿd states the same knowledge in more detail:

> *"God knows what every female carries, and what the wombs fall short of and what they exceed. And everything with Him is by measure."* (Qur'an 13:8)

*Wa-mā taghīḍu al-arḥāmu wa-mā tazdād* — what the wombs diminish and what they increase: the miscarriage, the premature birth, the twins, the extended gestation. The verse in al-Raʿd covers the outcomes that human knowledge in the seventh century could not have known, and that medical science now tracks with difficulty.

There is a pastoral dimension that the classical commentators recognised. Pregnancy in every age is the human experience most dominated by uncertainty and most inaccessible to anyone else's help. The woman carrying the child is alone with it in a way no other human being can enter; the husband, the physician, and the mother are all outside. To say that this occurs *bi-ʿilmihi* — with His Knowledge — is to say that the one place in human life where a person is most radically unobserved is the place where God's observation is most complete.

**The Long-Lived and the Shortened: A Verse That Generates a Doctrine**

The next clause is one of the most discussed in the sūrah because of a grammatical difficulty. *Wa-mā yuʿammaru min muʿammarin wa-lā yunqaṣu min ʿumurihi illā fī kitāb* — "and none is granted long life, nor is aught lessened of his life, except that it is in a Book." The puzzle: if the same person is both long-lived and shortened, the sentence contradicts itself.

Two readings resolve it, and both are classical. The first, from Ibn ʿAbbās and preferred by al-Ṭabarī, takes the pronoun in "his life" as referring to a *different* person than the one long-lived: some are granted long life, others are cut short, and each case is recorded. The second, developed by later commentators, takes both as the same person and distinguishes between two registers of decree: the term written in the Preserved Tablet, which is fixed, and the term recorded with the angels, which may be altered — as in the verse:

> *"God blots out what He wills and confirms, and with Him is the Mother of the Book."* (Qur'an 13:39)

The second reading is the one that generated a substantial theological literature on the two decrees — the absolute decree (*al-qaḍāʾ al-mubram*) and the suspended decree (*al-qaḍāʾ al-muʿallaq*) — and it is the reading that makes sense of the Prophetic reports that certain acts lengthen life:

> *"Whoever loves that his provision be increased and his term be extended, let him maintain the ties of kinship."* (al-Bukhārī 2067; Muslim 2557)

> *"Nothing repels the Decree except supplication, and nothing increases life except righteousness (*al-birr*)."* (al-Tirmidhī 2139; Ibn Mājah 90)

The scholars reconcile these with the fixed term by precisely the distinction the verse invites: what is extended is the term as it stands in the register of means, not the term as it stands in the register of God's final knowledge. The verse says both are *fī kitāb* — in a Book — and the Book contains the extension as well as the limit.

*Muʿammar* is a rare intensive passive form meaning "one who has been granted a long span of life"; its root *ʿ-m-r* gives ʿumr (life-span), ʿimārah (inhabitation), and ʿumrah (the lesser pilgrimage, so named for visiting an inhabited place). The word itself implies that long life is something *done to* a person, not something he produces.

**"Truly That Is Easy for God"**

*Inna dhālika ʿalā Allāhi yasīr* — "Truly that is easy for God." The clause appears throughout the Qur'an as a refrain after descriptions of acts that would be impossible or exhausting for a creature: creating without precedent (19:9), raising the dead (30:27), recording every life (this verse). *Yasīr* comes from the root *y-s-r*, ease, facility, the left hand — the side associated with lightness.

The theological function of the phrase is to prevent the reader from measuring divine action by human effort. A human registrar cannot track every pregnancy, every birth, every lifespan, every diminution of a lifespan, across the whole species. The verse says God does, and that doing it costs Him nothing. Ibn Kathīr notes that this is the standard Qur'anic closure to descriptions of divine omniscience: the enumeration of details is followed by the statement that the details present no difficulty.

**Living Inside a Recorded Term**

The verse, taken whole, describes a human being whose origin is dust, whose beginning is a drop, whose existence depends on a pairing he did not choose, whose gestation was known to God before anyone else knew it, and whose lifespan is recorded with its every alteration.

There are two possible responses to that description, and the Qur'an intends the second. The first is fatalism: if the term is written, effort is pointless. The second is liberation: if the term is written, anxiety about it is pointless. The tradition consistently adopts the second. The Companions asked the Prophet ﷺ directly whether their deeds were already decided, and his answer established the practice:

> *"Work, for everyone is facilitated to that for which he was created."* (al-Bukhārī 4949; Muslim 2649)

The recorded term is not an instruction to stop working; it is a permission to stop worrying. The person who knows that his lifespan is in a Book cannot lose it early by accident, cannot be robbed of it by an enemy, and cannot add to it by panic. What he can do is fill it.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:12

> **Not equal are the two seas: one sweet, satisfying, pleasant to drink, and the other salty, bitter. Yet from each you eat fresh meat, and bring out ornaments that you wear. And you see the ships therein plowing through, that you may seek of His Bounty, and that haply you may give thanks.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse About Difference That Begins With Inequality**

*Mā yastawī al-baḥrān* — "the two seas are not equal." The verse opens with a negative statement of equality, a construction the Qur'an uses repeatedly when it wishes to establish a distinction that people have blurred. Here the two seas are physically adjacent and functionally similar — both are water, both are bodies you can sail on, both produce food — and yet they are not equal.

The two are then defined by four adjectives, two each. The first sea: *ʿadhbun furātun sāʾighun sharābuhu* — "sweet, fresh, pleasant to drink." *ʿAdhb* is water that is sweet, free of salt and bitterness; *furāt* intensifies it to the most drinkable freshness, the water of a running river; *sāʾigh* — from the root *s-w-gh*, to pass down easily — describes a drink that goes down the throat without resistance or aftertaste. The second sea: *milḥun ujāj* — "salty, bitter." *Ujāj* is the extreme of salt and bitter combined, water that burns the throat and cannot be drunk.

The four adjectives are not redundant. They describe the difference from four angles: composition (sweet vs. salt), quality (fresh vs. bitter), and experience (pleasant to drink vs. impossible to drink). A reader who has tasted seawater knows exactly what *ujāj* means, and the verse's insistence on it is a demand that the difference be felt in the mouth, not merely noted on a map.

**The Barrier That Is Not Mentioned Here — But Is Mentioned Next Door**

Fāṭir states the inequality without explaining how the two remain distinct. Sūrah al-Furqān, the sūrah that immediately precedes it in the order of revelation according to the received tradition, supplies the missing clause:

> *"And He it is Who released the two seas — this one sweet and fresh, and this one salty and bitter — and He placed between them a barrier and a partition, forbidden."* (Qur'an 25:53)

And Sūrah al-Raḥmān states the same with the added detail of their meeting:

> *"He released the two seas, meeting side by side. Between them is a barrier they do not transgress. So which of the favors of your Lord will you deny? From them come forth the pearl and the coral."* (Qur'an 55:19–22)

The word *barzakh* — barrier, isthmus, partition — is used in the Qur'an both of the physical separation between waters and of the interval between death and resurrection (23:100). Its meaning in both cases is a boundary that is real but not visible: nothing marks the surface of the sea where fresh water meets salt, and yet the two do not blend as they would if no barrier existed.

The commentators of the earlier centuries described this phenomenon from observation, since river mouths and estuaries exhibit it visibly. Modern oceanography identifies the mechanisms — density stratification, pycnoclines, and the limited mixing at estuarine interfaces — and the Qur'anic statement remains a description of an observable fact whose explanation required fourteen centuries. What matters for the verse's argument, however, is not the mechanism but the consequence: two waters of opposite quality, adjacent, both productive, kept distinct.

**"Yet From Each You Eat Fresh Meat"**

The verse then does something unexpected. Having established that the two seas are unequal, it insists that both provide. *Wa-min kullin taʾkulūna laḥman ṭariyyan* — "and from each of them you eat fresh meat." *Laḥm ṭarī* is meat that is fresh, moist, newly taken — the opposite of preserved or dried. The Qur'an uses the same phrase in Sūrah al-Naḥl:

> *"And He it is Who subjected the sea, that you may eat from it fresh meat, and bring forth from it ornaments that you wear. And thou seest the ships plowing through it, and that you may seek of His Bounty, and that haply you may give thanks."* (Qur'an 16:14)

The parallel is nearly complete, which indicates that Fāṭir's treatment is a deliberate restatement rather than an independent observation.

Al-Rāzī uses this shared provision to argue against the dominant metaphorical reading of the verse, and his argument deserves attention because it is a model of exegetical honesty. Many commentators — al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī among them — read the two seas as symbols of the believer and the disbeliever: one sweet and pleasant, the other bitter and undrinkable. Al-Rāzī observes that the verse's own statement that *fresh meat comes from each* makes the metaphor difficult to sustain, since it would require the disbeliever to be a source of benefit comparable to the believer. His objection is not that the metaphor is wrong but that it cannot absorb the whole verse.

**The Ornaments: What Comes Out of the Bitter One**

*Wa-tastakhrijūna ḥilyatan talbasūnahā* — "and you bring out ornaments that you wear." *Ḥilyah* is adornment, jewellery, the thing worn for beauty; *tastakhrijūna*, from the root *kh-r-j*, is to extract, to bring out from inside. The ornaments are pearls and coral, as the commentators specify, and 55:22 names them directly: *al-luʾluʾ wa-l-marjān*.

The significance lies in where they come from. Pearls are produced in salt water — in the *ujāj*, the undrinkable, bitter sea. The most prized ornament of the pre-modern world is extracted from the water that cannot be drunk. The Qur'an makes this a general principle elsewhere:

> *"And it may be that you dislike a thing and God makes therein much good."* (Qur'an 4:19)

A sea that is useless for one purpose is uniquely productive for another. The verse's insistence on "from each" is thus not a hedge but a claim: the inequality of the two seas does not make one of them waste.

**"The Ships Plowing Through"**

*Wa-tarā al-fulka fīhi mawākhira* — "and thou seest the ships therein plowing." *Fulk* is a collective noun used for both one ship and many, and *mawākhira* is the active plural participle of *makhara*, "to cleave, to plow, to cut through" — the word used of a plowshare cutting soil and of a ship's prow cutting water. The image is deliberate: the sea is a field and the ship is the plow.

This is one of the Qur'an's favourite observations, and it appears with an argument attached:

> *"And among His signs are the ships in the sea like mountains. If He wills, He stills the wind and they remain motionless upon its surface. Truly in that are signs for every patient, thankful one."* (Qur'an 42:32–33)

The ship floats — a thing made of material denser than water, carrying a cargo, remaining on the surface. That it does so is treated by the Qur'an not as engineering but as sign. The point is that the sea has been *made* navigable: the density, the buoyancy, the wind that drives the sail are all pre-existing conditions the shipbuilder exploits but did not create.

**The Two Purposes Stated at the End**

The verse closes by naming why all this has been arranged: *li-tabtaghū min faḍlihi wa-laʿallakum tashkurūn* — "that you may seek of His Bounty, and that haply you may give thanks." Two purposes, and they belong to two dimensions of human life.

The first is economic: *tabtaghū min faḍlihi*, you seek of His Bounty. Commerce, fishing, travel, trade — the entire apparatus of livelihood is presented as a pursuit of divine bounty, not merely as human industry. The phrase *faḍl Allāh* is used in the Qur'an for lawful earning (62:10: *"and seek of God's Bounty"*) and for unmerited increase. The sea is a place where God's bounty is available for the taking, for those who will go and take it.

The second is spiritual: *laʿallakum tashkurūn*, that you may give thanks. *Shukr* is the recognition of a favour together with its attribution to its source and its use for the purpose for which it was given. The verse's structure makes gratitude the *reason* the provision exists, not merely a response to it. The sea gives fish so that gratitude may happen.

**Ibn ʿAjībah on the Two Oceans Within**

The spiritual tradition read the verse inwardly without abandoning its literal sense, and Ibn ʿAjībah's treatment is the most developed. He likens the Law (*sharīʿah*) to the sea that is sweet and pleasant to drink, and the Reality (*ḥaqīqah*) to the sea that is salty and bitter — because the latter requires the exertion of one's innermost self, the lowering of one's head, the spending of wealth, and the abandonment of the world and its people, whereas the Law does not demand as much. Fresh meat and ornaments are drawn from each: from the ocean of the Law one obtains the sweetness of proper conduct; from the ocean of Reality one tastes the sweetness of witnessing God and of spiritual knowledge. The ships, in his reading, are the spiritual reflections plowing through the seas of Dominion (*malakūt*) and Majesty (*jabarūt*) toward the completion of knowledge from God's Bounty, so that their passengers may be among those who give thanks.

Whether or not one adopts this allegorical reading, its underlying insight is straightforward: there are two kinds of religious experience, one agreeable and one harsh, and both are productive. The person who has only tasted the sweet sea has not been fed fully; the person who has only drunk the bitter one has missed the adornment.

**What the Verse Teaches About Comparison**

Read as a whole, verse 12 is a lesson in how to compare things that differ. The two seas are unequal — the verse says so twice — and both are blessings. The inequality does not make the lesser one worthless, and the worth of the lesser one does not cancel the inequality.

This is a discipline that human beings find genuinely hard. The ordinary instinct is either to deny difference ("they are all the same") or to deny value in the lesser ("what use is salt water?"). The verse refuses both. It states the difference in the strongest sensory terms available — sweet to the drinker, bitter to the throat — and then states the shared benefit with equal force: from each you eat.

Applied to human beings, the principle is immediately useful. People differ in capacity, circumstance, temperament, and endowment, and the differences are real; the verse does not ask anyone to pretend otherwise. But the differences do not determine who is productive. The bitter sea yields the pearl.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:13

> **He makes the night pass into the day and makes the day pass into the night, and He has made the sun and the moon subservient, each running for a term appointed. That is God, your Lord; to Him belongs sovereignty. As for those upon whom you call apart from Him, they do not possess so much as the husk of a date stone.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"He Inserts the Night Into the Day"**

*Yūliju al-layla fī al-nahār* — the verb is *awlaja*, from the root *w-l-j*, to enter, to penetrate, to cause to go inside. The image is not of one thing replacing another but of one thing being *inserted* into another: the night is made to enter the day and the day to enter the night, each taking a portion of the other's space.

The result is what every human being observes and almost nobody names: the length of the day is not constant. It grows and shrinks. In winter the night enters the day until the day is short; in summer the day enters the night until the night is short. The Qur'an uses this same verb in five places — 3:27, 22:61, 31:29, 35:13, and 57:6 — and in each it is offered as evidence of lordship:

> *"Hast thou not seen that God causes the night to enter the day and causes the day to enter the night, and He has subjected the sun and the moon, each running to a term appointed, and that God is Aware of what you do?"* (Qur'an 31:29)

The parallel is so close that al-Ṭabarī and later commentators treat the two as a single teaching stated twice, and Ibn Kathīr refers the reader of Fāṭir directly to the almost identical phrasing in Luqmān.

There is a further sense that the exegetes preserve. *Īlāj* also conveys alternation and succession — the night and the day taking turns, each inheriting the other's place. Sūrah al-Furqān makes this explicit:

> *"And He it is Who made the night and the day successive, for whosoever desires to remember, or desires gratitude."* (Qur'an 25:62)

Succession is the condition of human life: without it there would be no rest, no work, no calendar, no memory of duration. The alternation is itself a provision.

**"Subjected" — The Key Word**

*Wa-sakhkhara al-shamsa wa-l-qamar* — "and He has made the sun and the moon subservient." *Sakhkhara* is from the root *s-kh-r*, to compel to service, to subject, to make available for another's use without the thing's own choice. It is the same word used of the subjection of the sea to human navigation (16:14), of the wind to Solomon (21:81), and of everything on earth to humanity (45:13):

> *"And He has subjected to you whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is in the earth, all from Him. Truly in that are signs for a people who reflect."* (Qur'an 45:13)

The theological content of *taskhīr* is that the sun and the moon are not agents. They do not decide, negotiate, favour, or punish. They are instruments in service, running a course assigned to them. This is the exact opposite of the Arabian understanding of the heavenly bodies, which were venerated precisely because they were thought to act. By calling them *musakhkharāt*, the Qur'an demotes them from gods to equipment — and equipment cannot be prayed to.

**"Each Running for a Term Appointed"**

*Kullun yajrī li-ajalin musammā* — "each runs to a term appointed." The verb *yajrī* — to run, to flow — is used both of the sun's and moon's motion and of a ship's sailing (the same verb in 42:32). *Ajal musammā*, a term named or specified, is the Qur'an's standard expression for a determined endpoint, used of human life (6:2), of debt repayment (2:282), and of the whole cosmos:

> *"And the sun runs to a resting place for it. That is the decree of the Mighty, the Knowing. And the moon — We have appointed for it mansions, until it returns like an old date-branch. Neither is it for the sun to overtake the moon, nor does the night outstrip the day; and each floats in an orbit."* (Qur'an 36:38–40)

The verse in Fāṭir adds the eschatological dimension: the sun and moon have a term, which means they will end. Sūrah al-Qiyāmah describes that end:

> *"And the moon is darkened, and the sun and the moon are joined together."* (Qur'an 75:8–9)

A thing with a term is a thing that is passing. And a passing thing cannot be a god — a point the Qur'an makes through the story of Abraham, who rejected the star, the moon, and the sun in sequence precisely because each set: *"I do not love those that set"* (6:76).

**"That Is God, Your Lord"**

*Dhālikum Allāhu rabbukum* — "That is God, your Lord." The demonstrative *dhālikum* is the distant form, used — as at 2:2 — for exaltation: *that* God, the One just described. The verse performs a complete identification: the One who alternates night and day and subjects the sun and moon *is* the Lord you already claim to worship.

Then the conclusion: *lahu al-mulk* — "to Him belongs sovereignty." *Mulk* is dominion, kingship, ownership together with command. The verse has built to it through three steps: God controls the alternation of time (night and day); God controls the greatest bodies in the sky (sun and moon); therefore God holds the sovereignty. Each step is something the idolater already conceded, which makes the conclusion unavoidable.

**The Husk of a Date-Stone: The Smallest Unit of Ownership**

*Wa-alladhīna tadʿūna min dūnihi mā yamlikūna min qiṭmīr* — "and those upon whom you call apart from Him do not possess so much as the husk of a date-stone."

*Qiṭmīr* is the thin white membrane wrapped around the date-stone, the film between the stone and the flesh of the date. It is not merely small; it is worthless — a thing no one seeks, no one buys, no one stores, and no one misses. The Arabic commentators note that the Qur'an has three such words of minuteness, each attached to the same argument:

- *Qiṭmīr*, the husk of the date-stone (35:13).
- *Naqīr*, the small groove or notch on the back of the date-stone (4:124; 17:71).
- *Fatīl*, the thread-like fibre in that groove (4:49; 17:71).

> *"Say: Call upon those you assert apart from Him — they possess no power to remove harm from you, nor to change it. Those upon whom they call themselves seek the means of nearness to their Lord, hoping for His Mercy and fearing His Punishment. Truly the Punishment of thy Lord is to be feared."* (Qur'an 17:56–57)

> *"Truly those upon whom you call apart from God will never create a fly, even if they gathered together to do so. And if the fly should rob them of aught, they could not rescue it from it. Feeble are the seeker and the sought!"* (Qur'an 22:73)

The argument in 22:73 is the same as the argument here, and it is the Qur'an's most compressed test of divinity: the ability to create the smallest living thing, and the ability to recover the smallest thing taken away. The idols fail both. The date-stone husk is the equivalent test in the register of property: they own nothing at all.

**Why Ownership Is the Test**

The choice of ownership as the criterion is not arbitrary. In the Arabian religious economy, a god was approached for benefits — rain, victory, children, protection — and benefits require resources. A deity who owns nothing has nothing to give. The verse therefore does not attack the idols' moral character or their mythology; it examines their balance sheet, and finds it empty.

This is why the Qur'an so often couples *mulk* with *rizq* (provision) and with *duʿāʾ* (supplication). The One who owns is the One who can give; the One who can give is the One who may be called upon. Sūrah Sabaʾ, the twin of this sūrah, makes the sequence explicit:

> *"Say: Who provides for you from the heavens and the earth? Say: God. And surely either we or you are upon guidance or in manifest error."* (Qur'an 34:24)

**The Sovereignty That Follows**

Read together, the two halves of verse 13 form the sūrah's central argument in miniature. The upper half describes a system of immense scale operating without interruption: light and dark exchanging portions, two celestial bodies running courses measured to the second, all of it subject, all of it bound to a term. The lower half describes what stands opposite this system: carved objects that own a date-stone husk.

The disproportion is the argument. The verse does not ask the reader to choose between two comparable claims; it asks him to notice that there is only one claim being made. The idols do not claim to alternate night and day. They do not claim to run the sun. They claim nothing, because they are nothing.

That is why the verse's conclusion is stated as sovereignty rather than as power. *Lahu al-mulk* is a political term: kingship, jurisdiction, the right to command. What the sūrah establishes at this point is not merely that God is strong but that God is in charge — and that the alternative to His charge is not a rival administration but an absence of government altogether.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:14

> **If you call upon them, they hear not your call, and even if they heard, they would not respond to you; and on the Day of Resurrection they will disown your ascribing partners unto God. And none informs you like one who is Aware.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Failures in Ascending Order**

Verse 13 established that the idols own nothing. Verse 14 establishes that they do nothing, and it does so through a sequence of three statements, each more damaging than the last.

First: *in tadʿūhum lā yasmaʿū duʿāʾakum* — "if you call upon them, they do not hear your call." Not that they hear and refuse; they do not hear. The failure is at the level of perception.

Second: *wa-law samiʿū mā istajābū lakum* — "and even if they heard, they would not respond to you." The verse concedes a hypothetical for the sake of argument and then closes it: perception would not produce action. The failure is at the level of will.

Third: *wa-yawma al-qiyāmati yakfurūna bi-shirkikum* — "and on the Day of Resurrection they will disown your ascribing of partners." The failure is at the level of loyalty. Not only are they useless now; they will testify against the devotion later.

This is a rhetorical structure the Qur'an uses with precision. By granting the second clause as a supposition — "even if they heard" — the verse removes the worshipper's last escape. He cannot say "perhaps they hear but are testing me," or "perhaps they respond in ways I do not perceive." The verse has already assumed the most favourable case and shown that it changes nothing.

**"They Hear Not Your Call" — The Qur'an's Repeated Observation**

> *"And those upon whom you call apart from Him are not able to help you, nor can they help themselves. And if thou callest them to guidance, they hear not; and thou seest them looking at thee, but they do not see."* (Qur'an 7:197–198)

> *"And who is more astray than one who calls upon those apart from God who will not respond to him until the Day of Resurrection, and who are heedless of his call?"* (Qur'an 46:5)

The phrase in al-Aḥqāf — *wa-hum ʿan duʿāʾihim ghāfilūn*, "heedless of his call" — adds a detail of peculiar force. *Ghāfil* is one who is unaware, distracted, absent-minded. The idols are not hostile to the worshipper; they are not paying attention to him. The relationship he believes he has with them does not exist at their end at all.

Al-Qurṭubī observes that the verse describes the object of worship as less responsive than the least responsive creature, since even an inattentive human being may be roused. The stone cannot be roused.

**"They Will Disown Your Ascribing Partners"**

The verb is *yakfurūn* — from the root *k-f-r*, to cover, to conceal, and hence to disbelieve, to deny, to disown. The construction *yakfurūna bi-shirkikum* means they will reject, repudiate, and deny the association you made. The Qur'an describes this scene in several places:

> *"And when mankind is gathered, they will be enemies unto them, and will be deniers of their worship."* (Qur'an 46:6)

> *"And they shall say, 'Glory be to Thee! Thou art our Protector, not them. Nay, they worshipped the jinn; most of them were believers in them.'"* (Qur'an 34:41)

> *"And on the Day when We gather them all together, We shall say to those who associated partners: 'Where are your partners whom you asserted?' ... Then there shall be no trial for them except that they say: 'By God our Lord, we were not associators.'"* (Qur'an 6:22–23)

And, most starkly, the objects of worship speaking:

> *"And We shall extract from every community a witness and say: 'Produce your proof.' Then they shall know that the truth belongs to God, and there shall vanish from them that which they used to fabricate."* (Qur'an 28:75)

The pattern is consistent across the Qur'an: whatever or whoever was set beside God will, at the moment of judgment, be found to have been an unrelated party. The worship was unilateral. The love, the fear, the sacrifice, the years of devotion — all of it flowed in one direction and arrived nowhere.

This is the same disavowal Satan makes, in words the sūrah has already prepared for (see 35:6):

> *"Truly I am quit of thee. Truly I fear God, the Lord of the worlds."* (Qur'an 59:16)

**"None Informs You Like One Who Is Aware"**

The verse ends with a sentence that appears, at first reading, to be a general maxim: *wa-mā yunabbiʾuka mithlu khabīr* — "and none informs you like one who is Aware."

*Yunabbiʾuka* is from the root *n-b-ʾ*, to bring news, to inform of something weighty; *nabīʾ*, news, and *nabī*, prophet, come from the same root. *Khabīr* is from *kh-b-r*, to know a thing by acquaintance with its inner reality — not merely to have information about it but to have tested it. *Khabar* is a report; *khabīr* is the one who knows the matter from inside.

The exegetes give three readings, and all three are supported.

The general reading (al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn ʿAjībah): no one can inform you the way God informs you. Information from any other source is second-hand and partial; information from the *Khabīr* is direct.

The contextual reading (al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn ʿAjībah): the statement concerns the idols specifically — only God tells you the truth about them, because only He knows what they are. Your priests, your ancestors, and your own assumptions tell you something else; God tells you what is so.

The eschatological reading (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī): the statement concerns the Day of Resurrection — the One who informs you of what will happen on that Day is the One who will conduct it.

Al-Rāzī adds a decisive consideration: this matter pertains to the Unseen, and cannot be deduced by reasoning. You cannot prove by argument that an idol will disown you on the Day of Judgment. That has to be told to you, and its truth depends entirely on the reliability of the informer. The verse's final clause is therefore an argument for revelation itself: certain truths are accessible only by report, and the only report worth trusting is the report of the *Khabīr*.

**The Argument From the Quality of the Informant**

This closing sentence contains a principle of epistemology that runs through the entire Qur'an. When God says *"and none informs you like one who is Aware,"* He is inviting the reader to evaluate the source before evaluating the message.

The test is threefold. Does the informer know the matter directly? Is He free of interest in deceiving? Does He have the capacity to communicate clearly? On all three counts the Qur'an's claim is that God alone qualifies, and that every other informer fails at least one. Human beings do not know the Unseen; those who claim to inform about it either lie or speculate. Created beings may know and yet have an interest in concealment. And even the honest and the knowing communicate partially, because their knowledge is partial.

Applied to ordinary life, the principle is immediately useful. A great deal of human misery comes from taking information about matters one cannot see from people who cannot see them either — advice about the future from those who do not know it, assurances about the heart from those who do not have access to it, predictions about outcomes from those who have no information. The verse does not forbid listening to people. It forbids treating a non-*Khabīr* as though he were one.

**The Worshipper's Actual Position**

Read as a whole, verse 14 describes a person standing before an object, calling to it, unheard; whose call, if heard, would not be answered; and whose devotion will be publicly repudiated by the object itself. The verse is not an abstract theological proposition. It is a portrait, and its purpose is to make the portrait recognisable to the person inside it.

The Qur'an's method throughout the Makkan period is to describe idolatry in this concrete, almost documentary way, because idolatry is not primarily a belief system but a practice — hours spent, wealth given, requests made, hopes attached. The argument against it is therefore not that it is illogical in the abstract but that it does not work in the particular. Call. Nothing hears. Call again. Nothing answers. Wait until the Day. The thing you called will say it never knew you.

And then, after all that, one sentence: *and none informs you like one who is Aware.* The verse that has just dismantled the worship ends by identifying who told you the truth about it. The reader is left with a choice that is no longer about the idols but about the informer.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:15

> **O mankind! You are needful of God; and He is the Self-Sufficient, the Praised.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Definition of the Creature**

Eleven words in Arabic, and they contain the entire metaphysics of Islam's understanding of the human being. *Yā ayyuhā al-nās antum al-fuqarāʾu ilā Allāh* — "O mankind, you are the poor toward God." *Wa-Allāhu huwa al-ghaniyyu al-ḥamīd* — "and God, He is the Self-Sufficient, the Praised."

The verse is built as a perfect opposition. On one side, a plural noun in the definite form — *al-fuqarāʾ*, "the poor ones" — preceded by an emphatic pronoun *antum*, "you are." On the other, a Name with the emphatic pronoun *huwa*, "He is." The two sides are separated by a single preposition, *ilā*, "toward." Everything the human being is, is oriented toward God; everything God is, is complete in Himself.

**Faqīr: The One Whose Backbone Is Broken**

The word *faqīr* deserves attention, because its root is more physical than its modern usage suggests. *F-q-r* is the root of *faqārah*, a vertebra, and *fiqār*, the spine. The classical lexicographers explain that *faqīr* originally meant one whose backbone has been broken or displaced — a person crippled by injury, unable to stand straight, dependent on others for the most basic movement. Its companion word *miskīn*, from *s-k-n*, stillness, means one who has been brought to a halt.

When the Qur'an says *antum al-fuqarāʾ ilā Allāh*, it is not making an economic statement. It is saying that the human spine — the structural principle of the human being, that which makes him upright and independent — is broken in relation to God, and that nothing he does can set it. The upright posture of the human being, which the Qur'an elsewhere makes a sign of his honour (*fī aḥsani taqwīm*, 95:4), is here presented as a posture he cannot maintain on his own.

The definite article matters too. Not "you are poor toward God" as one attribute among many, but "you are *the* poor" — as though poverty were the species-name of humanity. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī both note this: the article restricts the description, as if to say poverty is your defining quality, the way heat is the defining quality of fire.

**Ghanī: The One Who Needs Nothing**

*Al-Ghanī* is from the root *gh-n-y*, to be free of need, to be sufficient unto oneself, to be wealthy in the sense of requiring nothing from outside. It is one of the Divine Names and appears in the Qur'an with *al-Ḥamīd* in this verse and in 22:64, and alone in numerous places:

> *"And to God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is in the earth. Truly God is the Self-Sufficient, the Praised."* (Qur'an 22:64)

> *"Your Lord is the Self-Sufficient, the Possessor of Mercy."* (Qur'an 6:133)

> *"God is the Rich, and you are the poor. And if you turn away, He will replace you with another people; then they will not be your likes."* (Qur'an 47:38)

The pairing in 47:38 is nearly identical to Fāṭir's, and it adds the consequence — which is verses 16 and 17 of this sūrah.

Al-Ghanī means not only that God does not need human worship but that He does not need anything at all: not the angels' obedience, not the prophets' faithfulness, not the praise of those who praise. The ḥadīth qudsī that Muslim records from Abū Dharr states this in the most expansive form the tradition possesses:

> *"O My servants! Were the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, to stand upon one hill and ask Me, and I gave each of them his request, that would not diminish what I possess — except as a needle diminishes [the sea] when it is dipped into it."* (Muslim 2577)

And earlier in the same report:

> *"O My servants! Were the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, to be as pious as the most pious heart among you, that would not add anything to My dominion."* (Muslim 2577)

Nothing human being can do increases God or decreases Him. That is the meaning of *al-Ghanī* in practice.

**Al-Ḥamīd: Praised Whether or Not He Is Praised**

The second Name, *al-Ḥamīd*, is from the root *ḥ-m-d*, praise. The intensive form indicates that praise belongs to Him as an intrinsic quality, not as an acquired one. A thing that is *maḥmūd* is praised by others; a thing that is *ḥamīd* is praiseworthy in itself, whether or not anyone notices.

Al-Maybudī draws the sharpest inference from this, as the source records: only those who fully realise poverty truly praise God. The reasoning is that praise from a person who believes himself self-sufficient is contaminated — it is gratitude for what he thinks he has earned, or admiration for what he thinks he could have produced. Only the person who knows that everything he has is a loan from the *Ghanī* can praise the *Ḥamīd* without reserve.

The two Names therefore work together to describe a single relationship: He is worthy of all praise because He is the source of all good, and He needs no praise because He is the source of all good. The human being praises for his own sake; God is praised for His own.

**The Two Povertys: Of Beginning and Of Every Moment**

Ibn ʿAjībah explains the verse in the terms al-Rāzī had used earlier in the sūrah: God is completely Self-Sufficient and human beings are completely poor — in need of Him both for being brought into existence and for being provided with sustenance. This is the distinction between the poverty of origination and the poverty of continuation, and the second is the one people forget.

Nobody doubts that he needed God to be created. Many doubt that they need Him now. Yet the continuation of existence is not self-guaranteeing: a thing that begins by dependency does not become independent by continuing. The Qur'an states this through the Name that carries it:

> *"God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Ever-Watchful. Slumber seizes Him not, nor sleep."* (Qur'an 2:255)

*Al-Qayyūm* — the Self-Subsisting, the One by Whom all else subsists. The verse of the Throne pairs the Living with the Sustainer precisely because the second follows from the first: He is alive in Himself and everything else lives through Him.

**Al-Tustarī: The Ordinance of Poverty**

The source preserves one of the most important statements in the early spiritual tradition on this verse. Al-Tustarī (d. 283/896) said:

> *"You depend upon Him in your very selves, for when God created creation, He ordained poverty for His servants in relation to Him, while He is the Rich. So whosoever claims wealth has been veiled from God. And whosoever shows his poverty in relation to Him, God will join his poverty to His wealth. The servant must feel poverty toward Him in his innermost being (*sirr*), cutting himself off from all that is other than Him, so that his servanthood may be pure; for pure servanthood is abasement and humility."* (al-Tustarī, as cited in the commentary on 35:15)

Three claims are made here, and they are worth separating.

First, poverty is *ordained* — it is not an accident of circumstance but a determination made at creation. A person is not poor toward God because he happens to lack resources; he is poor toward God by constitution, and resources do not alter it.

Second, the claim of wealth is a veil. *Fa-man addaʿā al-ghinā ḥujiba ʿan Allāh.* The person who presents himself as self-sufficient has placed the one thing between himself and God that God cannot work around, because God does not fill a vessel that declares itself full.

Third, the cure is disclosure, not acquisition: show your poverty, and God joins it to His wealth. The verse's preposition *ilā* — toward — becomes the whole of the spiritual life: the orientation of the empty toward the Full.

**A Saying Often Quoted, Rarely Verified**

The spiritual tradition circulates the phrase *faqrī fakhrī* — "my poverty is my pride" — as a Prophetic saying. It is quoted constantly in Sufi literature and almost never with a chain. The scholars of ḥadīth have judged it baseless as an attribution to the Prophet ﷺ. It should not be cited as ḥadīth.

Its meaning, however, is entirely consistent with this verse and with the practice of the Prophet ﷺ, who sought refuge from the state opposite to poverty toward God and described it in a supplication the tradition preserves authentically:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in Thee from anxiety and sorrow, from incapacity and laziness, from cowardice and miserliness, from the burden of debt and from the domination of men."* (al-Bukhārī 6369)

The distinction the tradition draws is between poverty *toward God*, which is a station of honour, and poverty *toward creatures*, which is a condition of humiliation. The first is what verse 15 describes. The second is what the Prophetic supplication asks to be spared.

**The Modern Ideology the Verse Contradicts**

Contemporary culture has made self-sufficiency its highest practical ideal. The vocabulary of modern life is the vocabulary of independence: financial independence, emotional independence, "owning your story," "not needing anyone." Dependence is treated as a developmental stage to be outgrown, and the goal of maturity is defined as the point at which one requires nothing.

Verse 15 states that this goal is metaphysically unreachable and that the attempt to reach it is the definition of the veil. The human being does not become independent by growing; he becomes more clearly aware that he never was. The verse is not a humiliation of humanity — it is addressed to *yā ayyuhā al-nās*, all mankind, and it is offered as a fact rather than a rebuke. Its practical effect is to relocate dignity: your worth does not rest on not needing, because you need; it rests on being needed-by and cared-for by the One who does not.

There is a psychological consequence that anyone who has lived long enough will recognise. The person who has organised his identity around self-sufficiency cannot accept help, cannot ask for it, and cannot be comforted, because each of those would falsify the identity. The person who has accepted that he is *faqīr ilā Allāh* can ask, can receive, and can be grateful — and gratitude is the only relation to a gift that does not diminish the recipient.

The verse ends, accordingly, with *al-Ḥamīd*. The poor are the ones who can praise.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:16

> **If He will, He could remove you and bring a new creation.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional That Holds Your Existence**

*In yashaʾ yudhhibkum wa-yaʾti bi-khalqin jadīd* — "If He wills, He removes you and brings a new creation." The verse consists of a condition and two consequences, and the condition is the entire point: *in yashaʾ* — if He wills.

The continuation of your existence, of your species, of your civilisation, and of your family depends on a single factor over which you have no influence: the Divine Will. Not on your strength, not on your numbers, not on your technology, not on your virtue. The verse does not say God will remove you; it says the removal is available to Him at will, and that nothing prevents it except His not willing it.

Al-Rāzī reads the verse in context as a demonstration of God's Self-Sufficiency following directly from verse 15, and of human poverty in its most extreme form: God's making people disappear depends only upon God's Will. The sequence is deliberate — first the statement of the relationship (you are poor, He is Self-Sufficient), then the demonstration of what that relationship means in fact (He could end you and start again).

**"He Could Remove You"**

The verb *yudhhib* is from the root *dh-h-b*, to go, to depart, to cause to vanish. It is a word of total removal, not of reduction or punishment — the thing does not become less, it goes. The Qur'an uses the same verb in a series of parallel warnings, each addressed to a people who believed themselves permanent:

> *"And thy Lord is the Self-Sufficient, the Possessor of Mercy. If He wills, He removes you and brings whomsoever He wills after you, as He produced you from the offspring of another people."* (Qur'an 6:133)

> *"O you who believe! Whoever among you turns back from his religion — God will bring a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble toward the believers, mighty toward the disbelievers, striving in the way of God, and fearing not the reproach of any reproacher. That is the Bounty of God; He gives it to whomsoever He will. And God is All-Embracing, Knowing."* (Qur'an 5:54)

> *"And if you turn away, He will replace you with another people; then they will not be your likes."* (Qur'an 47:38)

> *"Is He not Able to give life to the dead?"* (Qur'an 75:40)

The pattern in each case is identical: your position is not secured by your presence in it. God does not need you to occupy the place He has given you, and He has the capacity and the demonstrated willingness to fill it with others.

**"And Bring a New Creation"**

*Wa-yaʾti bi-khalqin jadīd* — "and bring a new creation." The commentators give two readings, and both are worth holding. Ibn Kathīr explains it as God replacing them with other people. Al-Rāzī develops the second: either another people who already exist, or an entirely new people whom God will create, better and more perfect than the previous ones.

The difference between the two readings is not trivial. The first says replacement is a matter of selection — there are others available. The second says replacement is a matter of creation — God does not even need there to be others; He can produce them. The second is the stronger statement of power, and the Qur'an elsewhere supports it explicitly:

> *"Hast thou not seen that God created the heavens and the earth in truth? If He wills, He removes you and brings a new creation. And that is not great for God."* (Qur'an 14:19–20)

The parallel in Ibrāhīm is so close that it includes the following verse of Fāṭir almost verbatim, which confirms that the two passages are one teaching stated in two sūrahs.

**The Historical Form of the Warning**

The verse is not merely theoretical, and the Qur'an does not leave it at the level of possibility. It repeatedly points to actual cases in which a people was removed and replaced:

> *"How many a generation We destroyed before them, and thou hearest not a whisper of them, or perceivest a murmur."* (Qur'an 20:128)

> *"And how many a city We destroyed while it was wrongdoing, so it is fallen upon its roofs, and a deserted well and a lofty palace!"* (Qur'an 22:45)

> *"And We did not destroy any city except that it had warners, as a reminder — and We were never unjust."* (Qur'an 26:208–209)

The Arabian Peninsula itself is a museum of removed peoples — the dwellings of Thamūd carved into rock at al-Ḥijr, which the Prophet ﷺ instructed his army to pass through quickly and without drawing water:
