> *"Do not enter upon these punished people except while weeping; and if you cannot weep, then do not enter upon them, lest what befell them befall you."* (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980)

The instruction is significant for the verse's meaning: the removal of a people is not an ancient abstraction but a physical fact visible on the route between Madinah and Syria, and the Prophet treated the site as an active warning rather than an archaeological curiosity.

**What the Verse Says About Human Importance**

The verse is uncomfortable, and its discomfort is deliberate. It tells the human being that the universe does not require him. He is not load-bearing. The world will not stop if his civilisation ends, and God's glory will not diminish if his species is replaced.

This is the exact opposite of the modern assumption that human existence is cosmically indispensable — an assumption that produces both arrogance (we matter so much that the universe depends on us) and despair (if we fail, everything fails). The verse removes both. You are not indispensable; therefore your failure is not cosmic. You are not necessary; therefore your survival is entirely gift.

The ḥadīth qudsī of Muslim states the same truth from the other direction: were all creation as pious as the most pious heart, it would add nothing to God's dominion. Human righteousness is not a contribution to God. It is a benefit to the one who performs it.

**The Corporate Form: Nations Turn Over**

Applied to collectivities, the verse establishes a principle of historical turnover that the Qur'an treats as a law:

> *"And those days We alternate among the people."* (Qur'an 3:140)

> *"And if God willed, He would have made you one community, but that He may test you in what He has given you."* (Qur'an 5:48)

No nation's dominance is permanent, and no nation's dominance is deserved in the sense of being owed. The rise and fall of peoples is described in the Qur'an not as a cycle of fortune but as an exercise of Divine Will with a moral dimension: God removes people who become corrupt and brings people who will, for a time, do better. 5:54 gives the criteria of the replacement — humility toward the believers, firmness toward oppression, striving, and indifference to reproach. Those are not measures of wealth or military capacity, which is itself a commentary on what actually preserves a community.

**The Personal Form**

There is a third application the spiritual commentators draw, and it is the one that makes the verse practical for an individual reader. *Yudhhibkum* — He removes you — is true of each person individually, not only of species and nations. You will be removed. The place you occupy in your family, your work, your community will be filled. The verse does not threaten this; it states it as available to God's Will at any moment, which means it is happening continuously and will complete at a term you do not know.

The response the tradition draws is not morbidity but ordering. A person who knows his position is contingent stops treating it as permanent, and a person who stops treating his position as permanent begins to use it rather than defend it. The energy ordinarily spent on preserving status is released for the work the status was given for. This is the practical meaning of remembering that God *could* bring a new creation: it makes the current one lighter in the hand.

The verse that follows supplies the theological conclusion — that none of this costs God anything.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:17

> **That is no great matter for God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Words That Complete the Argument**

*Wa-mā dhālika ʿalā Allāhi bi-ʿazīz* — "And that is not, upon God, hard." The verse is a single clause in Arabic, seven words, and it exists to close the door that verse 16 opened.

Verse 16 stated a possibility: God could remove you and bring a new creation. The human mind, hearing this, immediately begins to calculate the cost — how enormous a thing would that be? What effort would it require? Verse 17 refuses to let the calculation proceed: it is not great, not hard, not difficult, not burdensome. *ʿAzīz* here means unattainable through difficulty, arduous, taxing.

The preposition *ʿalā* — "upon God" — carries the sense of bearing a weight. The verse says no weight falls upon God from any act of creation or destruction. The negation is emphasised by the particle *bi-* attached to *ʿazīz*, which in Arabic grammar strengthens a negation: "not at all hard."

**The Same Root as the Same Name**

There is a linguistic feature of this verse that the classical commentators noticed and that is easily lost in translation. The word *ʿazīz* comes from the root *ʿ-z-z* — the same root as *al-ʿAzīz*, one of God's Names, and the same root as the *ʿizzah* (might) of verse 10. The root's core meaning is hardness, strength, and invincibility.

Applied to God as a Name, *al-ʿAzīz* means the Invincible, the One who cannot be overcome. Applied to an act, *ʿazīz* means hard, resistant, difficult to accomplish. The verse uses the word in the second sense and denies it of God: no act is *ʿazīz* — hard — for the *ʿAzīz* — the Invincible. The wordplay is exact and it carries a doctrinal point: God's might is not the kind of strength that strains. Human strength is achieved through effort and exhausted by use; Divine strength is a state, not an expenditure.

Sūrah Yāsīn makes the same point at the conclusion of its resurrection argument:

> *"Is He who created the heavens and the earth not able to create the like of them? Yes indeed, and He is the All-Knowing Creator. His command, when He wills a thing, is only to say to it, 'Be,' and it is."* (Qur'an 36:81–82)

*Kun fa-yakūn* — "Be, and it is." No process, no time, no effort. The command and the result are simultaneous in the divine act, whatever the interval may be in the created order.

**Where Else the Qur'an Says This**

The phrase *wa-mā dhālika ʿalā Allāhi bi-ʿazīz* occurs three times in the Qur'an: here, at 14:20, and at 14:19 in adjacent form. In Ibrāhīm it follows the identical statement about removal and replacement:

> *"If He wills, He removes you and brings a new creation. And that is not hard for God."* (Qur'an 14:19–20)

A closely related formula appears where the subject is human escape rather than divine action:

> *"Those who disbelieved will not escape upon the earth, and they have no protector apart from God."* (Qur'an 11:20, in context of God's ability)

And the same idea in different words at the beginning of the story of Maryam, where Zechariah asks how he can have a son in his old age:

> *"He said: 'So it shall be. Thy Lord has said: That is easy for Me (*hayyin*), and indeed I created you before, when thou wast nothing.'"* (Qur'an 19:9)

The Qur'an's habit is consistent: after any statement of divine action that would exhaust a creature, it adds a clause denying that the action costs God anything. The clause protects the reader from anthropomorphism — from imagining God as a very powerful worker rather than as the One whose will and act are one.

**Why the Verse Is Necessary**

One might ask why the statement is needed at all. Verse 16 already asserted the power. What does verse 17 add?

It adds the removal of a residue. Human beings naturally conceive of power as effort — the strong man lifts the heavy stone *with difficulty*, and we call him strong because he overcomes it. When the Qur'an says God could destroy and recreate humanity, a listener trained on human experience hears an immense labour. Verse 17 corrects the model: there is no labour. The destruction and the recreation are equally effortless, and both are equally within a will that is not stretched by either.

Al-Qurṭubī explains the verse in these terms: the statement underlines that what appears to human beings as an unimaginable act is, in relation to God's power, without magnitude. The greatness and smallness of things are properties of creatures, not of the Creator's relation to them. Creating one atom and creating all existence are not two different tasks for God, because neither is a task.

**The Theological Balance the Verse Preserves**

There is a danger in verses of this kind that the tradition addressed directly. If the destruction and replacement of humanity costs God nothing, does humanity matter? The verse answers by refusing to answer in those terms — and the sūrah answers elsewhere. Verse 15 established that God is *al-Ḥamīd*, the Praised, and *al-Ghanī*, the Self-Sufficient; verse 41 will establish that He maintains the heavens and the earth lest they fall apart, and describes Him as *al-Ḥalīm al-Ghafūr*, the Clement, the Forgiving, precisely because He continues to uphold a world in which He is denied.

The two truths are held together: the world is contingent to the point of nothingness, and it is sustained to the point of permanence. Neither is cancelled by the other. God's effortless power is not indifference; it is the reason the world survives its own fragility at all. A creation that depended on effort would depend on the continued willingness of the one making the effort; a creation that depends on a will that finds nothing difficult depends on nothing but the constancy of that will.

**The Human Lesson: On the Weight of Things**

There is an application of this verse to human life that the spiritual commentators draw, and it is worth stating because it is not obvious.

People carry a great many things they believe to be heavy: an unresolvable problem, a person who will not change, a loss that cannot be undone, a situation with no exit. The heaviness is real, and the verse does not deny it. What the verse denies is that heaviness is a property of things in themselves. Heaviness is a relation between a thing and the one carrying it. The stone is heavy for you; it is not heavy for the crane.

The practical instruction of *wa-mā dhālika ʿalā Allāhi bi-ʿazīz* is therefore about transfer. Whatever you are carrying that has become unmanageable is unmanageable *for you*. The verse invites you to place it in relation to a Power for which it is not great. This is the meaning of *tawakkul* in its most concrete form — not the abandonment of effort but the abandonment of the belief that the outcome is heavy in a way that only you can bear.

The verse is short because the point is short: *that is not hard for God.* Everything else in the human situation follows from believing it.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:18

> **And none shall bear the burden of another. And though one burdened heavily calls for his burden to be borne, naught of it will be borne, even if it be kin. Thou warnest only those who fear their Lord unseen and perform the prayer. And whosoever purifies himself purifies himself only for his own soul. And unto God is the journey's end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Law of Individual Liability**

*Wa-lā taziru wāziratun wizra ukhrā* — "and no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another." The verse states in seven Arabic words the fundamental rule of Islamic moral and eschatological law, and it is one of the handful of Qur'anic statements repeated in identical or near-identical form across the revelation: at 6:164, 17:15, 39:7, and 53:38.

The root *w-z-r* means to carry a heavy load. *Wizr* is the burden, and by extension the sin — a sin is a weight. *Wazīr*, the minister or vizier, is the one who carries the ruler's burden; the same root produces the Qur'anic term for the shelter sought on the Day of Judgment, *wazr*, in 75:11: *"Nay! There is no refuge"* — literally, no shelter, no place to carry the load.

The verb *taziru* is in the feminine form, and so is *wāziratun*. The grammarians explain that the feminine here is generic — the *nafs*, the soul, is feminine in Arabic, and the statement concerns every soul. The construction is absolute: no soul carrying a burden will carry another's burden.

Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, and al-Qurṭubī all gloss it the same way: no one bears the sins or the duties of another. Ibn ʿAjībah adds the theological ground: this points to God's Justice. It is unjust for a person to be punished for what he did not do, and the verse removes the possibility at the level of principle.

**Reconciling the Apparent Contradiction**

The verse raises an immediate difficulty, which the commentators addressed rather than avoided. Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt states:

> *"And they will surely bear their own burdens, and other burdens along with their own; and they will surely be questioned on the Day of Resurrection concerning what they used to fabricate."* (Qur'an 29:13)

If none shall bear the burden of another, how can some bear burdens along with their own? The reconciliation given by the exegetes — and it is precise — is that the two verses describe different burdens. The burden a person carries for another is not the other's sin transferred to him; it is *his own* sin of causing it. The corrupt leader who misleads bears the sin of having been misled himself, and the sin of having misled others. Both are his. Nobody's debt is paid by someone else; the person who led another into error has committed a second, distinct error.

The Prophetic tradition states the principle with its positive counterpart:

> *"Whoever calls to guidance has the like of the reward of those who follow him, without that diminishing their rewards at all. And whoever calls to misguidance has the like of the burden of those who follow him, without that diminishing their burdens at all."* (Muslim 2674)

Note the double protection in that report: the follower's reward is not reduced when the caller shares it, and the follower's burden is not reduced when the caller shares it. Sharing occurs without transfer. That is the mechanism by which both verses remain true.

**"Even If It Be Kin"**

The second clause intensifies the first with a specific case. *Wa-in tadʿu muthqalatun ilā ḥimlihi lā yuḥmal minhu shayʾun wa-law kāna dhā qurbā* — "and though one heavily burdened calls for her burden to be carried, nothing of it will be carried, even if he be a kinsman."

*Muthqalatun* is the feminine passive participle of *athqala*, "to be laden heavily" — the one crushed under weight. She calls (*tadʿu*) to her burden (*ilā ḥimlihā*) — the commentators read this as calling someone to carry it, or calling for help with it. The answer is a passive: *lā yuḥmal*, "nothing will be carried." There is no agent to name, because there is no one able.

Then the final restriction: *wa-law kāna dhā qurbā* — even if the one called were a relative. Blood relation, which in Arabian society was the strongest of all bonds and the basis of every claim of protection, is explicitly voided at this point. The Qur'an states the same void elsewhere with unbearable concreteness:

> *"On the Day when a man shall flee from his brother, and his mother and his father, and his wife and his sons — every man of them that day shall have a concern sufficient for him."* (Qur'an 80:34–37)

> *"And there is none of you but shall come to it. It is with thy Lord a matter decreed."* (Qur'an 19:71)

And the Prophetic practice confirms the verse in the most personal way possible. When the command came to warn his own clan — *"And warn thy nearest kinsfolk"* (26:214) — the Prophet ﷺ gathered them and said:

> *"O Banū ʿAbd al-Manāf! I can avail you nothing against God. O ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib! I can avail you nothing against God. O Ṣafiyyah, aunt of the Messenger of God! I can avail you nothing against God. O Fāṭimah bint Muḥammad! Ask me of my wealth what thou wilt — I can avail thee nothing against God."* (al-Bukhārī 2753; Muslim 206)

The nearest kinship in Islam — the daughter of the Prophet — is addressed with the same sentence as the rest. Verse 18 is not a theory that the tradition softened in practice; it was declared to the Prophet's own family by the Prophet himself.

**Intercession Under This Law**

The verse is sometimes read as though it abolished intercession (*shafāʿah*) entirely, and the tradition has consistently resisted that reading. The Qur'an affirms intercession in numerous places while restricting it absolutely:

> *"God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Ever-Watchful... Who is there that can intercede with Him except by His Leave?"* (Qur'an 2:255)

> *"And they intercede not except for whom He approves."* (Qur'an 21:28)

The reconciliation is straightforward once stated: intercession does not transfer a burden. It is not a kinsman carrying a relative's load; it is a permission granted by the Owner of the load to honour the one who asks. The burden remains the sinner's; the reduction of it is God's act, not the intercessor's carriage. Verse 18 denies the second mechanism, not the first.

**"Thou Warnest Only Those Who Fear Their Lord Unseen"**

The verse shifts from a universal law to a specific function: *innamā tundhiru alladhīna yakhshawna rabbahum bi-l-ghaybi wa-aqāmū al-ṣalāh*. "Thou warnest only those who fear their Lord unseen and have performed the prayer."

The particle *innamā* — "only" — is restrictive, and the restriction is not about the Prophet's task (he warns everyone) but about its efficacy. Warning reaches those who already have the capacity to be warned. Al-Ṭabarī explains: those who fear their Lord in the unseen — that is, when no one sees them, and in matters they cannot verify — and those who establish the prayer.

*Bi-l-ghayb* carries both senses and the commentators hold both: fear of God in His absence from sight (belief without seeing), and fear of God in one's own hidden life (reverence where no observer exists). The second is the harder and the rarer. A person who fears God only when watched has not fear but prudence.

Sūrah Yā Sīn states the same limitation:

> *"Thou warnest only him who follows the Reminder and fears the Compassionate in the unseen. So give him tidings of forgiveness and a generous reward."* (Qur'an 36:11)

**"Whosoever Purifies Himself, Purifies Himself for His Own Soul"**

*Wa-man tazakkā fa-innamā yatazakkā li-nafsihi* — the root *z-k-y* combines two meanings that English separates: purity and growth. To *tazakkā* is to purify oneself and thereby to increase. Zakāh, the alms-tax, is so called because giving purifies the wealth and makes it grow.

The clause is a statement of moral reflexivity, and the source correctly identifies it as a major Qur'anic principle: the ultimate impact of actions, good or bad, falls always upon the one who performs them. See 2:9, 2:272, 3:69, 6:123, 8:60, 9:34–35, 10:23, and 29:6:

> *"And whosoever strives, strives only for himself. Truly God is Free of need of all creation."* (Qur'an 29:6)

The pairing of 29:6 with this verse gives the complete doctrine: God gains nothing from your purification, and you lose nothing by neglecting it except yourself.

**"And Unto God Is the Journey's End"**

*Wa-ilā Allāhi al-maṣīr* — *maṣīr* from the root *ṣ-y-r*, to travel toward, to arrive; it is the place or state of arrival. The phrase appears at 2:285, 3:28, 8:44, 22:76, 24:42, 31:15, 35:18, 40:43, 42:15, 48:14, 49:4, 50:43, 57:5, 60:3, 62:8, 64:3. Its repetition is a rhythm in the Qur'an — a phrase that closes arguments about conduct by naming the destination.

The verse that began with the impossibility of carrying another's burden ends with the reason it is impossible: everyone is going to the same place, and each travels alone. The image is of a road on which every traveller carries his own pack, in which no one can lift another's, and at the end of which a single Arriver receives them all.

The practical conclusion the verse draws is not isolation but responsibility. You cannot be saved by your father, your teacher, your community, or your era. You also cannot be damned by them. What is available to you is what is asked of you: fear of God where no one sees, the established prayer, and the purification that returns to your own soul. And the road ends with God.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:19

> **Not equal are the blind and the seeing,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Beginning of the Sūrah's Four Pairs**

Verses 19 to 22 form a single continuous sentence in the Qur'an — four pairs of unequals, stated without a verb of comparison beyond the one that governs them all, *mā yastawī*, "are not equal." The construction is a litany, and its power comes from accumulation: the blind and the seeing; the darkness and the light; the shade and the scorching heat; the living and the dead.

The verse-by-verse method requires each to be treated separately, and each deserves separate treatment, because the four pairs are not synonyms repeated for effect. They operate on four different registers — sight, illumination, bodily comfort, and life itself — and each register carries its own argument.

**"Are Not Equal": The Verb That Judges**

*Yastawī* is from the root *s-w-y*, to be level, equal, equivalent, to be on the same plane. The same root gives *sawāʾ*, the middle or the level; *mustawī*, levelled; and *istiwāʾ*, the Divine Act of establishing Himself upon the Throne (20:5). To say two things *lā yastawiyān* is to say they cannot be placed on the same level — not that one is slightly better, but that the attempt to compare them is a category error.

The Qur'an uses this formula repeatedly, always to overturn an equation that people have accepted:

> *"Are those who know equal to those who do not know? Only those of understanding take heed."* (Qur'an 39:9)

> *"And the blind and the seeing are not equal; nor are those who believe and perform righteous deeds and the evildoer. Little do you reflect."* (Qur'an 40:58)

> *"Say: Is the blind equal to the seeing? Or are the darknesses equal to the light?"* (Qur'an 13:16)

> *"Not equal are the companions of the Fire and the companions of the Garden. The companions of the Garden are the triumphant."* (Qur'an 59:20)

The formula's rhetorical function is to expose a false equivalence that the listener has been living with. In every case, the two terms being compared look similar from the outside — the knower and the ignorant both speak, the believer and the evildoer both act, the blind man and the seeing man both walk the same street — and the verse insists that similarity of appearance conceals inequality of substance.

**Two Readings: The Metaphorical and the Doctrinal**

The commentators give three readings of this pair, and the source records all three.

The first, held by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn ʿAjībah: *seeing* is a metaphor for belief and *blindness* for disbelief. The pair continues the sūrah's central contrast between the two communities.

The second: the pair is a metaphor for knowledge and ignorance, parallel to 39:9 — *"Are those who know equal to those who do not know?"*

The third, also from Ibn ʿAjībah: the contrast is between God and the idols. God is the Seeing; the idols are blind, lifeless, and insensate. This reading connects verse 19 directly back to verses 13 and 14, where the idols are shown to own nothing and hear nothing.

The three readings are compatible, and the Qur'an's habit of allowing multiple compatible senses is part of its density. The pair works as a statement about belief, as a statement about knowledge, and as a statement about the difference between the Creator and the created — because all three are the same difference seen from three angles.

**The Physical Reading and Its Exclusion**

An important clarification: the verse is not about bodily blindness. The Qur'an does not treat physical blindness as a defect of the person or a sign of divine displeasure, and the tradition is emphatic on this. ʿAbdullāh ibn Umm Maktūm, a blind Companion, was among the earliest Muslims and was entrusted by the Prophet ﷺ with leading the prayer in Madinah during his absences. Sūrah ʿAbasa opens with a divine correction of the Prophet ﷺ himself for turning away from him:

> *"He frowned and turned away, because the blind man came to him. And what would make thee know — perhaps he might purify himself, or be reminded, and the reminder benefit him?"* (Qur'an 80:1–4)

The blind man in that passage is the one whom God honours, and the busy notables are the ones reproached. Any reading of 35:19 that treated blindness as a mark of deficiency would contradict this sūrah and the Prophetic practice.

The Prophetic tradition also contains the saying preserved in the commentary literature, which states the metaphorical sense explicitly: the one who is blind is not the one whose eyes are blind, but the one whose inner sight (*baṣīrah*) is blind. The Qur'an itself says the same:

> *"For it is not the eyes that are blind, but blind are the hearts that are within the breasts."* (Qur'an 22:46)

That verse is the key to verse 19. The blindness in question is located in the heart, and the heart's blindness does not prevent a man from being among the most perceptive of people in worldly matters, nor does the heart's sight require functioning eyes.

**Baṣīr and Aʿmā: Two Words, Two Conditions**

The Arabic terms are not symmetrical in form, and the asymmetry is meaningful. *Aʿmā* is a plain adjective meaning blind — a state, a condition one is in. *Baṣīr* is an intensive form from the root *b-ṣ-r*, meaning possessing keen sight, perceptive, one who sees into things. The same root gives *baṣīrah*, insight, and *al-Baṣīr*, the Divine Name of the All-Seeing.

The verse therefore does not contrast the blind with the merely sighted. It contrasts the blind with the *perceptive* — the one who sees with penetration. This matters, because many people have functioning eyes and no perception. They look at evidence and do not see it; they watch a life unfold and do not read it. The intensive form excludes them from the second term of the pair: to be *baṣīr* is to see meaning, not merely light.

**What the Blindness Consists In**

If the blindness is of the heart, its content can be specified, and the sūrah has already specified it. The blind man of verse 19 is the one described in verse 8: the person whose evil deeds have been beautified to him until he sees them as good. His blindness is not an absence of information; it is a distortion of valuation. He can see facts and cannot see worth. He can calculate cost and cannot calculate consequence. He can recognise a good opportunity and cannot recognise a good deed.

This is why the Qur'an treats the condition as unequal to seeing rather than merely inferior to it. A person with bad eyesight can be corrected by lenses; a person whose standard of value is inverted cannot be corrected by more information, because he will process the information through the inverted standard. The two are not on the same level, and no amount of argument between them changes that — which is why the verse simply states the inequality and moves on.

**The Pair That Opens a Sequence**

That verse 19 stands first among the four pairs is significant. Sight is the primary human sense, the one by which the world is organised, and the Qur'an repeatedly asks the reader to look:

> *"Say: Consider what is in the heavens and the earth. But the signs and the warnings avail not a people who do not believe."* (Qur'an 10:101)

By beginning with the blind and the seeing, the sūrah establishes that the difference between the two communities is a difference in perception, and that the three pairs that follow — darkness and light, shade and scorching heat, life and death — are elaborations of what perception reveals. The darkness and the light are the conditions in which one sees or cannot see. The shade and the heat are what one experiences as a result. The living and the dead are what one becomes.

The sequence is a diagnosis and a prognosis in four steps. Verse 19 names the defect. Verses 20 to 22 name the consequences.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:20

> **nor the darkness and the light,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Plural Against a Singular**

Four Arabic words: *wa-lā al-ẓulumātu wa-lā al-nūr* — "nor the darknesses and the light." The first term is plural, the second singular, and the Qur'an maintains this asymmetry with almost unbroken consistency throughout.

*Ẓulumāt* is the plural of *ẓulmah*, darkness. *Nūr* has no plural in Qur'anic usage when it refers to divine or true light. Every instance of the pairing uses the plural for darkness and the singular for light:

> *"God is the Protector of those who believe; He brings them out of the darknesses (*al-ẓulumāt*) into the light (*al-nūr*). And those who disbelieve — their protectors are idols, who bring them out of the light into the darknesses."* (Qur'an 2:257)

> *"A Book We have sent down to thee, that thou mayest bring mankind out of the darknesses into the light, by the Leave of their Lord, to the path of the Mighty, the Praised."* (Qur'an 14:1)

> *"He it is Who sends blessings upon you, and His angels, that He may bring you out of the darknesses into the light. And He is Merciful to the believers."* (Qur'an 33:43)

> *"That He may warn him who is living, and that the word may be established against the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 36:70, in context)

The commentators — al-Zamakhsharī, al-Bayḍāwī, al-Rāzī among them — draw the inference that has become standard in Islamic thought: truth is one and error is many. There is a single light and there are countless darknesses, because light is a positive reality with a single source, while darkness is the absence of that reality and can occur in countless ways.

This is a metaphysical claim stated as grammar. To be in error is not to hold the opposite of the truth; it is to be anywhere other than the truth, and "anywhere other" is an infinite set. Al-Rāzī develops the point: for every true proposition there are indefinitely many false ones, and the person who departs from the truth does not thereby arrive at another truth — he arrives at one of the darknesses.

**Darkness as a Condition, Not an Event**

The Qur'an describes darkness in terms that make it a place one inhabits rather than a thing one encounters. The most complete description is in Sūrah al-Nūr, where three kinds of darkness are stacked:

> *"Or [their deeds are] like darknesses in a deep sea, covered by waves, above which are waves, above which are clouds — darknesses, one above another. When he puts forth his hand, he can hardly see it. And he for whom God has appointed no light — for him there is no light."* (Qur'an 24:40)

Three observations about this image. First, the darkness is layered: *ẓulumātun baʿḍuhā fawqa baʿḍ*, darknesses one upon another — the depth, the waves, and the clouds each contributing. Second, it is disorienting at the level of the body: he puts out his hand and cannot see it, which means he has lost the reference point closest to him. Third, it is total: *"for him there is no light."*

The plural in that verse is not a stylistic variation. The person in error is not in one darkness but in several, and each of them is generated by a different layer of his own condition — his ignorance, his desire, his habit, his company. The layers are what make escape difficult, because removing one still leaves the others.

By contrast, the light is described in the same sūrah as singular and ungenerated:

> *"God is the Light of the heavens and the earth. The likeness of His light is as a niche in which is a lamp..."* (Qur'an 24:35)

One niche, one lamp, one glass, one oil, one blessed tree — *"light upon light."* The singularity of the image answers the plurality of the darkness.

**From Whom the Light Comes**

The asymmetry has a further consequence that the Qur'an states directly: the darknesses are entered by the action of false protectors, and the light is entered only by God's action. Look again at 2:257. God *brings out* of the darknesses into the light; the idols *bring out* of the light into the darknesses. Both movements are described with the same verb, *yukhriju*, but the destinations differ in number.

This is why the transition from belief to unbelief is described in the Qur'an as a descent into multiplicity — confusion, contradiction, division — and the transition from unbelief to belief as an arrival at unity. Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ states the logical form:

> *"Had there been therein gods besides God, both would have been ruined."* (Qur'an 21:22)

> *"God has not taken any son, nor is there any god along with Him; otherwise each god would have taken away what he created, and some of them would have risen over others."* (Qur'an 23:91)

Multiplicity at the level of the object of worship produces multiplicity at the level of the self. The person with many gods is a person pulled in many directions, and the Qur'an names that condition as one of the darknesses.

**The Experiential Difference**

The pairing in Fāṭir's verse is not only doctrinal; it is phenomenological, and every reader can verify it.

There is a state known to anyone who has resolved a long confusion: the moment when something becomes clear. The clarity is not the addition of information — often the information was already present. It is the arrival of organisation. Things that were scattered arrange themselves; a decision that was impossible becomes obvious; the anxiety that accompanied the confusion dissipates not because the difficulty has gone but because the shape of the difficulty has appeared. That is *nūr*.

There is a corresponding state known to anyone who has lived long in an unresolved contradiction: the sense of not being able to see one's own hand. Options multiply without becoming selectable. Advice arrives from every direction and increases the confusion. The person is not lacking data; he is lacking light. That is *ẓulumāt*, and it is plural because each new piece of advice adds a darkness rather than removing one.

Sūrah al-Anʿām describes the second state with a rhetorical question that the Qur'an treats as unanswerable:

> *"Is he who was dead, and to whom We gave life, and made for him a light by which he walks among mankind — like him whose likeness is in the darknesses, from which he does not emerge?"* (Qur'an 6:122)

Note the phrase *"by which he walks among mankind."* The light is not for private contemplation; it is for navigation in company. The person in the darknesses is not merely unhappy — he cannot walk among people without collision.

**Why the Verse Places Darkness Second**

Verse 19 contrasted two kinds of person: the blind and the seeing. Verse 20 contrasts two kinds of condition: the darknesses and the light. The shift is important, because a person can have sight and still be in darkness. The eye functions; the illumination does not. Sighted people in a dark room see nothing.

This is a precise diagnosis of a common religious state: the person who has the capacity for insight — intelligence, education, exposure to the truth — but lacks the illumination in which to use it. He is not the blind man of verse 19; he is the seeing man of verse 20 standing in the dark. The Qur'an's remedy for him is not the restoration of sight but the arrival of light, which is why the prayer for light is one of the most repeated supplications in the Prophetic tradition:

> *"O God, place light in my heart, and light in my hearing, and light in my sight, and light on my right, and light on my left, and light above me, and light below me, and light before me, and light behind me — and make light for me."* (al-Bukhārī 6316; Muslim 763, in the various wordings of the night supplication)

Every direction named. The supplication is structured as though the supplicant were standing in exactly the darkness verse 20 describes, and asking for the singular light to arrive in all of it.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:21

> **nor the shade and the scorching heat.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Pair Addressed to the Skin Rather Than the Eye**

The third of the four pairs changes register. The blind and the seeing are about perception; the darkness and the light are about illumination. The shade and the scorching heat are about the body — about what it feels when it stands in the sun at noon in Arabia, and what it feels when it moves under a tree.

*Ẓill* is shade, shadow, the covered place. *Ḥarūr* is the scorching heat, and the lexicographers specify it further: it is the hot, burning wind, and it is distinguished from *samūm* in that *samūm* blows by day and *ḥarūr* by night, though some hold that both apply to either. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record the distinction, and it matters for the verse's force: the *ḥarūr* is not merely the sun's heat, from which one can shelter when the sun sets, but a burning that continues into the night. There is no hour at which it relents.

**Why Shade Is a Serious Category in the Qur'an**

To a reader in a temperate climate, "shade" is a mild comfort. To the Qur'an's first audience it was survival. In the Arabian desert the difference between standing in the sun and standing in the shade is not a matter of preference but of endurance — a matter of hours. The Qur'an uses the word with that weight, and it uses it for Paradise consistently:

> *"But those who believe and perform righteous deeds — We shall bring them into Gardens beneath which rivers flow, abiding therein forever; therein they shall have purified spouses, and We shall bring them into shade spreading wide (*ẓillin ẓalīl*)."* (Qur'an 4:57)

> *"And its shade is near over them, and its fruit is made easy to be plucked with an easy plucking."* (Qur'an 76:14)

> *"And the companions of the right — what of the companions of the right! In thornless lote trees and layered acacias, and shade outspread (*ẓillin mamdūd*)."* (Qur'an 56:27–30)

> *"The likeness of the Garden promised to the reverent: therein are rivers of water unfailing, and rivers of milk whose taste changes not, and rivers of wine delicious to the drinkers, and rivers of honey purified; and therein they have all fruits and forgiveness from their Lord."* (Qur'an 47:15, in context of the contrast that follows)

And, for the Fire:

> *"And the companions of the left — what of the companions of the left! In scorching wind and boiling water, and in the shade of black smoke (*ẓillin min yaḥmūm*), neither cool nor noble."* (Qur'an 56:41–44)

That last phrase is one of the most devastating in the Qur'an. The Fire has a *shade* — and the shade is smoke, and it is neither cool nor noble. The thing that in this world is a relief is, there, an intensification. Ibn Kathīr quotes precisely this contrast when he comments on verses 19 to 22 of Fāṭir, as the source records: the believer settles in Gardens wherein are shade and springs, while the disbeliever ends amidst scorching wind and boiling liquid and the shadow of black smoke, neither cool nor refreshing.

**The Word Ḥarūr and the Absence of Relief**

The choice of *ḥarūr* over *samūm* or *ḥarr* is deliberate. *Ḥarr* is heat generally; *samūm* is the piercing hot wind of the day; *ḥarūr* is the burning that persists. The commentators note that the Qur'an's descriptions of the Fire consistently emphasise that its conditions are unrelieved by time:

> *"Truly Hell is a place of ambush, for the transgressors a destination, abiding therein for ages. They shall taste therein no coolness and no drink."* (Qur'an 78:21–24)

*Lā yadhūqūna fīhā bardan wa-lā sharābā* — no coolness and no drink. The two things a person in scorching heat wants — cool air and water — are both named and both denied.

The psychological force of this is in the word *bard*, coolness. It is not merely temperature; it is the relief that comes over the body when heat passes. The verse denies not only the drink but the *experience of relief*, which is worse than the pain, because pain that alternates with relief can be endured and pain that does not cannot.

**Ibn Kathīr on the Four Pairs as One Description**

Ibn Kathīr's summary of verses 19 to 22, preserved in the source, is worth quoting because it shows how the tradition read the sequence as a single portrait:

> *"The believer sees and hears, and walks in the light upon a straight path in this world and the Hereafter, so that he comes to settle in Gardens wherein are shade and springs. The disbeliever is blind and deaf, walking in darkness from which he cannot escape; he is lost in his misguidance in this world and the Hereafter, so that he ends up amidst scorching wind and boiling liquid, and the shadow of black smoke, neither cool nor refreshing."* (Ibn Kathīr on 35:19–22)

Note the double application — "in this world and the Hereafter." The shade and the scorching heat are not only eschatological destinations; they are present conditions. The believer lives in shade now: covered, protected, at a temperature he can bear. The disbeliever lives in *ḥarūr* now: exposed, uncovered, burning without the possibility of night.

**Shade as Protection: The Human Dimension**

The Arabic word *ẓill* carries an extended meaning that the Qur'an uses: protection, patronage, the covering of a stronger person over a weaker one. To be "in someone's shade" is to be under their care. The Qur'an applies the image to God's own protection on the Day of Judgment in a report the tradition holds in the highest regard:

> *"Seven will God shade in His shade on the Day when there is no shade but His shade: a just ruler; a youth who grew up in the worship of God; a man whose heart is attached to the mosques; two men who loved one another for the sake of God and met and parted upon it; a man whom a woman of rank and beauty called to herself and he said, 'I fear God'; a man who gave charity so secretly that his left hand did not know what his right hand spent; and a man who remembered God in solitude and his eyes overflowed."* (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031)

The report explains the verse's imagery from inside the tradition. On a Day described elsewhere as one in which the sun is brought near and people sweat according to their deeds, the only shade available is God's, and it is allocated by conduct. The seven categories named are not seven virtues among many; they are seven forms of shade-seeking behaviour, and each of them involves a refusal of something immediately attractive — power used unjustly, desire indulged, recognition pursued, wealth displayed, emotion suppressed.

The shade of this world is also, in the verse's register, a gift. The shade of a tree, of a roof, of a parent's care, of a just government, of a friend's discretion: all of these are *ẓill*, and all of them are the thing the scorching heat lacks.

**What the Scorching Heat Is in Ordinary Life**

The pair has an application that requires no allegory. There are lives lived in the shade and lives lived in exposure, and the difference is not always visible from outside.

The person in shade has a margin: time that is not fully claimed, resources that are not fully committed, relationships that can absorb a mistake. When something goes wrong, there is somewhere to move to. The person in *ḥarūr* has no margin: every hour is spoken for, every unit of income is allocated, every relationship is at its limit. When something goes wrong, there is nowhere to move, and the heat simply increases.

The Qur'an's treatment of this difference is not sentimental. It does not say the person in exposure is worse or the person in shade is better. It says the two are not equal — and the sūrah elsewhere makes clear what the believer owes in consequence. Verse 29 will describe those who *spend from that which We have provided them, secretly and openly*, and the spending is precisely the transfer of shade from those who have it to those who do not. The person who has margin and does not use it to make margin for others is, in the sūrah's logic, wasting the shade.

**The Fourth Pair Approaches**

Verse 21 is the third step in a sequence that moves steadily inward: from the eye (19), to the surrounding light (20), to the skin (21), and finally to life itself (22). Each step is a deeper layer of the human being. And the sequence's direction is toward the last and largest inequality — the one that cannot be compensated by any adjustment of perception, illumination, or comfort: *Not equal are the living and the dead.*

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:22

> **Not equal are the living and the dead. Truly God causes whomsoever He will to hear, but thou canst not cause those in graves to hear.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Largest Inequality**

The fourth pair is the one that contains the other three, because the difference between the living and the dead is absolute in a way that the difference between the blind and the seeing is not. A blind man can be led; a dead man cannot. Darkness can be relieved by a lamp; death cannot be relieved by anything in the world of the living.

*Wa-mā yastawī al-aḥyāʾ wa-lā al-amwāt* — and here both terms are plural: "the living ones" and "the dead ones." The shift from the singular pairs of the previous three verses — the blind and the seeing, the shade and the heat — to two plurals suggests communities rather than individuals. The living are a people; the dead are a people.

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as the answer to the rhetorical questions posed at 6:122 and 39:22: not equal are the living, whose hearts are alive with faith in God, and the dead, whose hearts are dead because they are covered with disbelief. The metaphor is standard in the Qur'an and is used without apology:

> *"Is he who was dead, and to whom We gave life, and made for him a light by which to walk among mankind, like him whose likeness is in the darknesses, from which he does not emerge?"* (Qur'an 6:122)

> *"That he may warn him who is living, and that the Word may be established against the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 36:70)

Note what 36:70 does: it states the *purpose* of the warning — to reach the living. The warning is not addressed to everyone in the same way, because not everyone is in the same condition. This is the same limitation verse 18 of Fāṭir stated: *Thou warnest only those who fear their Lord unseen.*

**"God Causes Whomsoever He Will to Hear"**

*Inna Allāha yusmiʿu man yashāʾ* — "Truly God makes to hear whomsoever He wills." The verb *yusmiʿu* is the causative of *samiʿa*, to hear: to cause to hear, to make someone hear. Ibn ʿAjībah explains that God's causing them to hear refers to His guiding them and supporting them; al-Ṭabarī relates it to the first part of the verse — those whose hearts are alive are those who hear the truth of revelation, while those whose hearts are buried in the grave of ignorance cannot hear it.

The distinction the verse is drawing is between two kinds of hearing, and the Qur'an has a precise vocabulary for it. *Samiʿa* is to hear the sound; *aṣghā* is to incline the ear; *waʿā* is to take in and retain; *faqihā* is to understand. A person can have the first without the last. Sūrah al-Aʿrāf describes the condition:

> *"And We have certainly created for Hell many of the jinn and mankind: they have hearts with which they do not understand, eyes with which they do not see, and ears with which they do not hear. Those are like cattle — nay, they are more astray. Those are the heedless."* (Qur'an 7:179)

The organs are present and functioning; the faculty is not engaged. That is the death the verse describes.

**"Thou Canst Not Cause Those in Graves to Hear"**

The second half of the verse is a negation with an unusual grammatical form: *wa-mā anta bi-musmiʿin man fī al-qubūr*. The construction *mā anta bi-* — "thou art not one who..." — is intensive. It does not merely deny the action; it denies the capacity and the identity. You are not, and cannot become, a hearer-maker of those in graves.

*Man fī al-qubūr* — "those in the graves" — is the sūrah's most concrete image of spiritual death. The heart that does not hear is in a grave, and the grave is a place from which no call reaches.

**The Well of Badr: A Literal Reading Held Alongside the Metaphor**

This verse cannot be discussed without the incident that the classical commentators consistently raise in connection with it and with its parallel at 27:80 and 30:52. After the Battle of Badr, the Prophet ﷺ stood at the edge of the pit into which twenty-four of the Quraysh leaders had been thrown, and called them by name:

> *"O so-and-so son of so-and-so! Did you find true what your Lord promised you? For I have found true what my Lord promised me."* ʿUmar said: "O Messenger of God, how do you speak to bodies that have no souls in them?" He said: *"By Him in Whose Hand is my soul, you do not hear what I say better than they do — but they cannot respond."* (al-Bukhārī 1370; 3976; Muslim 2873)

ʿĀʾishah, upon whom be peace, objected to a version of this report and cited the Qur'an against it, quoting *"Thou canst not make those in graves to hear"* (35:22) and *"Thou makest none to hear save those who believe in Our signs"* (30:53). She held that the Prophet ﷺ said rather that they knew the truth of what he said, not that they heard his voice.

The scholars reconciled the two positions by distinguishing the kinds of hearing, and the reconciliation is precise and worth stating. The hearing denied by this verse is the hearing that produces response — the hearing of guidance, which requires a living heart and a willing soul. The hearing affirmed in the ḥadīth of Badr is the perception granted to the dead by God for the purpose of that moment, which produces no response and no benefit. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both set out this distinction, and it is the standard position: the dead hear in a manner God creates, and they do not hear in the manner that changes anything.

The distinction is not merely technical. It preserves both truths that matter: that the human being's capacity to receive guidance belongs to God alone, and that the dead are not annihilated but exist in a state of awareness whose quality is fixed.

**Why the Verse Addresses the Prophet**

The verse turns in its second half from a general statement to a specific address — *wa-mā anta bi-musmiʿin*, "and thou canst not make hear" — and the address is consolatory. It follows verse 8, in which God told the Prophet not to let his soul be expended in regrets over those whose evil deeds have been beautified to them, and it prepares verse 23, which will say simply: *Thou art naught but a warner.*

The sequence is a pastoral programme. First: some people cannot be reached, and their unreachability is not your failure (v. 8). Second: hearing is God's gift, not your production (v. 22). Third: your function is delivery, not result (v. 23).

Every person who has tried to change someone — a parent with a child, a spouse with a spouse, a friend with a friend, a teacher with a student, a preacher with a congregation — will recognise the necessity of these three statements in order. The most common form of exhaustion in religious and moral work comes from assuming responsibility for an outcome that the verse explicitly removes: *thou canst not cause those in graves to hear.*

**The Living and the Dead in the Same Room**

The verse's application to ordinary life is immediate and unsettling. It is possible to be in the presence of the living and the dead simultaneously — to speak to two people, both of whom have functioning ears, and be heard by one. The Qur'an describes this repeatedly and treats it as a normal condition of prophetic work rather than an exceptional failure:

> *"Thou makest none to hear, save those who believe in Our signs and are submitters."* (Qur'an 30:53)

The qualification *"and are submitters"* — *muslimūn* — is significant. The two conditions for hearing are faith and submission, and neither is intellectual. A person may be perfectly capable of understanding an argument and unable to hear it, because hearing in the Qur'an's sense includes willingness.

This explains something that puzzles people who work with ideas: why clear arguments so often fail to persuade. The failure is not in the clarity. It is in the state of the hearer, and the state of the hearer is not under the control of the speaker. The person who understands this continues to speak — because the speaking is his duty — and stops measuring his success by the response, because the response is not his to produce.

**The Sequence Complete**

With verse 22, the four pairs are finished. The blind and the seeing; the darkness and the light; the shade and the scorching heat; the living and the dead. Four inequalities, moving from perception to illumination to bodily experience to life itself, and each one a restatement of the sūrah's central claim: the two communities are not on the same level, and the difference between them is not a matter of degree that could be closed by effort, argument, or time.

The verse that follows draws the consequence for the messenger. If the dead cannot hear, then what is he?

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:23

> **Thou art naught but a warner.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Whole Verse to Define a Role**

Four Arabic words: *in anta illā nadhīr* — "thou art not but a warner." The restrictive construction *in ... illā* is the strongest form of limitation in the language, and the verse uses it on the Prophet himself. Everything that the messenger is not has been removed from him in three words.

The brevity is not economy but emphasis. Verse 22 established that the dead cannot be made to hear; verse 23 states what remains of the messenger's function once that fact is accepted. Nothing remains but warning. Not converting, not compelling, not producing results, not being responsible for the outcome.

**Nadhīr: The One Who Warns of What Is Ahead**

*Nadhīr* comes from the root *n-dh-r*, whose core sense is to warn someone of a danger that lies ahead, in time for him to act. The same root gives *nadhr*, a vow — a commitment made in view of a feared consequence — and *mundhir*, one who gives the warning.

The word is distinguished from two others in Qur'anic usage. *Mukhawwif* would be one who frightens; *nadhir* is not that, because the warning is given for the benefit of the warned, not to terrify him. And *bashīr*, the bringer of glad tidings, is the paired term: the messenger both warns of what follows from rejection and announces what follows from acceptance. The Qur'an names the Prophet ﷺ by both together in many places (5:19; 7:188; 11:2; 17:105; 25:56; 33:45; 34:28; 48:8), and here — where the subject is the failure of the audience — by the second alone.

The warning has a specific structure: it is information about a future harm, delivered to someone who can still avoid it. Once the harm has arrived, warning becomes useless and is replaced by requital. That is why the warning belongs to this life and only to this life.

**The Same Sentence, Repeated to Different Messengers**

The formulation is not unique to Muhammad ﷺ. It appears in identical or near-identical terms throughout the Qur'an:

> *"Thou art naught but a warner, and for every people there is a guide."* (Qur'an 13:7)

> *"And this Qur'an has been revealed to me that I may warn you therewith and whomever it reaches."* (Qur'an 6:19)

> *"And thou art naught but a warner."* (Qur'an 11:12; 27:92; 34:46; 38:65; 79:45)

And in the passage that most fully develops the limitation, Sūrah al-Ghāshiyah:

> *"So remind — thou art naught but a reminder. Thou art not a controller over them. But whosoever turns away and disbelieves, God shall punish him with the greatest punishment. Truly unto Us is their return, then truly upon Us is their reckoning."* (Qur'an 88:21–26)

*Lasta ʿalayhim bi-muṣayṭir* — "thou art not a controller over them." *Muṣayṭir* is one who dominates and compels. The verse denies to the greatest of human beings an authority that people have constantly tried to claim in his name.

**"Upon the Messenger Is Only the Conveyance"**

The Qur'an states the same limitation in positive form several times, and each statement pairs the limitation with a transfer of responsibility:

> *"Obey God and obey the Messenger. And if you turn away, then upon Our Messenger is only the clear conveyance."* (Qur'an 16:82)

> *"And if they turn away — We have not sent thee as a guardian over them. Upon thee is only the conveyance."* (Qur'an 42:48)

> *"Upon the Messenger is only the conveyance. And God knows what you disclose and what you hide."* (Qur'an 5:99)

Three terms are denied to the messenger in these verses — *ḥafīẓ* (guardian), *muṣayṭir* (controller), *wakīl* (trustee over their affairs) — and one is affirmed: *balāgh*, conveyance. The messenger carries; he does not manage.

This is a structural feature of Islam's understanding of religious authority, and it has consequences that the tradition has not always honoured. The one who conveys the message has no jurisdiction over the recipient's soul. No scholar, no imam, no shaykh, no institution inherits authority the Prophet himself did not possess. The verse is a permanent limit on clerical power, stated by God to the highest of His messengers.

**Why the Limitation Is a Mercy to the Messenger**

The commentators — al-Ṭabarī explicitly — read verse 23 as an extension of verse 8: *So let not thy soul be expended in regrets over them.* The two verses serve the same purpose from different directions. Verse 8 addressed the grief; verse 23 addresses its cause. The cause of the grief is the assumption that the messenger is responsible for the response. The verse removes the assumption.

There is a recorded incident that illustrates the weight the Prophet ﷺ carried. When his uncle Abū Ṭālib lay dying, the Prophet ﷺ went to him and said:

> *"O uncle! Say 'There is no god but God' — a word with which I can defend you before God."* (Muslim 24; al-Bukhārī 1360, in the account of the death)

And Abū Ṭālib died without saying it, and the verse came down:

> *"Truly thou guidest not whom thou lovest; rather, God guides whomsoever He will. And He knows best those who are rightly guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

The most urgent case in the Prophet's life — his own uncle, who had protected him for forty years — was one in which his desire, his effort, and his love were all present and all insufficient. Verse 28:56 was the answer, and Fāṭir's verse 23 is the same answer in four words.

**The Function That Remains**

Stripped of the authority to compel, what does the warner actually do? Three things, and the verse implies all three.

He states the danger. Not his opinion about it, not his preference, not his interpretation — the danger as it has been revealed to him. This requires accuracy, and it is why the Islamic sciences of transmission developed with such rigour: a warner who misstates the danger has made the situation worse.

He states it clearly. The Qur'an insists on the clarity of the message repeatedly: *mubīn*, *bayyināt*, *kitāb mubīn*. A warning delivered in terms the warned cannot understand is not a warning.

He states it and then leaves. This is the hardest part, and it is the part the verse is about. The warner does not stay to supervise the response, adjust the message to improve reception, or repeat the warning in escalating tones. He delivers and moves on — because *"unto God are all matters returned."*

**The Application Beyond the Prophet**

Everyone who carries a message of any kind lives under this verse: the parent, the teacher, the physician giving unwelcome news, the friend who must say something difficult, the scholar whose work nobody reads. Each of them is a *nadhir* in the general sense, and each faces the same temptation — to convert the warning into control, to keep pushing after the delivery, to measure the day by the response.

The verse's discipline is available to all of them. Deliver the warning accurately, deliver it clearly, and then recognise that the hearing belongs to God and the outcome belongs to God. *"Thou art naught but a warner"* is, for the person carrying a difficult message, not a diminishment but a release.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:24

> **Truly We have sent thee with the truth as a bearer of glad tidings and as a warner. And there has been no community but that a warner has passed among them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sent "With the Truth": The Instrument of the Mission**

*Innā arsalnāka bi-l-ḥaqq* — "Truly We sent thee with the truth." The preposition *bi-* here has been read two ways, and both are preserved by the commentators. The first: sent *for* the truth, or *by means of* the truth — the truth is the content of the mission. The second: sent accompanied by the truth, the truth being the evidence, the miracles, and the message itself.

Either way, the verse locates the messenger's authority in something he did not produce. He was not sent with his own wisdom, his own eloquence, or his own status; he was sent with *al-ḥaqq*. The definite article makes it specific: not a truth among truths, but the Truth. Sūrah al-Baqarah states the same in the same words:

> *"Truly We sent thee with the truth, as a bearer of glad tidings and a warner; and thou shalt not be questioned about the companions of the Blaze."* (Qur'an 2:119)

The addition in 2:119 — *"thou shalt not be questioned about the companions of the Blaze"* — completes Fāṭir's verse 23. The messenger is not accountable for those who end in the Fire.

**The Two Offices: Bashīr and Nadhīr**

*Bashīran wa-nadhīran* — "a bearer of glad tidings and a warner." The two participles are the Qur'an's standard pair for the prophetic function, and their order is consistent: glad tidings first, warning second. This mirrors the order of verse 2 of this sūrah, where God's opening of mercy precedes His withholding, and the ḥadīth qudsī that *"My Mercy precedes My Wrath"* (al-Bukhārī 7404; Muslim 2751).

*Bashīr* is from the root *b-sh-r*, skin, surface, and hence to bring news that changes the face — news that shows on the skin, in the complexion. *Bushrā* is glad tidings. The word implies news that is felt physically, not merely registered.

*Nadhīr*, as established in the previous verse, is the one who warns of a coming danger in time for action.

The pairing is not a balancing act but a description of a single message with two consequences. There is one truth; accepting it produces glad tidings; rejecting it produces the warning. The messenger does not have two messages, one for the compliant and one for the resistant; he has one message with two outcomes, and he announces both.

The Qur'an attributes the same paired office to all the prophets, not only to Muhammad ﷺ:

> *"Mankind was one community; then God raised the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and as warners, and sent down with them the Book in truth, that He might judge between the people concerning what they differed about."* (Qur'an 2:213)

> *"Messengers, bearers of glad tidings and warners, so that mankind may have no argument against God after the messengers. And God is Mighty, Wise."* (Qur'an 4:165)

The purpose clause in 4:165 is the key to the whole doctrine: *li-llā yakūna li-l-nās ʿalā Allāh ḥujjah baʿda al-rusul* — so that people may have no plea against God after the messengers. Revelation exists to remove the human being's excuse. Before it, there is an excuse; after it, there is none.

**"There Has Been No Community but That a Warner Has Passed Among Them"**

*Wa-in min ummatin illā khalā fīhā nadhīr* — the construction *in ... illā* again, the same restrictive form as verse 23, but here applied to history: there is not a single community among whom a warner has not passed.

*Ummah* is a community bound by something — time, place, language, covenant. *Khalā* is from the root *kh-l-w*, to pass, to go by, to become empty; the same verb used of past generations (*al-qurūn al-khāliyah*). The warner has *passed among* them — moved through them, been present in them, and then gone.

This is one of the Qur'an's most consequential historical claims. It asserts that revelation is not the property of a particular lineage, region, or epoch, but has reached every human collectivity. Sūrah al-Naḥl states it in terms of a mission to each:

> *"And We raised among every community a messenger: 'Worship God and shun idols.' Then among them were those whom God guided, and among them were those upon whom error was established. So journey in the earth and see how the end of the deniers was."* (Qur'an 16:36)

> *"And for every community there is a messenger. And when their messenger comes, judgment is made between them with justice, and they are not wronged."* (Qur'an 10:47)

And Sūrah Ghāfir states the limit of human knowledge about the total:

> *"And We sent messengers before thee — among them are those We have related to thee, and among them are those We have not related to thee. And no messenger could bring a sign except by the Leave of God."* (Qur'an 40:78)

That verse is decisive for the doctrine: the Qur'an names twenty-five prophets and explicitly says others existed who were not named. The absence of a prophet from the Qur'an's list is not evidence of his absence from history.

**The Scale of the Claim**

The tradition attempted to quantify it. In the long ḥadīth of Abū Dharr recorded by Aḥmad and others, the Prophet ﷺ was asked about the number of the prophets, and the report gives 124,000 prophets, of whom 315 were messengers. The chain of this report has been graded weak by a number of ḥadīth scholars and accepted by others; it should not be cited as established. But its existence shows what the community understood verse 24 to mean: not that a handful of Semitic prophets carried a local message, but that warning had been delivered continuously, everywhere, to everyone.

The theological consequence is stated at 4:165 and again at 17:15:

> *"Whosoever is guided is guided only for his own soul, and whosoever goes astray goes astray only upon it. And no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another. And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

The last clause — *wa-mā kunnā muʿadhdhibīna ḥattā nabʿatha rasūlā* — is the foundation of Islamic teaching on the fate of those unreached by revelation. Punishment presupposes a warner. Where no warner passed, no punishment is due. Verse 24 of Fāṭir asserts that warners did pass among every community, which closes the possibility that an entire people was left without access.

The scholars then debated what happens to the individual within a reached community who nonetheless never encountered the message in an intelligible form — the person born in isolation, the person who received only a distorted account. Al-Ghazālī, in his treatment of the categories of people who never received a true presentation of Islam, held that such a person is excused; Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim took a similar position on those to whom the message reached only in a corrupted form. The debates are extensive; the principle underlying all of them is verse 24 together with 17:15.

**What the Verse Answers**

The immediate context is consolation and rebuttal. Consolation: the Prophet ﷺ is not the first to be denied, and he is not the only warner to have passed among a people who rejected him. Rebuttal: the Quraysh's claim that no warner had come to them — or that they would have been better guided than the Jews and Christians had one come — is answered by the assertion that warners have passed among *every* community, including theirs.

The following verse (25) develops this: those before them also denied, and their messengers came with clear proofs, scriptures, and the luminous Book. Verse 24 states the universal law; verse 25 gives the specific history.

**The Modern Question the Verse Addresses**

Every thoughtful reader eventually asks: what about the civilisations of which we have no prophetic record — the Americas before contact, the interior of Africa, the peoples of East Asia? Verse 24 answers in the only way the Qur'an ever answers such questions: not by naming what we cannot know, but by establishing the principle that governs it. There has been no community without a warner. What we do not know is which warners; what we do know is that the record is not ours to keep.

The answer is not a claim that all religions are equally valid — the Qur'an is emphatic that they are not (3:19; 3:85). It is a claim about the fairness of the accounting: no people was left without warning, and no person will be punished for a warning that never reached him. Between those two principles lies the whole of the question, and the Qur'an leaves the particulars to the One who knows them:

> *"Truly God is of His servants Aware, Seeing."* (Qur'an 35:31)

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:25

> **If they deny thee, those before them also denied: their messengers brought them clear proofs, scriptures, and the luminous Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Consolation, Now With the Evidence Itemised**

Verse 4 of this sūrah said: *If they deny thee, messengers before thee were denied, and unto God are all matters returned.* Verse 25 returns to the same statement and adds the detail that makes it an argument rather than a comfort. The earlier verse told the Prophet that he was not alone; this verse tells him why the denial happened and where the fault lay.

*Wa-in yukadhdhibūka fa-qad kadhdhaba alladhīna min qablihim* — "And if they deny thee, those before them also denied." Then the crucial addition: *jāʾat-hum rusuluhum bi-l-bayyināt wa-bi-l-zubur wa-bi-l-kitābi al-munīr* — "their messengers came to them with clear proofs, with scriptures, and with the luminous Book."

The verse's purpose is to establish that the denial was not caused by insufficient evidence. The messengers did not arrive empty-handed, obscure, or ambiguous. They came with three categories of support, each named separately, and the peoples denied anyway. The denial is thereby located in the denier rather than in the message.

The parallel in Sūrah Āl ʿImrān is nearly identical and is cited by the source:

> *"And if they deny thee — messengers before thee were denied, who came with clear proofs, scriptures, and the luminous Book."* (Qur'an 3:184)

**Bayyināt: The Clear Proofs**

*Bayyināt* is the plural of *bayyinah*, from the root *b-y-n*, to be clear, to be separated, to be evident. A *bayyinah* is something that makes a distinction visible — a proof that separates truth from falsehood by its own clarity.

The commentators give the term two referents, and the source records both. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī read it as the miracles that bear witness to the truth of the prophetic mission: Moses's staff and hand, Jesus's healing of the blind and the leprous, Ṣāliḥ's she-camel, the Qur'an itself. Al-Qurṭubī adds the revealed laws, which are also *bayyināt* in that they make the permissible and the forbidden clear.

Both readings are supported by the Qur'an's own usage:

> *"And We gave Moses nine clear proofs (*bayyināt*)."* (Qur'an 17:101)

> *"And We sent Moses with Our signs and a clear authority, to Pharaoh and his chiefs; but they followed the command of Pharaoh, and the command of Pharaoh was not right."* (Qur'an 11:96–97)

> *"And when Our clear signs are recited to them, thou knowest the denial in the faces of those who disbelieve."* (Qur'an 22:72)

The last of these is important for understanding verse 25: the *bayyināt* are recognisable, and their recognition is visible on the face. The denier does not fail to perceive the proof; he perceives it and refuses it. That is why the Qur'an consistently treats denial as a moral failure rather than an intellectual one.

**Zubur: The Scriptures**

*Zubur* is the plural of *zabūr*, from the root *z-b-r*, whose basic sense is to write firmly, to inscribe upon stone — a *zabūr* is a written scripture, and by extension a book of revealed wisdom. The Qur'an uses the singular as a proper name for the revelation given to David:

> *"And We gave David a Zabūr."* (Qur'an 4:163; cf. 17:55)

> *"And We have certainly written in the Zabūr, after the Reminder, that the earth shall be inherited by My righteous servants."* (Qur'an 21:105)

But the plural *zubur* is used more broadly, of revelations generally, as the source notes: at 3:184, 16:44, 23:53, 26:196, and 54:43. In Sūrah al-Naḥl it describes the function of the scriptures:

> *"With clear proofs and scriptures. And We sent down to thee the Reminder, that thou mayest make clear to mankind what has been sent down to them, and that haply they may reflect."* (Qur'an 16:44)

The word choice is precise. *Bayyināt* are demonstrations; *zubur* are records. A miracle is witnessed once by those present and then must be transmitted; a scripture persists, can be read, compared, checked, and returned to. The two together provide both immediate evidence and lasting evidence.

**Al-Kitāb al-Munīr: The Luminous Book**

The third term is *al-kitāb al-munīr* — the Book that gives light. *Munīr* is the active participle of *anāra*, to illuminate, from the root *n-w-r* that gave *nūr*, light, in verse 20 of this same sūrah. The pairing is deliberate: verse 20 spoke of the darknesses and the light as the conditions of the soul; verse 25 speaks of the luminous Book as the source of the light.

The Qur'an uses the same description of itself and of the Torah:

> *"O mankind! There has come to you a clear proof from your Lord, and We have sent down to you a luminous light."* (Qur'an 4:174)

> *"Indeed We sent down the Torah, in which is guidance and light..."* (Qur'an 5:44)

> *"And We made it a light by which We guide whomsoever We will of Our servants."* (Qur'an 42:52)

The illumination metaphor carries a specific claim: the Book does not merely contain information, it changes the conditions of perception. A lamp does not add facts about a room; it makes the room visible. The *kitāb munīr* is what makes the *bayyināt* and the *zubur* legible — without light, evidence cannot be seen.

This connects verse 25 back to verse 22: *God causes whomsoever He will to hear.* The luminous Book is the means by which hearing becomes possible, and the light is God's provision.

**Why Three Terms**

The verse could have said "their messengers came with proofs." The threefold enumeration does specific work, and al-Rāzī's method of reading Qur'anic repetition applies here: each term excludes a possible excuse.

*Bayyināt* excludes the excuse of insufficient demonstration: "we were not shown anything."

*Zubur* excludes the excuse of insufficient transmission: "the message did not reach us in a durable form."

*Kitāb munīr* excludes the excuse of insufficient intelligibility: "we could not understand what was shown."

After the three, nothing remains. The denier cannot claim ignorance, cannot claim obscurity, cannot claim ambiguity. This is why the verse follows verse 24's assertion that no community was left without a warner: verse 24 establishes the fact of the warning; verse 25 establishes its adequacy.

**The Sentence That Follows in the Source's Argument**

The verse ends without a conclusion, and the following verse supplies it: *"Then I seized those who disbelieved."* The sequence — evidence given, evidence rejected, seizure — is the standard Qur'anic pattern of history, and it appears in the same order in Sūrah al-Muʾmin:

> *"And indeed We sent down to thee clear signs, and none denies them but the transgressors... They disputed by means of falsehood in order to reject the truth thereby; so I seized them. How, then, was My Retribution!"* (Qur'an 40:5)

Note the diagnosis in that verse: the disputing was *by means of falsehood* — *bi-l-bāṭil* — and its purpose was *to reject the truth thereby*. The denier of verse 25 is not confused; he is arguing, and his argument is instrumental. The threefold evidence is met not by counter-evidence but by contrivance.

**For the Person Denied**

The verse's first application is to the Prophet ﷺ, and through him to everyone who carries a message that is refused. The pattern of verse 25 gives the messenger three things to know.

First, that the refusal has a history: *those before them also denied.* It is not a novel event requiring novel explanation.

Second, that the refusal is not evidence of the message's inadequacy: the earlier messengers came with proofs, scriptures, and a luminous book, and were denied. Adequacy and reception are independent variables.

Third, that the refusal has a sequel, and the sequel is not the messenger's to write. Verse 26 supplies it in five words.

The practical effect of these three is a particular kind of steadiness. The person who knows that clear evidence has been refused before, by people who had every capacity to receive it, stops treating refusal as a crisis and starts treating it as a datum. He continues to bring the *bayyināt*, the *zubur*, and the *kitāb munīr* — demonstrations, records, and clarity — because that is what he was sent with. And he leaves the seizure where verse 26 places it.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:26

> **Then I seized those who disbelieved. How, then, was the change I wrought!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shift to the First Person**

Verse 25 spoke of the messengers in the third person: *their messengers came to them.* Verse 26 turns and speaks in the first: *thumma akhadhtu alladhīna kafarū* — "Then I seized those who disbelieved."

The shift is the same technique noted at verse 9, where the sūrah moved from "God sends the winds" to "We drove them." *Iltifāt*, the turning of grammatical person, marks a point of direct engagement. Here it marks the moment at which God takes the matter into His own hand. The messengers delivered; the peoples denied; and then — *I* seized them.

The verb *akhadha* is from the root *ʾ-kh-dh*, to take, to seize, to grasp hold of. In Qur'anic usage applied to God it denotes a sudden, complete, and irresistible taking: *akhdha al-ẓālimīn*, the seizing of the wrongdoers. It is not the language of gradual pressure or of negotiation. The same verb is used of the torment that came upon the earlier peoples:

> *"So each of them We seized by his sin: some of them We sent upon a wind of stones, and some of them were taken by the Cry, and some of them We made the earth swallow, and some of them We drowned. And God would not wrong them, but they were wronging themselves."* (Qur'an 29:40)

Four methods of seizure, named in a single verse: the storm of stones (the people of Lot), the Cry (Thamūd), the swallowing of the earth (Qārūn), the drowning (Pharaoh and the people of Noah). The variety is the point — the seizure comes through whatever element the seized believed was their security.

**The Sequence: Evidence, Denial, Seizure**

The *thumma*, "then," marks a gap. The seizure did not follow the denial immediately. Between them lay the respite — the time in which the warning was delivered, the proofs displayed, the refusal made, and the patience of God extended. The Qur'an is careful about this interval and names it repeatedly:

> *"And how many a city I gave respite while it was wrongdoing, then I seized it. And unto Me is the journey's end."* (Qur'an 22:48)
