> *"And thy Lord is Forgiving, the Possessor of Mercy. Were He to take them to task for what they have earned, He would surely hasten the punishment for them; but they have an appointed term from which they shall find no escape."* (Qur'an 18:58)

The respite is real and it is long, and the Qur'an warns against mistaking it for approval:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve suppose that the respite We grant them is good for them. We only grant them respite that they may increase in sin, and theirs shall be a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

The Prophetic tradition states the mechanism with a precision that has made it one of the most quoted reports on divine justice:

> *"Truly God gives respite to the wrongdoer, until when He seizes him, He does not let him escape."* Then he recited: *"Such is the seizure of thy Lord when He seizes the cities while they are wrongdoing. Truly His seizure is painful, severe."* (al-Bukhārī 4686; Muslim 2583, from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī)

The report ends with the Qur'anic verse from Sūrah Hūd (11:102), which is the fullest statement of the same doctrine: the seizure is delayed, it is complete, and it is severe.

**"How, Then, Was the Change I Wrought!"**

*Fa-kayfa kāna nakīr* — literally, "and how was My *nakīr*?" The word is the difficulty of the verse, and the commentators' range of readings shows how much it carries.

*Nakīr* is from the root *n-k-r*, which has two connected fields of meaning. The first is denial and disavowal: *nakara* means to deny, to refuse to acknowledge, to find a thing unrecognisable; *munkar* is the disapproved act, that which the sound nature refuses. The second is change and alteration: *nakira* and *ankara* mean to change a thing's state, to make it other than it was.

Al-Qurṭubī states that both translations imply the same thing: "How severe was My Punishment!" The first reading gives "how was My disavowal of them" — the reversal in which God refuses them as they refused Him. The second gives "how was the change I wrought" — the transformation of their condition from security to ruin. The source notes that it could also be rendered "How was the nature of My reproach!"

The interrogative form is rhetorical and it is used identically in several places:

> *"And how many a city was stronger in might than thy city that drove thee out! We destroyed them, and there was no helper for them."* (Qur'an 47:13)

> *"How many a generation We destroyed before them! Then they called out — but the time for escape was not."* (Qur'an 38:3)

> *"And how many a city We destroyed while it was wrongdoing, so it is fallen upon its roofs..."* (Qur'an 22:45)

> *"So do they feel secure against the coming of the punishment of God? None feels secure from the punishment of God except the losing people."* (Qur'an 7:99)

The question *"how was My nakīr?"* is addressed to the reader, not to the destroyed. It asks him to go and look. Sūrah al-Ḥajj completes it with the instruction that Fāṭir will give at verse 44:

> *"Have they not travelled in the earth, so that they may have hearts with which to understand, or ears with which to hear? For it is not the eyes that are blind, but blind are the hearts within the breasts."* (Qur'an 22:46)

**The Change: What Nakīr Actually Does**

If *nakīr* is read as alteration, the verse describes something more specific than destruction. Destruction ends a thing; alteration reverses it. The peoples seized by God were not merely removed — their condition was inverted. The strong became nothing; the inhabited became deserted; the arrogant became a cautionary tale told by people who never met them.

The Qur'an's descriptions of the aftermath consistently emphasise inversion rather than annihilation:

> *"So it is fallen upon its roofs, and a deserted well and a lofty palace!"* (Qur'an 22:45)

The palace still stands and the well still holds water. What has changed is who is in them. The structures remain as evidence of the reversal, which is why the Qur'an instructs the reader to travel and look at them (30:9; 47:10; 27:69).

This is the meaning of *nakīr* as a warning to the Quraysh, who passed the ruins of Thamūd on their trade route to Syria twice a year and knew exactly what the verse was describing.

**The Warning Applied to the Present**

Al-Qurṭubī explains that the verse indicates that the punishment befalling the disbelievers of this generation will be as severe as that which befell earlier ones. The past tense — *akhadhtu*, "I seized" — describes completed events, and the perfect tense in Qur'anic warning functions as a guarantee: what God has done in the pattern, He will do in the instance.

The principle is stated as a law in verse 43 of this sūrah: *Thou shalt find no alteration in the wont of God, and thou shalt find no change in the wont of God.* The seizure of verse 26 is a specimen of the wont; the wont does not vary.

**The Reader's Position**

Verse 26 leaves the reader with an uncomfortable question that it does not answer: which of the two parties am I in? Verse 25 described the people who were given proofs, scriptures, and a luminous book. Verse 26 described what happened to those who denied them. Both verses are in the past tense, and both are addressed to a present reader who is currently receiving the same three things.

The verse's rhetorical question — *how was the change I wrought!* — is a demand that the reader look at the evidence of history rather than speculate about the future. He does not need to imagine what divine seizure is like. It has happened repeatedly, in datable places, to civilisations that were stronger than his, and the ruins are still standing. Sūrah al-Rūm makes the instruction explicit:

> *"Have they not travelled in the earth and seen how those before them fared in the end? They were greater than them in strength, and they cultivated the earth and built upon it more than these have built, and their messengers came to them with clear proofs. And God would not wrong them, but they were wronging themselves."* (Qur'an 30:9)

The final clause is the one that verse 26 leaves standing behind its question. *Wa-mā kāna Allāhu li-yaẓlimahum wa-lākin kānū anfusahum yaẓlimūn* — God did not wrong them; they wronged themselves. The seizure was severe, and it was deserved, and the deserving is what verse 27 turns to examine — through rain, and fruit, and the colours of mountains.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:27

> **Hast thou not considered that God sends down water from the sky, wherewith We bring forth fruits of diverse colors? And in the mountains are streaks of white and red, of diverse hues, and others pitch-black.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Hast Thou Not Considered?" — The Address That Includes the Reader**

*A-lam tara* — literally, "have you not seen?" The commentators explain that this opening is addressed formally to the Prophet ﷺ, but al-Rāzī notes that it can be read as a direct address to any reader or listener, and al-Qurṭubī explains that it refers to seeing with the vision of the heart (*baṣīrah*), which enables one to know the true nature of things.

The distinction matters. *Ruʾyah* in Arabic covers both optical sight and mental perception, and the phrase *a-lam tara* is used in the Qur'an precisely where what follows is not something one has literally witnessed but something one can verify by attention:

> *"Hast thou not considered how thy Lord dealt with ʿĀd?"* (Qur'an 105:1, in the sense of 89:6)

> *"Hast thou not considered those who went forth from their homes in thousands, fearing death?"* (Qur'an 2:243)

> *"Hast thou not considered how God strikes a parable — a good word, like a good tree?"* (Qur'an 14:24)

The verse is asking for an act, and the act is not research but observation. The material is already in front of you.

**The Shift From "God" to "We"**

*A-lam tara anna Allāha anzala min al-samāʾi māʾan* — "hast thou not considered that God sends down water from the sky." Then: *fa-akhrajnā bihi thamarātin* — "and *We* bring forth thereby fruits." The same *iltifāt* noted at verses 9 and 26: the third person gives way to the first at the moment of the generative act.

The pattern is consistent in this sūrah. The general governance of creation is described with the Name *Allāh*; the specific act of bringing something out of something is described with *We*. The effect on the listener is that the fruit in his hand is not the terminal product of a natural process but the output of a personal act. Somebody brought it forth.

**"Fruits of Diverse Colors"**

*Thamarātin mukhtalifan alwānuhā* — "fruits whose colours differ." The word *thamarah* is from the root *th-m-r*, to bear fruit, to produce a yield; it is used of the fruit of trees and, by extension, of any productive outcome.

The emphasis on colour is the verse's central claim, and it is an emphasis on variety within a single cause. The same water, falling on the same earth, produces fruit of every colour: the green of the unripe grape, the purple of the ripe one, the yellow of the citron, the red of the pomegranate, the black of the date at full ripeness, the white of the flesh inside. The Qur'an makes the same argument with even greater force in Sūrah al-Raʿd:

> *"And in the earth are neighbouring tracts, and gardens of vines, and crops, and palm trees in pairs and single, watered with one water — and We make some of them excel others in fruit. Truly in that are signs for a people who understand."* (Qur'an 13:4)

*Yusqā bi-māʾin wāḥidin wa-nufaḍḍilu baʿḍahā ʿalā baʿḍin fī al-ukul* — watered with one water, yet preferred in taste. The argument is identical to the one modern biology calls the argument from the genome: the input is uniform and the output is various, which means the variation is not in the water.

Sūrah al-Naḥl states the same through honey:

> *"There comes forth from their bellies a drink differing in colours, wherein is healing for mankind. Truly in that is a sign for a people who reflect."* (Qur'an 16:69)

**"Streaks of White and Red"**

*Wa-min al-jibāli judadun bīḍun wa-ḥumrun mukhtalifun alwānuhā* — "and in the mountains are streaks of white and red, of diverse hues."

*Judad* is the plural of *jiddah*, a stripe, a streak, a band of colour running along a surface — the mark on the neck of a horse, or the veins of colour in stone. Some read the word as *judud*, the plural of *jadīd*, meaning a path or a new thing; the dominant reading is the one that gives streaks, and the context confirms it, because what follows is a description of colour.

The verse names three colour categories in the mountains: white, red, and black. To anyone who has looked at a mountain range this is a description rather than a symbol. Limestone and quartz are white; iron oxide — hematite, sandstone — is red; basalt and certain volcanic and metamorphic rocks are black. The Arabian landscape contains all three in visible proximity: the white limestone of the Hijaz, the red sandstones of the northwest, the black basalt fields (*ḥarrāt*) that stretch for hundreds of kilometres east of Madinah and which the early Muslims knew by name.

That a text revealed in seventh-century Arabia specifies mineral colour banding in mountains as a sign of divine action is a detail worth noting. The verse is not making a geological claim in the modern technical sense, and it should not be read as a textbook. It is making an observational claim that any traveller could verify — that mountains are not uniformly coloured but streaked — and treating the streaks as evidence.

**"And Others Pitch-Black": Gharābīb Sūd**

*Wa-gharābību sūd* — the phrase is grammatically remarkable, and the commentators discuss it at length. *Gharābīb* is the plural of *ghurbūb* or *ghirbīb*, an intensive term for deep, intense black — the black of the raven (*ghurāb*), from which the word is derived. The Arabs say *aswad ghirbīb*, "black as a raven," for the most intense black.

The word order inverts the normal Arabic construction. The expected phrase would be *sūdun gharābību* — "black ones, intensely black" — with the noun first and the intensifier second. The Qur'an places the intensifier first: *gharābību sūd*. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the inversion and reads it as a suppressed construction: "and black, intensely black [stones]." The effect of the inversion is to lead with the intensity and let the plain word confirm it — a rhetorical move that puts the depth of the blackness before the reader's eye first.

**Why Colour, and Why Here**

The placement of these verses in the sūrah is deliberate. Verses 25 and 26 described the seizure of the deniers; verses 27 and 28 turn immediately to the natural world. The transition is the argument. Having shown what God does to those who deny Him, the sūrah shows what He has done for everyone — and the thing He has done is not merely provision but *variety*.

God could have made all fruit one colour and all mountains one colour. Nothing in the requirements of nutrition or geology demanded the variation. The verse's insistence on *mukhtalifan alwānuhā* — differing in their colours — twice in one verse, indicates that the difference itself is part of the message.

The Qur'an makes this explicit in Sūrah al-Rūm:

> *"And among His signs are the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the variation in your tongues and your colours. Truly in that are signs for those who know."* (Qur'an 30:22)

Variation is a sign — that is, it carries information. What information? That the Maker is not constrained by utility. A purely functional creator would produce uniformity; a creator who produces variety is giving something beyond function. The excess is the evidence of generosity.

**Ibn ʿAjībah: The Colours of Knowledge**

Ibn ʿAjībah reads the imagery inwardly, as the source records: the fruits of diverse colours correspond to different forms of knowledge, and the different streaks upon the mountains to the various paths by which knowledge is obtained.

The reading follows the verse's own logic. Just as one water produces fruits of many colours, one revelation produces knowledges of many kinds — legal, theological, linguistic, spiritual — and none of them is the water. Just as the mountains have streaks of different colours running through a single rock, a single science has paths of different character running through it: the path of transmission, the path of reasoning, the path of experience, the path of the heart.

The practical conclusion Ibn ʿAjībah draws is tolerance within the variety: the person who has tasted one fruit should not deny the existence of the others. This is the classical Islamic attitude to the schools of law and to the disciplines of knowledge — variety produced by a single source is not contradiction.

**The Instruction: Look at Colour**

The verse's most immediate application is the simplest. It asks the reader to notice colour. Not to study it, not to explain it — to notice it.

The capacity to be moved by colour is a capacity most adults have largely lost. Children notice it constantly: the specific red of a particular apple, the specific black of a particular stone. The loss is not a loss of eyesight but of attention, and it is a loss the Qur'an treats as a spiritual deficit. The people who do not see the signs are described not as blind but as heedless (*ghāfilūn*), and heedlessness is inattention.

A person who reads verse 27 and then looks at a piece of fruit, or at a hillside, or at the variation of stone in a building, has done what the verse asked. The act takes five seconds and requires no learning. That is characteristic of Qur'anic method: its evidence is available to everyone, and its difficulty is not intellectual but attentional.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:28

> **And of mankind, beasts, and cattle there are, likewise, those of diverse colors. [Yet] only those among His servants who know fear God. Truly God is Mighty, Forgiving.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Category of Colour**

Verse 27 described the colours of fruits and of mountains. Verse 28 extends the same observation to living creatures: *wa-min al-nās wa-l-dawābbi wa-l-anʿāmi mukhtalifun alwānuhu ka-dhālik* — "and of mankind, beasts, and cattle there are, likewise, those of diverse colours."

*Dawābb* is the plural of *dābbah*, from the root *d-b-b*, to creep, to move with small steps — the word used of every creature that moves upon the earth:

> *"And there is no creature moving on the earth, nor a bird flying on its wings, but they are communities like you."* (Qur'an 6:38)

*Anʿām* are the grazing livestock — camels, cattle, sheep, goats — the animals on which Arabian life depended for transport, milk, wool, meat, and wealth.

Ibn Kathīr connects this to 30:22, *"the variation in your tongues and your colours,"* and reads the verse as a reference to the differentiation of races and ethnicities. Al-Qurṭubī adds that it can also be understood as a reference to the manner in which the inner states of human beings differ. Both readings have been held in the tradition, and neither excludes the other: the outward variety and the inward variety are produced by the same hand.

**"Only Those Among His Servants Who Know Fear God"**

The second clause of the verse is one of the most quoted sentences in the Qur'an on knowledge: *innamā yakhshā Allāha min ʿibādihi al-ʿulamāʾ*.

The Arabic word order is unusual and it carries the meaning. The particle *innamā* restricts; the object of the verb (*Allāha*) comes first; the verb (*yakhshā*) follows; and the subject (*al-ʿulamāʾ*, the scholars) is postponed to the end. Read literally: "Only — fears — God — among His servants — the knowing ones." The postponement of the subject to the final position, combined with the restrictive particle, produces the exclusivity that the translations render as "only those who know."

The exegetes note that this construction implies both directions: the scholars are the ones who fear God, and the ones who truly fear God are the scholars. Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī gloss *al-ʿulamāʾ* as those who know that God is Powerful over all things — not those who have information, but those who know His power.

**Khashyah Is Not Khawf**

The verb is *yakhshā*, from the root *kh-sh-y*, and the distinction between *khashyah* and *khawf* is one the classical lexicographers and commentators drew carefully.

*Khawf* is fear of harm — the fear of a thing that can hurt you, which may be present in an animal and in a child, and which does not require any knowledge of the thing feared. *Khashyah* is fear mixed with reverence, awe, and estimation; it is the fear felt toward someone known to be great, and it presupposes knowledge of that greatness. The lexicographers summarise it: *khashyah* is fear from one whose power and majesty you know.

This is why the verse uses *yakhshā* rather than *yakhāf*, and why the restriction to the scholars is not arbitrary. Awe is a cognitive emotion. It cannot be felt toward a being one has not apprehended. The person who knows God's power, mercy, precision, and independence feels *khashyah*; the person who knows only that God exists feels, at most, *khawf*.

The Qur'an describes the physical effect of this knowledge elsewhere:

> *"God has sent down the best of speech, a Book consistent, repeating, whereat shiver the skins of those who fear (*yakhshawna*) their Lord; then their skins and their hearts soften to the remembrance of God."* (Qur'an 39:23)

> *"Those who convey the messages of God and fear (*yakhshawnahū*) Him, and fear none but God. And sufficient is God as a Reckoner."* (Qur'an 33:39)

**The Prophet's Own Claim**

The tradition contains a statement from the Prophet ﷺ that the commentators bring to this verse, and it establishes the connection between knowledge and awe from the highest possible case:

> *"By God, I am the most fearful of God among you, and the most knowledgeable of Him."* (al-Bukhārī 5063; Muslim 1401, from ʿĀʾishah)

The context is instructive. Three Companions came to the houses of the Prophet's wives asking about his private worship, and on being told, considered their own efforts insufficient. One said he would pray all night forever; one that he would fast every day forever; one that he would abstain from marriage forever. When the Prophet ﷺ heard of it, he said:

> *"What is the matter with people who say such and such? I pray and I sleep, I fast and I break my fast, and I marry women. Whoever turns away from my Sunnah is not of me."* (al-Bukhārī 5063; Muslim 1401)

And then, in some wordings, the statement about being the most God-fearing and the most knowledgeable. The sequence is significant: the men's excessive asceticism was a form of *khawf* without *khashyah* — fear without knowledge — and it produced an unsustainable and unapproved practice. The one who knew God most feared Him most, and his fear expressed itself in balance, not in extremity.

The source records the companion report cited by al-Zamakhsharī: *"The one among you who has the most knowledge of God is the one whose fear of Him is most intense."*

**The Superiority of Knowledge Over Worship**

Al-Rāzī reads the verse as indicating the superiority of knowledge over worship, and the source quotes the famous ḥadīth in support:

> *"Whosoever sets upon a path to seek knowledge, God will set him upon a path to the Garden. Verily the angels lower their wings, being content with the one who seeks knowledge. And whosoever is in the heavens and on earth, even the fish in the sea, ask forgiveness for the person of knowledge. The superiority of one who has knowledge over the worshipper is as that of the moon over all other heavenly bodies. Those who have knowledge are the heirs of the prophets, for the prophets did not leave behind dīnārs and dirhams; they only left behind knowledge; and whosoever receives it, receives an abundance of good fortune."* (Abū Dāwūd 3641; al-Tirmidhī 2682, who graded it ḥasan; Ibn Mājah 223 — from Abū al-Dardāʾ)

Muslim records the first clause of it in the ṣaḥīḥ collection:

> *"Whoever travels a path seeking knowledge, God will make easy for him a path to Paradise."* (Muslim 2699)

Al-Bayḍāwī adds the qualification that the source preserves, and it is essential: the manner in which this verse is followed by the mention of the obligatory acts of worship (v. 29) demonstrates that such knowledge must be combined with worship and manifest in action in order to be of benefit. Knowledge that does not produce prayer and spending is not the knowledge verse 28 describes.

**The Test the Verse Provides**

Read as a criterion, the verse gives every person a way to evaluate his own knowledge. Do not ask how much you know; ask whether what you know has produced *khashyah*. A man may have memorised the entire Qur'an, mastered the sciences of jurisprudence, and read the whole of the classical commentary tradition — and if his knowledge has made him bolder rather than more careful, prouder rather than humbler, quicker to speak rather than quicker to hesitate, then by the criterion of this verse he has not yet attained the knowledge that counts.

The tradition is blunt about the possibility of knowledge without awe. The Prophet ﷺ taught a supplication that names it:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in Thee from knowledge that does not benefit, and from a heart that does not fear, and from a soul that is not satisfied, and from a supplication that is not answered."* (Muslim 2722, from Zayd ibn Arqam)

*ʿIlmin lā yanfaʿ* — knowledge that does not benefit — is placed first among the four things the believer seeks refuge from. And *qalbin lā yakhshaʿ*, a heart that does not fear, is the second. The two belong together, and Fāṭir's verse is the doctrinal statement of their connection.

**"Truly God Is Mighty, Forgiving"**

*Inna Allāha ʿazīzun ghafūr* — the two Names close the verse, and their pairing answers the emotion the verse produces.

*Al-ʿAzīz*, the Mighty, the Invincible, is the object of *khashyah*. Awe requires that the one feared be genuinely beyond one's power. If God could be managed, negotiated with, or evaded, awe would be impossible.

*Al-Ghafūr*, the Forgiving, prevents that awe from collapsing into despair. The person who knows God's might also knows his own deficiency, and the knowledge of both together would be unbearable if the verse ended with *ʿazīz*. It ends with *ghafūr*.

The same pairing occurs at verse 2 of this sūrah (*al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥakīm*), verse 7 (*al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥakīm*), verse 38 (*ʿAlīm*), and verse 41 (*al-Ḥalīm al-Ghafūr*). Fāṭir consistently ends its statements about God's power with a Name of mercy, and its statements about human deficiency with a Name of forgiveness. The verse about the scholars who fear God ends with the Forgiving — because the scholars are, first of all, the ones who know that they need it.

**The Sequence of the Three Verses**

Verses 27, 28, and 29 form a unit, and verse 29 begins with *inna*, confirming it. The sequence is: observe the variety of creation (27), know that observation produces awe in those who understand (28), and let awe issue in recitation, prayer, and spending (29). Perception, knowledge, action — three stages, in order, with each one requiring the previous.

The verse that most people quote about knowledge in the Qur'an is therefore not a statement about knowledge at all. It is a statement about what knowledge does.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:29

> **Truly those who recite the Book of God, perform the prayer, and spend from that which We have provided them secretly and openly hope for a commerce that will never perish,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Practices, Three Faculties, One Hope**

The verse names three acts and, in naming them, maps the whole of Islamic practice onto the three things a human being has to give: his tongue, his body, and his wealth. Al-Rāzī draws exactly this reading, and adds that the mention of fearing God in the preceding verse alludes to the fourth — the heart. Recitation is worship with the tongue; prayer is worship with the body; spending is worship with wealth; awe is worship with the heart.

The order is not random. Recitation comes first because it is the source of the other two: a person who does not recite does not know what to pray or why to spend. The Prophetic tradition treats recitation as the practice from which the rest flows:

> *"The best of you are those who learn the Qur'an and teach it."* (al-Bukhārī 5027, from ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān)

**"Those Who Recite the Book of God"**

*Yatlūna kitāba Allāh* — the verb *yatlū* is from the root *t-l-w*, to follow, to recite in sequence. The word combines two meanings that English separates: recitation and following. To *recite* the Book is to go through it in order; to *follow* it is to go where it goes. The Qur'an uses the same verb in both senses:

> *"Recite what has been revealed to thee of the Book, and perform the prayer. Truly the prayer forbids indecency and evil. And the remembrance of God is greater."* (Qur'an 29:45)

> *"Those to whom We gave the Book, they recite it as it should be recited (*yatlūnahu ḥaqqa tilāwatihi*) — those are the ones who believe in it."* (Qur'an 2:121)

The phrase in 2:121 is the tradition's standard gloss on this verse: reciting as it should be recited means not merely pronouncing the words but acting on them. Ibn ʿAjībah, as the source records, sees in *those who recite the Book of God* a reference to those who recite the Qur'an constantly — the people of the *wird*, the daily portion.

Muslim records the promise attached to the practice:

> *"Recite the Qur'an, for it will come on the Day of Resurrection as an intercessor for its companions."* (Muslim 804, from Abū Umāmah)

And al-Tirmidhī records the arithmetic:

> *"Whoever recites a letter from the Book of God has one good deed, and a good deed is tenfold. I do not say *Alif Lām Mīm* is a letter; rather *Alif* is a letter, *Lām* is a letter, and *Mīm* is a letter."* (al-Tirmidhī 2910, from Ibn Masʿūd; he graded it ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

**"And Perform the Prayer"**

*Wa-aqāmū al-ṣalāh* — "and they established the prayer." The verb is not *ṣallaw* (they prayed) but *aqāmū* (they established), from the root *q-w-m*, to stand, to make upright, to set something firmly in place. The Qur'an's consistent choice of *iqāmah* over bare performance is doctrinal: prayer is not an act but an institution one erects and maintains.

The commentators note that the three practices named here include both their obligatory and supererogatory forms, as al-Qurṭubī records. The verse does not specify which, and the generality is intentional — the *tilāwah* covers both the obligatory recitation in prayer and the voluntary reading; the *ṣalāh* covers the five daily prayers and the *nawāfil*; the *infāq* covers zakāh and voluntary charity.

**"And Spend From What We Have Provided Them"**

*Wa-anfaqū mimmā razaqnāhum* — "and they spend from what We have provided them." Two details in this phrase carry weight.

The first is *min* — "from." The spending is from the provision, not of it entirely. God does not ask for the whole; He asks for a portion. The zakāh obligation is 2.5% of qualifying wealth, and the Qur'an's general language of spending is *min*, partitive.

The second is *razāqnāhum* — "We have provided them." The verb *razaqa* is used exclusively of God in the Qur'an. The wealth is described as provision from God even while it is in the person's possession, which means the spending is not a gift from the person to the poor but a transfer within a provision that was never absolutely his. This is the same principle as verse 2: *whatsoever mercy God opens unto mankind, none shall withhold.*

**"Secretly and Openly"**

*Sirran wa-ʿalāniyatan* — "in secret and in open." The Qur'an pairs these two modes of giving repeatedly (2:274; 13:22; 14:31; 16:75), and the tradition explains the purpose of each, as the source records: give openly so that others are encouraged to do the same, and give privately so as not to be boastful.

The two modes have different risks and different rewards. Open giving risks ostentation (*riyāʾ*) but produces a social effect: it normalises generosity and gives others a model to imitate. Secret giving removes the risk but forgoes the model. The verse requires both, which means it requires the giver to manage the risk in each case — to give openly when the encouragement is worth the danger to his own sincerity, and secretly when the danger is too great.

The highest form of the secret is described in the ḥadīth of the seven:

> *"...and a man who gave charity and concealed it, such that his left hand did not know what his right hand spent."* (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031)

The image is not literal but describes a giving so unremarked that the giver himself does not tally it. This is the opposite of the modern practice of documenting one's generosity, and the verse's requirement of *sirran* is a direct challenge to it.

The Qur'an warns about the nullification of open giving:

> *"O you who believe! Do not nullify your charities with reminder and injury, like one who spends his wealth to be seen by mankind."* (Qur'an 2:264)

**"They Hope for a Commerce That Will Never Perish"**

*Yarjūna tijāratan lan tabūr* — the verse's central metaphor and its most striking word choice.

*Tijārah* is commerce, trade, the business of buying and selling for a return. The Qur'an uses it for the religious life deliberately:

> *"O you who believe! Shall I guide you to a commerce that will save you from a painful punishment — that you believe in God and His Messenger, and strive in the way of God with your wealth and your selves? That is better for you, if you only knew."* (Qur'an 61:10–11)

The metaphor is chosen because it captures five features of the religious life that no other image captures as well. It involves capital, which must be committed and cannot be committed elsewhere. It involves risk, because the return is not visible at the time of the investment. It involves a counterparty, and the transaction depends on his acceptance. It involves a return that exceeds the capital if the trade is good. And it involves a moment of settlement at which the account is closed.

*Tabūr* is from the root *b-w-r*, to perish, to be ruined, to lie waste — the same verb used at verse 10 of this sūrah: *wa-makru ulāʾika huwa yabūr*, "and their plotting shall come to ruin." The sūrah thus sets two uses of the same word against each other: the commerce of the believers never *tabūr*, and the plotting of the evildoers *yabūr*. One investment cannot be ruined; the other cannot survive.

**The Hope: Rajāʾ as a Station**

*Yarjūna* — "they hope." The verse places hope at the centre of the religious life, and the tradition made it a technical term. *Rajāʾ* is one of the stations of the heart, distinguished from *tamannī* (mere wishing) by the fact that it is attached to action. The one who hopes for a commerce has invested in it; the one who wishes for it has not.

The classical formulation balances *rajāʾ* against *khawf* and *khashyah* — and the placement of verse 29 immediately after verse 28 is precisely this balance. The scholars fear God (v. 28) and they hope for a commerce that will never perish (v. 29). Neither emotion alone is religion: fear without hope produces despair, and hope without fear produces presumption. The verse of Fāṭir that most directly addresses the balance is:

> *"Their sides forsake their beds, calling upon their Lord in fear and hope, and they spend from what We have provided them."* (Qur'an 32:16)

Fear and hope, in one sentence, with spending as the outcome — the same three elements as Fāṭir 29, in the same order of consequence.

**Al-Rāzī on the Sincerity Implied**

Al-Rāzī reads the hope for a commerce that will never perish as an allusion to the sincerity of these people: they seek only the Face of God and do not perform their deeds to be seen by men, as at 2:264, 4:38, 4:142, and 8:47. The reasoning is that a person who is seeking the attention of people has already received his return and has no commerce outstanding. The one who hopes for a return from God is the one who has not taken payment in advance from anyone else.

The Prophetic statement of the principle is in the ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"I am so free of need of any association that, of the deeds done for Me and for another besides Me, I accept only that which is purely for Me."* (Muslim 2985, in the report of Abū Hurayrah)

**The Verse as a Complete Programme**

Read as a whole, verse 29 describes a person with a daily recitation, an established prayer, and a regular expenditure of a portion of his provision — given both visibly and invisibly — all of it directed toward a return he cannot see and has chosen not to be paid for elsewhere.

That is not a description of the spiritual elite. It is a description of the minimum. Nothing in the verse is extraordinary: no vigils, no fasts beyond the obligatory, no pilgrimage, no martyrdom. The three practices named are the three that every Muslim already has, and the verse's claim is that these three, done sincerely and consistently, constitute a commerce that cannot be ruined.

The word *lan* — never — is the strongest form of future negation in Arabic. It does not say the commerce will not perish for a long time. It says it will not perish. In a world where every investment eventually fails — businesses close, currencies collapse, property is lost, reputation is destroyed, and the body itself is spent — the verse identifies one class of transaction that is exempt, and names the three acts that constitute it.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:30

> **that He may pay them their rewards in full and increase them from His Bounty. Truly He is Forgiving, Thankful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Grammar of the Reward**

The verse begins with a *lām* of purpose attached to the previous verse: *li-yuwaffiyahum ujūrahum wa-yazīdahum min faḍlih* — "that He may pay them their rewards in full and increase them from His Bounty." The hope of verse 29 is here given its object, and the object has two parts.

*Yuwaffī* is from the root *w-f-y*, to fulfil, to complete, to pay in full without deficiency. *Tawfiyah* is the exact and complete discharge of an obligation — the term used in commercial Arabic for settling an account to the last unit. *Ujūr* is the plural of *ajr*, a wage, a recompense due for work performed.

The first part of the reward is therefore *exact*: their wages, in full, nothing withheld, nothing discounted. The second part is *ziyādah*, increase — *wa-yazīdahum min faḍlih*, "and He increases them from His Bounty." *Faḍl* is unearned favour, grace beyond what is due; the same word used at verse 29 for the provision (*razāqnāhum*) and throughout the Qur'an for God's generosity beyond obligation.

The two parts answer two different human anxieties. The first answers the fear of being cheated: nothing you did will be lost. The second answers the fear of having done too little: what you did will be exceeded.

**"Rewards in Full and Increase"**

The same formulation appears in Sūrah al-Nisāʾ, and the parallel confirms the reading:

> *"As for those who believe and perform righteous deeds, We shall pay them their rewards in full, and We shall increase them from Our Bounty. And as for those who were disdainful and arrogant, We shall punish them with a painful punishment, and they will find for themselves no protector or helper apart from God."* (Qur'an 4:173)

The contrast in that verse is instructive: the believers receive payment plus increase; the arrogant receive punishment without a helper. There is no corresponding "increase" in the punishment, because — as verse 7 of this sūrah established — the good is multiplied and the evil is not.

The arithmetic of the increase is specified elsewhere:

> *"Whoever brings a good deed shall have ten times its like, and whoever brings an evil deed shall be recompensed only with its like."* (Qur'an 6:160)

> *"The likeness of those who spend their wealth in the way of God is as the likeness of a grain that grows seven ears, in every ear a hundred grains. And God multiplies for whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 2:261)

The phrase *wa-Allāhu yuḍāʿifu li-man yashāʾ* — "and God multiplies for whomsoever He wills" — is the key to the *ziyādah* of verse 30. The tenfold is the floor and the seven-hundredfold is an illustration; the actual multiple is God's choice, and the verse of Fāṭir leaves it unspecified.

**"Truly He Is Forgiving, Thankful"**

*Innahu ghafūrun shakūr* — two intensive Names, and their pairing is the theological content of the verse.

*Al-Ghafūr* is from the root *gh-f-r*, to cover, to shield, to protect from the consequence of a thing. It is the intensive form: the One who covers extensively and repeatedly. In the context of verse 30, the covering answers the deficiency in the deeds — the distractions in the prayer, the reluctance in the giving, the ostentation mixed into the sincerity, the recitation performed without attention. None of the three practices of verse 29 is performed perfectly by anyone, and *al-Ghafūr* is the Name that deals with the imperfection.

*Al-Shakūr* is the intensive of *shākir*, from the root *sh-k-r*, gratitude, thankfulness, the recognition of a favour together with its acknowledgment and its increase. Applied to God, the term requires explanation, because gratitude in human beings is a response to a benefit received, and God receives no benefit. The commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī — explain that God's *shukr* consists in His accepting the small deed, praising the one who performs it, and multiplying its reward beyond its measure. Al-Qurṭubī states the two Names' functions in the terms the source records: He forgives bad deeds and accepts good deeds.

That is the complete economy of the verse. The bad is covered; the good is accepted and multiplied. Between the two, nothing is lost in either direction.

**Why God Is Called "Thankful"**

The Name deserves attention because it makes an extraordinary claim: God thanks. The Qur'an uses the same description of itself elsewhere and connects it to precisely this context:

> *"Truly our Lord is Forgiving, Thankful."* (Qur'an 35:34 — spoken by the inhabitants of Paradise)

> *"And whoever is thankful is thankful only for himself. And whoever disbelieves — truly God is Self-Sufficient, Praised."* (Qur'an 31:12)

> *"If you disbelieve — truly God is Self-Sufficient of you, and He is not pleased with disbelief for His servants. And if you are thankful, He is pleased with it for you."* (Qur'an 39:7)

The tradition contains a report that makes the meaning concrete. In the ḥadīth qudsī of Muslim:

> *"God says: 'O My servants! You will not attain My harm and so harm Me, and you will not attain My benefit and so benefit Me... O My servants! Were the first of you and the last of you... to be as pious as the most pious heart among you, that would not add anything to My dominion.'"* (Muslim 2577)

God gains nothing from human obedience. And yet He calls Himself *Shakūr*. The Name therefore describes not a benefit received but an acknowledgment given: He recognises the effort, weighs it at more than its worth, and pays it beyond its due. There is no word in human language for this other than gratitude, which is why the Qur'an uses it.

**The Ḥadīth Qudsī That Explains the Increase**

The mechanism of the *ziyādah* is described in one of the most reported of the divine sayings:

> *"God says: 'I am as My servant thinks I am. And I am with him when he remembers Me. If he remembers Me in himself, I remember him in Myself; and if he remembers Me in a gathering, I remember him in a gathering better than it. And if he draws near to Me a span, I draw near to him a cubit; and if he draws near to Me a cubit, I draw near to him a fathom; and if he comes to Me walking, I come to him running.'"* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

The ratios in that report are the *shukr* of verse 30 in numerical form: a span answered by a cubit (four times), a cubit by a fathom (four times again), walking by running. Every movement toward God is answered with a greater movement from Him. That is what it means for the reward to be paid in full *and increased*.

**The Effect on the Person Doing the Work**

The verse's practical function is to address a specific and common spiritual problem: the feeling that one's practice is too poor to count.

Everyone who has prayed with a wandering mind, given while reluctant, or recited without understanding knows the feeling. The practice is performed, and immediately afterwards the conscience produces a critique — that it was half-hearted, that the intention was mixed, that it was less than it should have been. Left untreated, this critique becomes a reason to stop. The reasoning is not stated but it is real: if my prayer is this bad, it is not worth praying.

Verse 30 answers the critique with two Names rather than with an argument. *Ghafūr* deals with the deficiency: it is covered. *Shakūr* deals with the remainder: it is accepted, praised, and multiplied. Neither Name requires the deed to have been excellent. Both require it to have been done.

There is a discipline embedded in this that the tradition has always drawn: perform the deed, and do not audit it afterwards. The audit belongs to the *Ghafūr* and the *Shakūr*, who are better at it than you are and who are not, as the verse insists, inclined to reduce. The person who audits his own worship before submitting it is doing the work of a judge who has already been appointed.

**The Sequence Completed**

Verses 28, 29, and 30 form a complete cycle: knowledge producing awe; awe producing recitation, prayer, and spending; and the three practices producing a full payment and an increase from a Lord who is Forgiving and Thankful.

Nothing in the cycle depends on the believer's excellence. It depends on his knowledge, his practice, and his hope — and the last of these is guaranteed by the two Names that close the verse. The commerce of verse 29 cannot perish because the One who settles it is *ghafūr* toward its defects and *shakūr* toward its substance.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:31

> **And that which We have revealed unto thee from the Book is the truth, confirming what came before it. Verily God is of His servants Aware, Seeing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That Which We Have Revealed to Thee From the Book"**

*Wa-alladhī awḥaynā ilayka min al-kitābi huwa al-ḥaqq* — the verse identifies the Qur'an by three descriptions in one clause: it is *revealed* (not composed), it is *from the Book* (not the whole of it), and it is *the truth* (not a truth among others).

The preposition *min* — "from" — has generated the two readings the source records. The first: the Qur'an is part of *the Book* in the sense of revelation generally, the body of divine guidance. The second, from al-Rāzī and others: *the Book* is the Preserved Tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*), the heavenly archetype of all revelation, and what was revealed to Muhammad ﷺ is taken from it:

> *"Nay, it is a glorious Qur'an, in a Preserved Tablet."* (Qur'an 85:21–22)

> *"And indeed it is in the Mother of the Book with Us — exalted, wise."* (Qur'an 43:4)

The second reading places the Qur'an within a larger divine reality: it is not a text that began in the seventh century but a descent from something that already existed. The same understanding underlies the description of the revelation of previous scriptures:

> *"God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Ever-Watchful. He sent down to thee the Book in truth, confirming what came before it; and He sent down the Torah and the Gospel aforetime, as guidance for mankind."* (Qur'an 3:2–4)

**"Confirming What Came Before It"**

*Muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi* — "confirming what is before it," that is, what preceded it. *Muṣaddiq* is from the root *ṣ-d-q*, truth, veracity; the causative form means to declare true, to corroborate, to confirm. The same root gives *ṣiddīq*, the one who is intensely truthful — the title of Abū Bakr.

The Qur'an describes itself as *muṣaddiq* in more than a dozen places: 2:41, 2:89, 2:91, 2:97, 2:101, 3:3, 3:81, 5:48, 6:92, 10:37, 12:111, 46:12, 46:30. The most complete statement is in al-Māʾidah:

> *"And We sent down to thee the Book in truth, confirming what came before it of the Book, and a guardian (*muhaymin*) over it. So judge between them by what God has sent down, and follow not their desires away from what has come to thee of the truth."* (Qur'an 5:48)

Two functions are named there, and both are implied in Fāṭir's verse: confirmation (*taṣdīq*) and guardianship (*haymanah*). The Qur'an confirms what the earlier scriptures contain of truth, and it stands over them as a criterion — the standard by which what remains in them is assessed. Al-Rāzī's reading in the source is that the Book confirms what came before it so that there is no difference between it and previous revelations, and he cites:

> *"Naught has been said unto thee save that which has been said unto the messengers before thee."* (Qur'an 41:43)

**Confirmation as an Evidential Argument**

The doctrine of *taṣdīq* is not merely a statement of continuity; it is an argument, and the Qur'an uses it as one. When a claim agrees with testimony given independently and earlier, the agreement corroborates the claim. A forger has an interest in departing from prior testimony; a truthful reporter has no reason to depart from it.

Sūrah Yūsuf makes the argument explicit:

> *"It is not a narration invented, but a confirmation of what was before it, a detailed explanation of all things, a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 12:111)

Sūrah al-Aḥqāf records a specific case — the Jews who recognised the Qur'an because it agreed with what they had:

> *"Say: Have you considered — if it is from God and you disbelieve in it, while a witness from the Children of Israel testifies to its like and believes, while you are arrogant? Truly God guides not the wrongdoing people."* (Qur'an 46:10)

And:

> *"And when it is recited to them, they say: 'We believe in it. Truly it is the truth from our Lord. Indeed we were, before it, submitters.'"* (Qur'an 28:53)

**What Confirmation Does Not Mean**

The doctrine requires precision, because it is frequently misstated in either direction. Confirmation is not the claim that the Qur'an is identical to the earlier scriptures — 5:48 says it is a *guardian* over them, which implies differences to be adjudicated. Nor is it the claim that the earlier scriptures are false — the Qur'an repeatedly affirms the Torah and the Gospel as revelations:

> *"Say: We believe in God, and in what was sent down to us, and in what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes, and in what was given to Moses and Jesus, and in what was given to the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we are submitters."* (Qur'an 2:136)

The position is therefore specific: the earlier revelations were true when given, they were subsequently altered in transmission, and the Qur'an confirms what remains of their original truth while correcting what has entered them. The verses about alteration are explicit:

> *"Among the Jews are those who distort words from their places..."* (Qur'an 4:46)

> *"So woe to those who write the Book with their own hands, then say, 'This is from God,' that they may sell it for a small price."* (Qur'an 2:79)

**"Verily God Is of His Servants Aware, Seeing"**

*Inna Allāha bi-ʿibādihi la-khabīrun baṣīr* — two Names, and both are names of knowledge. *Khabīr* is from *kh-b-r*, acquaintance with the inner reality of a thing — knowledge gained by contact rather than by inference. *Baṣīr* is from *b-ṣ-r*, seeing with penetration. The same pair appeared at verse 14 in the phrase *"none informs you like one who is Aware."*

The two Names together cover the interior and the exterior: God is acquainted with what is inside the servant and sees what he does outside. In the context of this verse, they answer a question that the claim of confirmation raises: who is qualified to say what the earlier scriptures contained and what they now contain? The answer is the One who is *Khabīr* of His servants — who knows what they preserved, what they altered, what they intended, and what they concealed.

Al-Rāzī connects the phrase to the covenant: it is a reminder not to break the covenant either outwardly or secretly, because God knows what lies within the breasts — the same statement made at verse 38 of this sūrah.

**The Verse's Position in the Sūrah**

Verse 31 stands between two sections. Verses 29 and 30 described the practices of the believers and their reward. Verses 32 and 33 will describe the bequest of the Book and the three categories of those who received it. Verse 31 is the hinge: it establishes that the Book being bequeathed is the truth and that it confirms what came before.

The logic of the sequence is complete. The Book is true (v. 31). It has been given as an inheritance to a chosen people (v. 32). That people includes three grades, from the self-wronging to the foremost (v. 32). All of them enter Gardens of Eden (v. 33). The truth of the Book is what makes the inheritance possible, and the confirmation of the earlier revelations is what makes it continuous with the whole history of prophecy.

**What the Verse Asks of the Reader**

The doctrine of *taṣdīq* produces a specific attitude in the Muslim toward prior revelation, and the attitude is neither rejection nor uncritical acceptance. The Muslim believes that God sent the Torah and the Gospel; that what reached him of them is mixed; that the Qur'an is the criterion by which the mixture is separated; and that the One who is *Aware* and *Seeing* of His servants knows which parts came from God and which came from men.

That position requires a discipline the verse implies but does not state: the reader must not claim to know more than the criterion tells him. Where the Qur'an confirms, he confirms. Where it corrects, he corrects. Where it is silent, he is silent — because he is not *Khabīr*, and the verse's final clause exists to remind him of that.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:32

> **Then We bequeathed the Book to those of Our servants whom We had chosen. Among them are those who wrong themselves, those who take a middle course, and those who are foremost in good deeds, by God's Leave. That is the great bounty:**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Inheritance, Not an Achievement**

*Thumma awrathnā al-kitāba alladhīna iṣṭafaynā min ʿibādinā* — "Then We bequeathed the Book to those of Our servants whom We had chosen."

The verb is *awrathnā*, from the root *w-r-th*, inheritance. To inherit is to receive something one did not earn, from someone who owned it, by a transfer that is not a transaction. The Qur'an uses the same verb of the Garden:

> *"That is the Garden which We cause to be inherited by those of Our servants who are reverent."* (Qur'an 19:63)

> *"And We caused the people who were oppressed to inherit the eastern parts of the earth and the western parts thereof, which We had blessed."* (Qur'an 7:137)

And of the earlier bequest, in the same terms:

> *"And indeed We gave Moses the Guidance, and We caused the Children of Israel to inherit the Book."* (Qur'an 40:53)

The choice of the inheritance verb does substantial doctrinal work. The Book is not a prize for the excellent; it is an estate left to a people. This is why the verse can include, among its recipients, those who wrong themselves.

**"Those Whom We Had Chosen": Iṣṭifāʾ**

*Iṣṭafaynā* is the eighth form of the root *ṣ-f-w*, whose basic sense is clarity and purity — *ṣafw* is the purest part of a thing, the clear sediment-free portion. The eighth form *iṣṭafā* means to select, to choose for oneself, to single out. It is the verb used of God's choice of the prophets:

> *"Truly God chose Adam, and Noah, and the family of Abraham, and the family of ʿImrān, above all creation."* (Qur'an 3:33)

The selection described in verse 32 is therefore not a selection of the morally perfect but a selection for a purpose. God chose this community to carry the Book, as He chose earlier communities to carry theirs.

**Who Are the Chosen? The Range of Classical Answers**

The commentators gave several identifications, all recorded in the source, and the range matters because it determines who is included.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Thaʿlabī report the view that the chosen are the prophets. Others, citing the famous ḥadīth, identify them as the people of knowledge — *"The people of knowledge are the heirs of the prophets"* — a report the tradition holds widely, transmitted by Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī. The dominant view, held by al-Bayḍāwī and others, is that the chosen are the whole of the Muslim community.

The third reading is the one that explains the rest of the verse, because only a community-wide bequest can include all three of the categories that follow. If the Book were bequeathed only to the prophets or only to the scholars, the self-wronging could not be among the recipients — and the verse explicitly places them among the recipients.

The source also records the Shīʿite reading, in which the chosen refers to the descendants of the Prophet ﷺ or to the Imams specifically, with the fifth and sixth Imams reported to have said of the verse: *"This refers specifically to us."* The existence of this reading across the two traditions is worth noting: both agree that the bequest is real, specific, and divinely made; they differ on its recipients. The Sunni reading that follows from the verse's own threefold division is the one that makes the categories in the second half apply to the whole community.

**The Three Categories**

*Fa-minhum ẓālimun li-nafsihi wa-minhum muqtaṣidun wa-minhum sābiqun bi-l-khayrāti bi-idhni Allāh* — "and among them are some who wrong themselves, some who take a middle course, and some who are foremost in good deeds, by God's Leave."

Each of the three terms is precise.

*Ẓālim li-nafsihi* — "one who wrongs himself." *Ẓulm* is from the root *ẓ-l-m*, to place a thing where it does not belong, to deprive someone of his due, to act in the dark. When the object of the wronging is the self, the term describes the person who damages his own interest — and the Qur'an uses it constantly:

> *"And whosoever exceeds the limits of God — those are the ones who wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 65:1)

> *"They said: 'Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves. And if Thou dost not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we shall surely be among the losers.'"* (Qur'an 7:23)

The self-wronging of this category, in the Sunni reading recorded by Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī, is the negligence of the believer: omitting some obligatory actions and committing some forbidden ones. He is not outside the community; he is inside it and failing.

*Muqtaṣid* — from the root *q-ṣ-d*, to aim at, to intend, to take the middle path. *Iqtiṣād* is moderation, the mean between excess and deficiency — the same root as *qaṣd*, purpose and direction, and as *al-iqtiṣād*, economy. The *muqtaṣid* is the one who holds the middle: he performs the obligatory and avoids the forbidden, but neglects praiseworthy deeds and does some things that are disliked. He is sound, not outstanding.

*Sābiq bi-l-khayrāt* — "one who races ahead in good deeds." *Sābiq* is the active participle of *sabaqa*, to precede, to outstrip, to win a race; the same root gives *sābiqūn* in 56:10 and *sabq* as the prize for precedence. The *sābiq* performs both the obligatory and the recommended, and avoids both the forbidden and the disliked.

**The Ranking, and Its Basis in Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah**

The three categories correspond to a division the Qur'an makes elsewhere, and the source records the comparison:

> *"And you shall be three classes: those of the right hand — what of those of the right hand! And those of the left hand — what of those of the left hand! And the foremost, the foremost — those are the ones brought near."* (Qur'an 56:7–11)

The commentators who read the *ẓālim* as the disbeliever or hypocrite place him with the companions of the left, in the Fire; those who read him as a negligent believer place all three in Paradise, in different ranks. Al-Ṭabarī records both, and the dispute between them has been one of the most discussed questions in the exegesis of this verse.

The tradition preserves a report attributed to the Prophet ﷺ, transmitted through several Companions including Abū al-Dardāʾ, ʿUthmān, and Ibn ʿAbbās, in which he recited this verse and said that all three are in Paradise. The chains of this report vary considerably in strength — some are graded weak, others accepted — and it should not be cited as an established ḥadīth without noting the grading. What can be said with confidence is that the majority Sunni reading of the verse, as recorded by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ṭabarī, holds that the bequest includes the self-wronging believer, and that the threefold outcome is: the self-wronging is forgiven, the middle is given an easy reckoning, and the foremost enter without reckoning.

**"By God's Leave"**

The phrase *bi-idhni Allāh* attaches specifically to the third category, and its placement is a deliberate deflation. Even the racing ahead in good deeds is by permission, not by capacity. The *sābiq* did not achieve precedence by his own power; he was enabled to it.

This is the same principle that the Prophetic tradition states in its most famous formulation:

> *"None of you will be saved by his deeds." They said: "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: "Not even me, unless God envelops me with mercy from Him."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)

The verse's structure confirms it: the highest category is qualified by *bi-idhni Allāh*, and the reward of all three is described at the end as *al-faḍl al-kabīr* — the great bounty. Nothing in the verse is earned in the strict sense.

**Al-Tustarī and Al-Ḥasan: Two Other Divisions**

The source preserves two further readings that divide the categories differently. Al-Tustarī relates them to knowledge: the foremost is the one who is learned; the one who takes a middle course is the one who is learning; the one who wrongs himself is the one who is ignorant. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī relates them to the balance of deeds: the foremost are those whose good deeds outweigh their misdeeds; the middle are those whose good and bad balance; the self-wronging are those whose misdeeds outweigh their good.

Al-Ḥasan's reading is particularly useful because it makes the categories diagnostic rather than typological. They are not three fixed kinds of people but three states, and a person can move between them within a single day.

**The Order of the Listing Is the Argument**

The verse lists the three from lowest to highest — self-wronging, middle, foremost — and the source correctly observes that this order can be read as the trajectory of the bequest itself: the Book brings a person from the lowest state to the highest, by God's Leave.

That reading is the hope of the verse, and it is why the ordering matters. Had the verse listed the foremost first, it would have been a description of ranks. Listed as it is, it is a description of a road, and the reader who finds himself in the first category is being told that the second and third lie ahead of him, in that order, by a permission that is available.

**"That Is the Great Bounty"**

*Dhālika huwa al-faḍlu al-kabīr* — the emphatic *huwa* between the subject and predicate produces the identification: *that — it is — the great bounty.* The referent is disputed: the Gardens of Eden of the next verse; the bequest of the Book itself; or the fact that precedence in good deeds is a bounty bestowed rather than a rank achieved.

All three are consistent, and the widest reading is the most accurate: the whole arrangement is the bounty. That a Book should exist, that it should be given to a people, that the people should include failures, and that the failures should be among the inheritors — that is what the verse calls *al-faḍl al-kabīr*.

**The Supplication of the First Category**

The verse's practical conclusion belongs to the one who recognises himself in *ẓālim li-nafsihi*, and the tradition has provided him the words. When Abū Bakr asked the Prophet ﷺ to teach him a supplication to say in his prayer, he was taught:

> *"O God, truly I have wronged myself much (*ẓalamtu nafsī ẓulman kathīran*), and none forgives sins but Thou. Forgive me with a forgiveness from Thee, and have mercy upon me. Truly Thou art the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (al-Bukhārī 834; Muslim 2705)

The phrase of the verse and the phrase of the supplication are the same: *ẓalamtu nafsī*. The person who wrongs himself is not thereby excluded from the bequest; he is included in it, and he is given a prayer appropriate to his condition. That is the whole of verse 32 in one duʿāʾ.

The colon at the end of the verse in the received text is not editorial but grammatical: what follows is the content of the bounty.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:33

> **Gardens of Eden which they will enter, adorned therein with bracelets of gold and pearls; and their garments therein will be of silk.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**ʿAdn: The Garden of Permanent Residence**

*Jannātu ʿadn* — "Gardens of Eden," or more precisely "Gardens of Settling." The root *ʿ-d-n* means to dwell permanently in a place, to settle, to take up residence; *ʿadana bi-l-makān* is to remain in a place without leaving it. The same root gives *maʿdin*, a mine — the place where a metal permanently resides.

The word therefore does not describe the garden's beauty but its permanence. It answers the deepest objection the human being raises against every good thing he has: that it ends. *Jannāt ʿadn* are gardens you do not leave.

The Qur'an uses the phrase repeatedly and consistently in this sense:

> *"The Gardens of Eden, which the Compassionate promised His servants in the unseen. Truly His Promise shall come."* (Qur'an 19:61)

> *"Gardens of Eden — they shall enter them, adorned therein with bracelets of gold and pearls, and their garments therein of silk."* (Qur'an 35:33)

> *"And whosoever comes unto Him believing, having performed righteous deeds — those shall have the highest ranks, the Gardens of Eden beneath which rivers flow, abiding therein forever. That is the reward of him who purifies himself."* (Qur'an 20:75–76)

*Khālidīna fīhā* — abiding therein forever — is the standard accompaniment, and it states in words what *ʿadn* states in a name.

**"Adorned Therein": The Passive of Divine Dressing**

*Yuḥallawna fīhā* — "they are adorned therein." The verb is the passive of *ḥallā*, from the root *ḥ-l-y*, to adorn with jewellery; *ḥilyah* is the ornament itself. The passive is significant: they do not adorn themselves, they are adorned. The dressing of the inhabitants of the Garden is done to them, by attendants, as an honour — the same grammar as being served:

> *"And there shall go round about them youths of perpetual freshness, with goblets and ewers and a cup from a flowing spring."* (Qur'an 56:17–18)

> *"And there go round about them servants of their own, as though they were hidden pearls."* (Qur'an 52:24)

**A Word That Returns From Verse 12**

The *ḥilyah* of this verse is the same word used at verse 12 of this sūrah: *wa-tastakhrijūna ḥilyatan talbasūnahā* — "and you bring out ornaments that you wear," from the salty sea. The internal echo is exact, and it is not accidental.

In this world, the ornament is extracted from the bitter sea by labour — diving, risk, exhaustion, and the loss of life that the pearl trade always involved. In the Garden, the ornament is placed upon the wearer without labour. The same object appears in both verses, and the difference between them is the difference between the two worlds: in one it costs everything to obtain; in the other it is given.

**"Bracelets of Gold and Pearls"**

*Min asāwira min dhahabin wa-luʾluʾan* — *asāwir* is the plural of *suwār*, a bracelet worn on the forearm; *dhahab* is gold; *luʾluʾ* is pearls. Sūrah al-Kahf and Sūrah al-Insān describe the same adornment:

> *"For them are Gardens of Eden beneath which rivers flow; they shall be adorned therein with bracelets of gold, and shall wear green garments of fine silk and rich brocade, reclining therein upon couches. Excellent is the reward, and beautiful the resting place."* (Qur'an 18:31)

> *"And upon them shall be garments of green silk and brocade, and they shall be adorned with bracelets of silver, and their Lord shall give them a pure drink."* (Qur'an 76:21)

The mention of silver alongside gold in 76:21 shows that the descriptions are not a single fixed inventory but varied accounts of the same reality. The commentators note this and treat the different lists as complementary rather than contradictory.

Gold and pearls were, in seventh-century Arabia, the two most inaccessible luxuries: gold because of its rarity and price, pearls because of the danger of obtaining them. Both are named here for the same reason — they were what the poor could never have. The adornment of the Garden is described in the currency of the world's most extreme inequality, and distributed without inequality.

**"Their Garments Therein Will Be of Silk"**

*Wa-libāsuhum fīhā ḥarīr* — the clothing is silk. The verse is remarkable in its context because silk is prohibited to the men of this community in this world, and the prohibition is explicit in the tradition:

> *"These two are forbidden for the males of my community and permitted for the females: silk and gold."* (Abū Dāwūd 4057; al-Tirmidhī 1720; Ibn Mājah 3595 — the wording varies)

> *"Whoever wears silk in this world shall not wear it in the Hereafter."* (al-Bukhārī 5831; Muslim 2069)

> *"Do not wear silk, for whoever wears it in this world shall not wear it in the Hereafter."* (al-Bukhārī 5830)

The second of these is a severe warning, and the tradition records the exception made for necessity: ʿUmar and others were permitted silk for the treatment of skin conditions and itching, and al-Zubayr and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf received a concession for the same reason (al-Bukhārī 5839).

The relationship between the prohibition here and the grant there is one of the clearest statements of the Islamic understanding of postponement. The pleasure is not denied; it is deferred. The thing forbidden in this world is granted in the next, and the forbidding is not a declaration that the thing is bad but that its time has not come.

There is a moral structure in this that the tradition drew out. The person who abstains from silk in this world receives silk in the next; the person who takes it here forfeits it there. The abstainer is not losing something and the taker is not gaining something. Both are making the same transaction at different times, and only one of them is receiving it twice.

**The Necessity of the Unseen Qualifier**

All such descriptions require the qualification the tradition supplies, without which they become literalism. The ḥadīth qudsī recorded in both collections states:

> *"God said: 'I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human heart has conceived.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3244; Muslim 2824)

The Prophet ﷺ then recited: *"So no soul knows what is kept hidden for them of coolness of eyes, as a reward for what they used to do"* (32:17).

Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said, in a statement widely quoted by the commentators though not established with a sound chain: there is nothing in Paradise of the things of this world except the names. The meaning is that gold, silk, pearls, water, milk, honey, and fruit in the Garden share names with their worldly counterparts and do not share their limitations — they do not tarnish, tear, spoil, or satiate.

The two statements together give the correct reading of verse 33: the descriptions are true and they are not exhaustive; they are given in the vocabulary of human desire so that human desire is engaged, and they point to something that vocabulary cannot contain.

**Why the Reward Is Described Bodily**

The insistence on garments, jewellery, food, drink, couches, and shade is a deliberate feature of Qur'anic eschatology, and it distinguishes Islam from traditions that spiritualise the afterlife into a state of disembodied contemplation. The resurrection the Qur'an announces is bodily, and the reward is bodily, because the human being is bodily:

> *"Does man suppose that We shall not assemble his bones? Yes indeed — We are able to reshape his very fingertips."* (Qur'an 75:3–4)

The detail of the fingertips is telling. The resurrection is not a general reassembly but a reconstruction precise enough to restore the unique ridges of a hand. And the reward is correspondingly concrete: not a state of mind but silk on skin, gold on the arm, shade on the face.

This concreteness is not a concession to crude imagination. It is a claim about the goodness of the body. The body that fasted, stood in prayer, endured heat, and abstained from silk is the body that is rewarded — not a soul released from it. The Islamic doctrine of the Garden therefore affirms matter rather than escaping it.

**The Reversal of Verse 21**

Verse 21 of this sūrah contrasted the shade and the scorching heat. Verse 33 delivers the shade's inhabitants and clothes them. The reversal is complete: the ones who stood in *ḥarūr* are now in silk; the ones who had no ornament are now adorned; the ones who extracted pearls from the bitter sea by risk are now given pearls without labour.

And, as verse 34 will record, the first thing they say is not about the gold or the silk but about the grief that has been taken away.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:34

> **And they will say, "Praise be to God, Who has dispelled grief from us. Truly our Lord is Forgiving, Thankful,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Speech of Paradise**

The verse records what the inhabitants of the Garden say on entering it, and what they say is not about the gold, the pearls, the silk, the rivers, or the shade. It is about the absence of something. *Al-ḥamdu li-llāhi alladhī adhhaba ʿannā al-ḥazan* — "Praise be to God, Who has removed grief from us."

The choice defines the entire Qur'anic anthropology of happiness. The good of Paradise is not presented primarily as the acquisition of pleasure but as the removal of sorrow. Before the description of what the Garden contains comes the description of what it does not: no grief.

The same declaration appears in Sūrah al-Zumar, where it is fuller:

> *"And they shall say: 'Praise be to God, Who has fulfilled His Promise to us, and caused us to inherit the earth, settling in the Garden where we will.' Excellent is the reward of the workers."* (Qur'an 39:74)

And in Sūrah al-Aḥqāf:

> *"And they shall say: 'Praise be to God, Who has guided us to this. And we would not have been guided had God not guided us. Truly the messengers of our Lord came with the truth.'"* (Qur'an 7:43)

Note what all three have in common: the first act of Paradise is *ḥamd*. The state of the blessed begins with praise, and it begins with praise for a removal rather than an addition.

**Ḥazan and Hamm: Two Different Burdens**

The Arabic vocabulary of sorrow distinguishes what English collapses, and the distinction is essential to this verse.

*Ḥazan* is grief over what has passed — sorrow directed backward, at loss, at what was done or not done, at what cannot be undone. *Hamm* is anxiety over what is coming — sorrow directed forward, at what may happen, at what one cannot control. Together they cover the entire temporal range of human suffering: the past one cannot change and the future one cannot know.

The Prophetic supplication names both, and the tradition treats them as the pair from which the believer most needs protection:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in Thee from anxiety (*al-hamm*) and grief (*al-ḥazan*), from incapacity and laziness, from cowardice and miserliness, from the burden of debt and from the domination of men."* (al-Bukhārī 6369)

The eight things named in that supplication are the ordinary contents of a burdened life, and the first two are the interior states that accompany all the rest. A person may have no debt, no enemy, and no incapacity, and still be destroyed by *hamm* and *ḥazan*.

Verse 34 states that *al-ḥazan* has been removed — using the definite article, which the commentators read as comprehensive: *the* grief, grief as such, all of it. Not some of it; not the acute grief while the background unease remains. The category is abolished.

**What the Grief Consisted Of**

The commentators specify what this grief was, and their list is the list of everything that grieves a human being in this life. Al-Ṭabarī records it as the sorrow arising from the fear of the Fire. Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī record it as the anxieties of this life and the next. Al-Zamakhsharī adds Satan and his whispering. Al-Ṭabarī also records death itself — the grief of mortality, which will not return, since they will not die again.

The four are worth separating because they are the four griefs of ordinary human existence.

The fear of the Fire: the specific religious anxiety of the believer, who has done less than he intended and knows what is promised.

The anxieties of this life: provision, health, family, status, the outcome of every undertaking — the running background of worry that most people experience as normal and that is, in fact, the largest single consumer of human mental life.

Satan and his whispering: the interior voice that accuses, discourages, beautifies, and disturbs — a grief that is present even when circumstances are good, and whose removal is the removal of an inhabitant rather than a problem.

Death: the grief of every loss, since every loss is a rehearsal of the last one.

The Qur'an states the removal of all of them in a formula it repeats more than a dozen times:

> *"Surely those who say 'Our Lord is God' and then remain upright — the angels descend upon them: 'Fear not, and grieve not, and receive glad tidings of the Garden which you were promised.'"* (Qur'an 41:30)

> *"No fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve."* (Qur'an 2:38, 62, 112, 262, 274, 277; 3:170; 5:69; 6:48; 7:35; 10:62; 46:13)

The formula pairs the two removals: *khawf* (fear of what is coming) and *ḥazan* (grief over what has passed). Together they exhaust the possibilities, and both are cancelled.

**"Our Lord Is Forgiving, Thankful"**

*Inna rabbanā la-ghafūrun shakūr* — the two Names return from verse 30, where they described God in relation to those who recite, pray, and spend. There they were stated by the revelation; here they are stated by the recipients, who have now seen the settlement and can confirm it from experience.

The shift from *Allāh* in the first clause to *rabbanā* — "our Lord" — in the second is a shift from the Name of majesty to the Name of relationship. *Rabb* is the one who nurtures, sustains, educates, and brings to completion; it is the Name the believer uses when he asks. Having praised God for the removal of grief, the inhabitants turn to the Lord who raised them, and the sentence becomes intimate.

The two Names answer two different deficiencies. *Ghafūr* deals with what was wrong: the sins, the negligence, the self-wronging of verse 32. *Shakūr* deals with what was right: the small deeds, the imperfect prayers, the reluctant giving — accepted, praised, and multiplied.

The inhabitants of Paradise are therefore describing, in two words, the entire mechanism by which they arrived. Nothing they did was sufficient; the *Ghafūr* covered the deficiency. What they did was small; the *Shakūr* increased it. The verse is a receipt.

**The Connection to Verse 32: The Self-Wronging Arrive**

The source records a report that ties this verse directly to the first of the three categories in verse 32: the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said that the one who wrongs himself will be afflicted by distress and sorrow in that state, and that this is the meaning of *"Praise be to God, Who has dispelled grief from us"* (as recorded by al-Ṭabarī).

The reading makes verse 34 the arrival of the *ẓālim li-nafsihi*. He was forgiven at verse 32; he entered at verse 33; and at verse 34 he speaks. His speech is not the confident declaration of the foremost but the relieved exclamation of one who expected the worst and did not receive it — which is why the grief he names is fear of the Fire.

This is why the verse's praise is for the *removal* rather than the addition. The foremost in good deeds might reasonably have praised God for the honour; the self-wronging believer praises God for the escape. That both are in the same Garden saying the same sentence is one of the strongest statements of the sūrah's doctrine of bounty.

**The Grief That Remains in This Life**
