The verse has an application to the present that the tradition consistently draws. Grief is not abolished in this life, and the Qur'an does not promise that it will be. The Prophet ﷺ grieved — at the death of his son Ibrāhīm, at the rejection of his people, at the loss of his Companions — and his grief is recorded without reproach:

> *"The eye weeps and the heart grieves, and we say nothing but what pleases our Lord. And truly, O Ibrāhīm, we are grieved by thy parting."* (al-Bukhārī 1303; Muslim 2315)

The verse does not ask the believer to be griefless now. It tells him what the end of grief looks like, and that knowledge changes the character of the grief he has. Grief that ends is not the same experience as grief that will not end — a fact anyone who has sat with a person in prolonged suffering knows. The patient who believes his condition is permanent and the patient who believes it is terminal but time-limited endure different amounts of the same pain.

Verse 34 supplies the second belief. The grief is real, it is comprehensive, and it has an end that has been announced in advance by the One who will remove it. The praise in the verse is, in that sense, available now — as an expectation rather than an experience, and as the reason the believer can carry grief without being destroyed by it.

**The Sentence That Does Not Finish**

Verse 34 ends with a comma, not a period, because the speech continues into verse 35. The inhabitants of Paradise are mid-sentence, and the Qur'an leaves them mid-sentence — which is a small stylistic fact with a large implication. The praise of the Garden does not conclude. It is not a declaration made once upon entry and then replaced by enjoyment; it is the ongoing condition of the blessed, running without interruption.

The next verse completes the clause, and it completes it by naming the reason the praise will not stop.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:35

> **Who, of His Bounty, has caused us to dwell in the Abode of Everlasting Life, wherein no weariness shall touch us, nor fatigue befall us."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Abode of Settling"**

*Alladhī aḥallanā dāra al-muqāmah min faḍlih* — "Who has caused us to settle in the Abode of Residence, of His Bounty."

*Dār* is a dwelling, an abode, a house together with its courtyard. *Muqāmah* is from the root *q-w-m*, to stand, to remain standing, to reside; the form denotes the place or state of settled residence. The phrase *dār al-muqāmah* is therefore the dwelling in which one stays — and it stands in direct contrast with the phrase the Qur'an uses of this world:

> *"And the life of this world is naught but play and diversion; and the Abode of the Hereafter is better for those who are reverent. Will you not then understand?"* (Qur'an 6:32)

> *"O my people! The life of this world is but a passing enjoyment, and truly the Hereafter is the Abode of Settling (*dār al-qarār*)."* (Qur'an 40:39)

The two abodes are named in the Qur'an by their defining characteristic: this one passes, that one settles. The word *muqāmah* here and *qarār* there both come from the idea of coming to rest after movement.

The verb *aḥallanā* is causative: He *caused us to settle*. The inhabitants did not arrive under their own power, find the place, and establish residence. They were settled. The same grammar appeared at verse 33 — *yuḥallawna*, they are adorned — and it governs the whole description of Paradise: everything there is done to the inhabitants. They are entered, adorned, served, settled, and relieved. Their activity is praise.

**"Of His Bounty": The Qualifier That Removes All Claim**

*Min faḍlih* — "of His Bounty." The phrase is the theological core of the verse, and the commentators tie it directly to the report preserved in both collections:

> *"None will enter Paradise by virtue of his deeds — not even me — unless God encompasses me with Mercy and Bounty."* (as cited by Ibn Kathīr on 35:35; al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)

The inhabitants of Paradise say the same thing about themselves that the Prophet ﷺ said about himself. Their residence is not the wage of their work; it is the bounty of their Lord. This is consistent with verse 32, which called the whole arrangement *al-faḍl al-kabīr*, and with verse 30, which separated the payment of wages (*ujūr*) from the increase *from His Bounty* (*min faḍlih*).

The distinction is precise and it matters: the wages are earned and paid in full; the entry, the dwelling, and the increase are bounty. A worker receives his wage; he does not receive the house he lives in by his wage alone.

**Two Words for Weariness**

*Lā yamassunā fīhā naṣabun wa-lā yamassunā fīhā lughūb* — "no weariness shall touch us therein, nor shall fatigue touch us therein."

The verse uses two near-synonyms, and the commentators distinguish them, as the source records. Al-Rāzī explains that *lughūb* is fatigue and weariness, while *naṣab* is the toil and labour that produces it — so the verse removes both the effort and its consequence. Ibn Kathīr records a second distinction: *naṣab* is weariness of the body and *lughūb* weariness of the soul or spirit.

Both readings are supported by the lexical field. *Naṣab* comes from the root *n-ṣ-b*, to set up, to install, to labour at something — the same root gives *nuṣbah* (an erected marker) and *tanṣīb* (installation). It denotes exertion directed at an object. *Lughūb* comes from *l-gh-b*, to be exhausted, to become weak and spent; *lāghib* describes one who has been used up.

The combination is comprehensive. Whatever form exhaustion takes — the tiredness of work, the tiredness of the body after illness, the tiredness of the mind after concentration, the tiredness of the spirit after long grief, the tiredness of hope deferred — the verse names it and removes it.

The verb used for the removal is *yamassunā*, "shall touch us," from the root *m-s-s*, to touch, to make contact. The negation is not that weariness will be light or occasional; it is that it will not make contact.

**The Same Promise in Sūrah al-Ḥijr**

> *"And We shall remove whatever is in their breasts of resentment — as brothers, upon couches facing one another. No weariness shall touch them therein, nor shall they be expelled therefrom."* (Qur'an 15:47–48)

The parallel is nearly verbatim in its central clause — *lā yamassuhum fīhā naṣab* — and adds a second removal: expulsion. The two removals answer the two insecurities of every good thing in this life. Every pleasure here is threatened either by exhaustion (it tires you) or by loss (it is taken from you). Paradise removes both.

**What the Removal of Toil Means**

The claim is more radical than it appears, because toil is not merely an accompaniment of good things in this world — it is structurally bound to them.

Everything a human being values requires effort that eventually exceeds the value obtained. The body that exercises tires. The mind that studies fatigues. The person who loves carries the weight of caring. The worker who succeeds must continue working to maintain the success. Even rest, in this world, is the cessation of effort and is therefore defined by the effort it follows — one cannot rest without having first been tired, and the rest ends.

Verse 35 describes a state in which the enjoyment is undiminished by the obtaining of it. The inhabitant of the Garden does not work for what he has, does not tire of it, does not defend it, and does not lose it. The commentators note that this is why the descriptions of Paradise include no labour: no planting, no building, no cooking, no earning. The fruit is within reach; the rivers flow without irrigation; the garments are given, not made.

The tradition adds details that complete the picture. Muslim records:

> *"The first group to enter Paradise will be like the full moon on the night of the full moon, and the next group like the brightest star in the sky. They will not urinate, nor defecate, nor spit, nor blow their noses. Their combs will be of gold, their perspiration of musk... Their hearts will be as the heart of one man, glorifying God morning and evening."* (Muslim 2834, from Abū Hurayrah)

Every bodily burden — excretion, illness, aging, sleep — is removed, along with the interior burden of disagreement: one heart among them all.

**"There Is No Life but the Life of the Hereafter"**

The verse's evaluation of this world is stated most directly by the Prophet ﷺ in a report recorded in both collections. At the Battle of the Trench, digging with the Companions, covered in dust, he said:

> *"O God, there is no [true] life except the life of the Hereafter. So forgive the Anṣār and the Muhājirūn."* (al-Bukhārī 2834; Muslim 1805)

The statement was made at the moment of maximum physical effort in the most demanding circumstances of his life — cold, hunger, an enemy army, and a trench to dig by hand. It is not the observation of a comfortable man about the troubles of others. It is the verdict of a man in the middle of exhaustion on the relative reality of the two abodes.

Verse 35 supplies the reason for the verdict. The *dār al-muqāmah* is the only dwelling in which the toil described in that trench does not exist.

**The Fatigue of the Soul**

Ibn Kathīr's reading — that *naṣab* is of the body and *lughūb* of the soul — deserves particular attention, because the second is the fatigue most people actually suffer.

Bodily tiredness resolves with sleep. The fatigue of the soul does not. It accumulates from years of unresolved situations, relationships that cannot be repaired, responsibilities that cannot be discharged, griefs that cannot be processed, and hope deferred. Its distinguishing mark is that rest does not relieve it; the person wakes as tired as he lay down, and often more so.

The Qur'an names this condition elsewhere and treats it as a real affliction rather than a weakness of character:

> *"And We know that thy breast is straitened by what they say."* (Qur'an 15:97)

> *"He said: My Lord, expand my breast for me, and ease my task for me, and untie the knot from my tongue."* (Qur'an 20:25–27)

Moses asks for expansion of the breast before asking for success in the mission, which indicates that the constriction is the primary obstacle. Verse 35 promises its permanent removal: no *lughūb*, no exhaustion of the spirit, no accumulation that cannot be discharged.

**The Verse's Function in the Speech**

Verses 34 and 35 together are a single utterance, and its structure is worth noting. It begins with praise for a removal (*Who has dispelled grief from us*), moves to a praise for a settlement (*Who has caused us to dwell in the Abode of Everlasting Life*), and ends with a description of the settled state (*wherein no weariness shall touch us*).

The order is the order of every genuine relief: first the removal of the burden, then the arrival at a place, then the discovery that the place requires nothing. The inhabitants of Paradise describe, in three clauses, the complete end of the human condition as it is presently constituted — grieving, travelling, and tired.

And the closing quotation mark in verse 35 is the only place in the Qur'an where the speech of Paradise is allowed to finish.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:36

> **As for those who disbelieve, theirs shall be the Fire of Hell. They will neither be done away with so as to die; nor will aught of its punishment be lightened for them. Thus do We requite every disbeliever.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn From Bounty to Requital**

Verses 29 to 35 described the believers in seven verses: their practices, their reward, their inheritance, their adornment, and their speech. Verse 36 turns to the others in a single verse, and the brevity is not negligence but proportion. The Qur'an consistently devotes more space to the reward than to the punishment, and the ratio is itself a statement — the same one carried by verse 7 of this sūrah, where disbelief receives one penalty and belief two gifts.

*Wa-alladhīna kafarū lahum nāru jahannam* — "And those who disbelieve, theirs is the Fire of Hell." The construction is the same as verse 7: the pronoun *lahum*, "theirs," fronted to indicate an allocation that is fixed and specific.

**The Names of the Fire**

*Jahannam* is one of several names the Qur'an gives, and the tradition enumerated them. *Al-Nār*, the Fire, is the most general. *Jahannam* appears seventy-seven times. *Al-Jaḥīm*, the Blazing Fire, denotes intensity. *Saʿīr*, the Blaze — used at verse 6 of this sūrah — denotes kindled flame. *Saqar*, which does not leave and does not spare (74:28). *Al-Ḥuṭamah*, the Crusher (104:4). *Lazāʾ*, the leaping flame (70:15). *Hāwiyah*, the Abyss (101:9).

Each name isolates one property, and the accumulation is not redundancy but description from multiple angles — as verse 27 described colour from multiple angles. The Qur'an does not have a single image of the Fire; it has several, and they are not fully reconcilable into one picture, which the tradition reads as an indication that the descriptions are true and are not exhaustive.

**"Neither Done Away With So as to Die"**

*Lā yuqḍā ʿalayhim fa-yamūtū* — literally, "it shall not be decreed for them, so that they would die." The construction is a negated passive followed by a subjunctive of result: the decree does not occur, and therefore the death does not occur. The verse denies the one exit that every suffering creature in this world possesses.

Sūrah Ṭā Hā states the resulting condition in the Qur'an's most compressed description of it:

> *"Verily whosoever comes unto his Lord guilty — surely his shall be Hell, wherein he neither dies nor lives."* (Qur'an 20:74)

> *"Then he neither dies therein, nor lives."* (Qur'an 87:13)

The two negations produce a state that has no name in ordinary experience. To live is to be capable of change, and therefore of ending. To die is to end. The condition described is neither: an existence from which the possibility of cessation has been removed.

**"Nor Will Aught of Its Punishment Be Lightened"**

*Wa-lā yukhaffafu ʿanhum min ʿadhābihā* — "and nothing of its punishment shall be lightened for them." *Yukhaffaf* is from the root *kh-f-f*, lightness — the same root as *khafīf*, light in weight, and the opposite of *thaqīl*, heavy. The punishment is a weight, and the weight is not reduced.

The Qur'an states this repeatedly and with increasing finality:

> *"The punishment shall not be lightened for them, nor shall they be granted respite."* (Qur'an 2:162; cf. 2:86; 3:88; 16:85)

> *"Every time it abates, We shall increase for them a blazing flame."* (Qur'an 17:97)

The last of these removes the possibility that the verse's negation of lightening is temporary. The Fire does not merely stay constant; when it abates, it is increased. The Qur'an's logic here is the inverse of the logic of verse 30, where the good is paid in full and then increased. There the increase is of bounty; here the increase is of requital, and both follow the same grammar.

**Why No Lightening Is Granted**

The verse states the fact; the Qur'an states the reason elsewhere, and the reason is the respite that has already been given. The disbelievers were not seized immediately. They were given long life (v. 37), sent a warner (v. 37), shown clear proofs and scriptures (v. 25), and reprieved until an appointed term (v. 45). The opportunities were consumed.

Sūrah al-Baqarah gives the reasoning in full:

> *"Those who disbelieve and die while they are disbelievers — upon them is the curse of God, the angels, and mankind altogether. Abiding therein, the punishment shall not be lightened for them, nor shall they be granted respite."* (Qur'an 2:161–162)

The condition attached — *"and die while they are disbelievers"* — is decisive. The punishment is not lightened because the period in which it could have been avoided has closed. Respite was the mercy; its expiry is the justice.

**"Thus Do We Requite Every Disbeliever"**

*Ka-dhālika najzī kulla kafūr* — *najzī* is from the root *j-z-y*, to recompense, to repay, to give the due of an action; *jazāʾ* is the recompense. The verb is neutral in form — it is used of reward as well as punishment — and its use here makes a specific claim: what happens to the disbeliever is a *payment*, not an arbitrary infliction.

*Kafūr* is the intensive form of *kāfir*, from the root *k-f-r*, to cover, to conceal — the one who covers the truth he has seen, and hence the intensely ungrateful. The intensive form indicates persistence and completeness: not one who disbelieved once but one whose disposition became disbelief.

The pairing of *najzī* with *kafūr* states the moral structure of the verse. The requital corresponds to the disposition. God does not punish the one who stumbled; He requites the one who made covering and ingratitude his settled state.

**The Question of Eternity**

The verse's claim — no death, no lightening — raises the question the tradition addressed at length: is the punishment of disbelievers eternal?

The mainstream position of Sunni Islam, based on the Qur'an's repeated formula *khālidīna fīhā abadan* — "abiding therein forever" (4:169; 9:68; 72:23; 4:57 and elsewhere) — is that it is. The consensus of the scholars holds that belief in the permanence of Paradise and of the Fire for the disbelievers is part of the known religion.

Alongside the consensus, the tradition records discussions of a different kind. Reports attributed to some of the Companions — including a statement attributed to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb about the Fire's duration being as long as the sand of ʿAlij and yet having an end — were transmitted and debated. Ibn Taymiyyah and his student Ibn al-Qayyim examined these reports and discussed the possibility of a terminus for the Fire itself, while affirming the permanence of the reward of Paradise and the scriptural statements about the punishment of disbelievers. Their discussion was contested in their own time and remains a minority position.

The honest statement of the situation is this: the Qur'an's explicit texts describe permanence; the consensus reads them as permanence; and the minority discussion has never displaced the consensus. Verse 36 belongs to the explicit texts.

**The Function of This Verse in the Moral Life**

The Qur'an's descriptions of the Fire are not ornamental and they are not designed to produce terror as an end. They perform a specific function in the moral economy of the revelation: they make the consequences of choices vivid at the moment of choice, when vividness is the only thing that can affect it.

Human beings are systematically bad at weighing distant consequences against present satisfactions — a fact the behavioural sciences have quantified as temporal discounting, and which the Qur'an describes as *ghurūr*, the delusion of verse 5. The description of a punishment that cannot be escaped by death and cannot be reduced by time is the counterweight to the delusion. It is not a threat designed to control; it is a disclosure designed to inform, made in terms strong enough to survive the discounting.

The tradition balances it accordingly. The Prophet ﷺ taught both hope and fear as a pair, and the scholars formulated the rule that the believer should travel to God between the two wings: *khawf* and *rajāʾ*. Fear alone produces despair, which the Qur'an forbids:

> *"And despair not of the Mercy of God. Truly none despairs of the Mercy of God except the disbelieving people."* (Qur'an 12:87)

Hope alone produces presumption, which the Qur'an also forbids:

> *"Are they then secure from the plan of God? None feels secure from the plan of God except the losing people."* (Qur'an 7:99)

Verse 36 supplies the fear, verse 35 supplied the hope, and the sūrah places them adjacent — which is itself the instruction.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:37

> **They will cry out therein, "Our Lord! Remove us, that we may work righteousness other than that which we used to do." "Did We not give you long life, enough for whosoever would reflect to reflect therein? And the warner came unto you, so taste [the punishment]! The wrongdoers shall have no helpers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb of Shrieking**

*Wa-hum yaṣṭarikhūna fīhā* — "and they cry out therein." The verb is the eighth form of the root *ṣ-r-kh*, to cry out, to shout; the eighth form *iṣṭarakha* intensifies it to shrieking, calling for help in extremity. It is a rare word in the Qur'an, and its rarity is part of its force — the ordinary verb for calling (*dāʿā*, *nādā*) has been replaced by one that describes the sound of a person beyond self-possession.

The verse thus presents not a request but a scream. The distinction matters, because what follows is a considered plea in articulate words, and the juxtaposition tells us that the articulation comes out of the shrieking.

**The Request: Let Us Do It Differently**

*Rabbanā akhrijnā naʿmal ṣāliḥan ghayra alladhī kunnā naʿmal* — "Our Lord, bring us out; we shall work righteousness other than that which we used to do."

Three elements: the address (*Rabbanā* — the same intimate term the inhabitants of Paradise used at verse 34), the request (*bring us out*), and the promise (*we shall work righteousness other than what we used to do*). The promise is specific — not "we shall be good" but "other than what we used to do," an acknowledgment that the previous work was identifiable and wrong.

The Qur'an records the same request in several places, and the repetition establishes that it is a real feature of the scene rather than a rhetorical device:

> *"Would that we were sent back! Then we would not deny the signs of our Lord, but we would be among the believers!"* (Qur'an 6:27)

> *"Until, when death comes to one of them, he says: 'My Lord, send me back, that I may do righteousness in what I have left behind.'"* (Qur'an 23:99–100)

> *"And they will say, had we but heard or reasoned, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze."* (Qur'an 67:10)

**The Answer: Three Statements**

The reply is given without an introductory verb — no "He will say," no "it will be answered." The three clauses simply appear, which is how the Qur'an renders speech from God in the midst of a scene.

The first: *a-wa-lam nuʿammirkum mā yata dhakkaru fīhi man tadhakkara* — "Did We not grant you a life long enough for whoever would take heed to take heed?"

*Yuʿammir* is from the root *ʿ-m-r*, the same root as *muʿammar* in verse 11 of this sūrah — the one granted long life. The verse thus answers the earlier one: the lifespan recorded in the Book has now been spent, and its purpose is stated. The term of life was not merely a duration; it was an opportunity whose sufficiency is defined by reference to the person who would use it. Whoever would reflect *had enough*. The measure is not a number of years but the presence of the capacity.

The commentators connect this clause to the Prophetic report:

> *"God excuses a man whose life He extends to sixty years."* (al-Bukhārī 6419, from Abū Hurayrah)

The report is cited as an indication of the age by which the opportunity has been ample, and the scholars have read it as a description rather than a limit — the point being that there comes a stage at which the plea of insufficient time is no longer available.

The second: *wa-jāʾakumu al-nadhīr* — "and the warner came to you." This is verse 24 in its final application. The sūrah asserted at verse 24 that no community has been without a warner; verse 37 presents the assertion as a charge in the record. The two provisions were both given: time sufficient to reflect, and a warner who told you what to reflect about. The first is internal capacity; the second is external revelation. Neither was missing.

The third: *fa-dhūqū* — "so taste."

**"Taste": The Verb of Direct Experience**

*Dhāqa* is to taste — the most immediate of the senses, the one that cannot be mediated, reported, or imagined accurately. To taste is to know by contact. The Qur'an uses the imperative in a series of places, always as the answer to a denial:

> *"Taste! Truly thou art the mighty, the noble."* (Qur'an 44:49)

> *"That is for what thy hands put forward, and because God is not unjust to the servants."* (Qur'an 3:182)

> *"Taste the punishment! This is what you used to hasten."* (Qur'an 51:14)

> *"And they will say: O our woe! This is the Day of Judgment."* (Qur'an 37:20)

The choice of *taste* over *suffer* or *endure* is deliberate. The disbelievers were told; they did not believe the telling. What they now receive is the thing itself, unreported. The verb marks the transition from information to experience — which is the transition they refused to make when information was available.

**Why the Request Is Refused**

The Qur'an answers the question explicitly, and the answer is not that God is unwilling to grant a second chance but that a second chance would not produce a different result:

> *"Nay! It is but a word that he speaks. And behind them is a barrier until the Day they are resurrected."* (Qur'an 23:100)

> *"And if thou seest when they are made to stand before the Fire and say, 'Would that we were sent back, and we would not deny the signs of our Lord, but we would be among the believers!' — nay, there has become clear to them that which they used to conceal before. And if they were sent back, they would surely return to what they were forbidden. Truly they are liars."* (Qur'an 6:27–28)

That last clause is the doctrinal core: *wa-law ruddū la-ʿādū li-mā nuhū ʿanhu* — "if they were returned, they would return to what they were forbidden." The promise made in the shriek is not sincere; it is the speech of pain. The person who promises change in order to escape consequence, and who would resume his former conduct once the consequence were removed, is not repenting — he is bargaining.

Sūrah al-Nisāʾ states the juridical consequence:

> *"And repentance is not accepted from those who do evil deeds, until when death comes to one of them he says, 'Truly now I repent' — nor from those who die as disbelievers. For those We have prepared a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 4:18)

Repentance has a term. That term is life.

**"The Wrongdoers Shall Have No Helpers"**

*Fa-mā li-l-ẓālimīna min naṣīr* — "there is for the wrongdoers no helper." *Naṣīr* is from the root *n-ṣ-r*, to give victory, to aid in conflict; the word implies not merely assistance but rescue in a contest. The negation with *min* is emphatic: no helper of any kind, from any quarter.

The clause answers the social structure of the world the wrongdoers left. In this life, the powerful have advocates, the connected have intercessors, the wealthy have lawyers, the noble have kin who act. The verse states that all of these are absent. Verse 18 of this sūrah established the principle — no soul carries another's burden, *even if it be kin* — and verse 37 applies it at the point where help would matter most.

Al-Rāzī and al-Bayḍāwī note the logic of the clause: they have no helpers because they turned away from God and from the help He sent them during their life in this world. The refusal of help given produces the absence of help needed. The warner was help; the long life was help; the proofs and the scriptures were help. Every one of them was refused, and the refusal is what the verse reports as complete.

**The Whole Dialogue as an Extended Discourse**

The source correctly notes that this verse belongs to a larger exchange between God and the inhabitants of the Fire, developed most fully in Sūrah Ghāfir:

> *"And they shall say: 'Our Lord, Thou hast caused us to die twice and given us life twice, and we have confessed our sins. Is there any way of exit?' — 'That is because when God was called upon alone, you disbelieved; but when partners were ascribed to Him, you believed. Judgment belongs to God, the Exalted, the Great.'"* (Qur'an 40:11–12)

That exchange supplies what Fāṭir's verse compresses: the confession (*wa-ʿtarafnā bi-dhunūbinā*), the request for exit (*fa-hal ilā khurūjin min sabīl*), and the answer identifying the cause. The two passages should be read together; each explains the other.

**The Verse's Address to the Living**

Verse 37 is spoken by the dead and is addressed to the living. Its entire purpose is to be heard before it is too late to matter, and the two provisions it names are both available to the reader at this moment.

The long life is running. The warner has come — the Qur'an, which the reader is reading. The two conditions of the plea's refusal are the two conditions of the plea's non-necessity. There is no third condition; the inhabitants of the Fire do not say "we lacked a third thing," because there is no third thing.

The practical conclusion the tradition draws is the discipline of *mubādara*, hastening to good works before the term expires, and the specific warning against the postponement named at verse 5 of this sūrah. The person who says "later" is using the one resource the verse identifies as already sufficient — and spending it on the refusal to use it.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:38

> **Truly God knows the unseen of the heavens and the earth. Truly He knows what lies within breasts.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Knowledge That Makes the Refusal Coherent**

Verse 37 ended with a refusal: the request to be sent back will not be granted. Verse 38 states the attribute that makes the refusal intelligible, and the connection is precise. God refuses because He knows what the requesters would do if returned — a knowledge they do not have of themselves:

> *"And if they were sent back, they would surely return to what they were forbidden."* (Qur'an 6:28)

The person in the Fire sincerely believes he would act differently. God knows he would not. The difference between the two beliefs is the difference between knowledge of the unseen and ignorance of it, and verse 38 identifies which of the two parties possesses it.

**"The Unseen of the Heavens and the Earth"**

*Inna Allāha ʿālimu ghaybi al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ* — "Truly God is Knower of the unseen of the heavens and the earth."

*Ghayb* is from the root *gh-y-b*, to be absent, concealed, beyond reach; it denotes everything not present to the senses. The Qur'an's standard formula pairs it with its opposite:

> *"He is God — there is no god but He, Knower of the unseen and the seen."* (Qur'an 59:22; cf. 6:73; 9:94, 105; 13:9; 23:92; 32:6; 39:46; 62:8; 64:18)

The source notes that Fāṭir's formulation is one of only two places in the Qur'an where the phrase is *"the unseen of the heavens and the earth"* rather than *"the unseen and the seen"* — the other being 49:18:

> *"Truly God knows the unseen of the heavens and the earth, and God is Seeing of what you do."* (Qur'an 49:18)

The difference is worth marking. *ʿĀlim al-ghayb wa-l-shahādah* states the range of God's knowledge across the two categories of the knowable. *ʿĀlim ghaybi al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ* states something narrower and stranger: God knows what is hidden *within* the created order — the concealed parts of the heavens and of the earth. Not merely the categories, but the contents of the concealment inside creation.

That includes what no creature can reach: what is beneath the earth, what is beyond the observable heaven, what is inside the closed room, what will happen next year, what happened before anyone recorded it. The Qur'an makes the same claim about the extent:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the unseen — none knows them but He. And He knows what is on the land and in the sea. Not a leaf falls but that He knows it, nor a grain in the darknesses of the earth, nor anything wet or dry, but that it is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 6:59)

**"What Lies Within Breasts"**

*Innahu ʿalīmun bi-dhāti al-ṣudūr* — "Truly He is Knowing of what is in the breasts." The phrase *dhāt al-ṣudūr* is literally "the possessor of the breasts" — an idiom meaning what the breasts contain, what dwells in the chest. The Qur'an uses it twelve times: 3:119, 3:154, 5:7, 8:43, 11:5, 29:10, 31:23, 39:7, 42:24, 57:6, 64:4, and 67:13.

*Ṣadr* is the chest, the upper front of the torso, and in Qur'anic usage the seat of the interior life — the place where intention, feeling, concealment, and decision reside. The heart (*qalb*) is within it:

> *"Do they not reflect within themselves (*fī anfusihim*)?"* (Qur'an 30:8)

> *"And We are nearer to him than his jugular vein."* (Qur'an 50:16)

> *"He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the breasts conceal."* (Qur'an 40:19)

That last verse supplies the detail: *khāʾinat al-aʿyūn*, the treachery of the eyes — the glance that is stolen, the look that is averted, the momentary expression that is suppressed. The knowledge extends past the deliberate thought to the involuntary movement.

**The Interior as the Object of Judgment**

The verse's placement after the dialogue of the Fire identifies its function: it is the ground of judgment. The judgment described in verses 36 and 37 is not of outward conduct alone, because outward conduct is only partly voluntary and often misrepresents the agent. It is of *dhāt al-ṣudūr*.

Sūrah al-Baqarah states the consequence for the believer:

> *"Unto God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is in the earth. And whether you disclose what is in your souls or hide it, God will bring you to account for it. Then He forgives whomsoever He will and punishes whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 2:284)

The tradition records that this verse distressed the Companions intensely, and that the two verses which follow it — *"God tasks no soul beyond its capacity"* (2:286) — were revealed as relief. The reconciliation the scholars reached is that the accountability of the interior concerns what is settled and adopted, not what passes through the mind unbidden:

> *"Truly God has absolved my community from what their souls whisper and suggest, so long as they do not act upon it or speak it."* (al-Bukhārī 5269; Muslim 127, from Abū Hurayrah)

The distinction is crucial and it is merciful. The intrusive thought, the flash of envy, the moment of doubt, the sudden anger — these pass and are not accounted. What is accounted is what the person *keeps*: the intention he nurses, the decision he makes, the resentment he cultivates, the hypocrisy he adopts as policy.

**Two Effects of Being Known**

The doctrine produces two opposite effects, and both are intended.

For the hypocrite, it is exposure. There is no interior that can be maintained. The person who performs righteousness to be seen has already been seen by the only audience whose seeing determines the value of the act, and verse 10 of this sūrah stated the consequence: the deeds of those who plot do not ascend. The knowledge of God makes hypocrisy not merely wrong but futile — the one strategy guaranteed to produce nothing.

For the sincere, it is recognition. There is a specific loneliness in doing good that nobody notices: the charity given secretly, the sin avoided without credit, the patience maintained without praise, the obligation discharged while being blamed for something else. The human being is built to be witnessed, and unwitnessed goodness is one of the hardest things to sustain. Verse 38 supplies the witness: *He knows what lies within breasts.* Nothing done in the interior goes unobserved, and nothing observed by God goes unrewarded (v. 30).

Al-Rāzī, as the source records, reads the verse in this light as a reminder not to break the covenant either outwardly or intentionally or secretly, because God knows and holds one accountable for what lies within the breasts.

**The Verse as a Refutation of the Idols**

In the immediate context of the sūrah, verse 38 also closes the argument begun at verses 13 and 14. The idols do not possess a date-stone husk; they do not hear the call; they will disown their worshippers. And now: they do not know. God knows the unseen of the heavens and the earth and what lies within breasts; the objects of the idolaters' devotion know neither.

The comparison is total. Knowledge is the attribute the Qur'an most consistently contrasts between God and the false objects of worship:

> *"Say: Do you inform God of what He knows not in the heavens and the earth? Glory be to Him, and Exalted above what they associate!"* (Qur'an 10:18)

> *"And they have taken gods apart from Him, who create naught and are themselves created, and who have no power over harm or benefit to themselves, and no power over death, or life, or resurrection."* (Qur'an 25:3)

**Living in a Known Interior**

The practical discipline of this verse is the one the tradition calls *murāqabah* — the awareness of being observed. It is not the anxious surveillance of a person afraid of being caught, but the settled consciousness of a person who has stopped maintaining a second self.

Most people live with a division between the presented self and the actual self, and the maintenance of the division consumes a great deal of energy. The person who accepts verse 38 discovers that the division is unsustainable in any case — One party to it already knows both sides — and the only available relief is to make the two sides the same. That is the practical meaning of sincerity (*ikhlāṣ*): not performing well for an audience, but eliminating the difference between what is inside the breast and what is outside it.

The verse therefore functions as both the ground of judgment and the ground of peace. *He knows what lies within breasts* is a sentence of terror for the one maintaining a lie and a sentence of relief for the one who has stopped.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:39

> **He it is Who appointed you vicegerents upon the earth. So whosoever disbelieves, his disbelief is to his detriment. The disbelief of the disbelievers increases them with their Lord in naught but odium. And the disbelief of the disbelievers increases them in naught but loss.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Khalāʾif: The Office Humanity Holds**

*Huwa alladhī jaʿalakum khalāʾifa fī al-arḍ* — "He it is Who made you vicegerents in the earth."

*Khalāʾif* is the plural of *khalīfah*, from the root *kh-l-f*, to come after, to succeed, to follow, to represent. The one who is *khalīfah* occupies a position after and in place of another — a successor, a deputy, a steward who acts on behalf of one who is absent or above him. The same root gives *khilāfah*, the caliphate, and *khalaf*, the generations that come after.

The Qur'an's foundational statement of the office is at the beginning of the revelation's account of humanity:

> *"And when thy Lord said to the angels: 'Truly I am placing in the earth a vicegerent (*khālifah*)'..."* (Qur'an 2:30)

And it generalises the office to the whole species in several places:

> *"And He it is Who made you successors (*khalāʾif*) upon the earth, and raised some of you above others in degrees, that He may test you in what He has given you. Truly thy Lord is swift in reckoning, and truly He is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 6:165)

> *"And it is He Who made you successors upon the earth."* (Qur'an 27:62)

> *"And remember when He made you successors after the people of Noah, and increased you abundantly in stature. So remember the favors of God, that haply you may prosper."* (Qur'an 7:69)

> *"And We wished to confer favour upon those who were oppressed in the earth, and to make them leaders, and to make them the inheritors."* (Qur'an 28:5)

The office has three components, and all three are present in verse 39's context. Authority: the earth has been made over to you. Succession: you hold it after others, and others will hold it after you. Accountability: an office held on behalf of another must be answered for.

Sūrah al-Ḥajj states the extent of the delegation:

> *"Hast thou not seen that God has made subservient to you whatsoever is in the earth, and the ships that run upon the sea by His Command, and He holds the sky from falling upon the earth except by His Leave? Truly God is toward mankind Kind, Merciful."* (Qur'an 22:65)

**Disbelief as Abdication**

The verse draws an immediate consequence: *fa-man kafara fa-ʿalayhi kufruhu* — "so whosoever disbelieves, his disbelief is upon him."

The preposition *ʿalā* denotes a burden resting upon the person — the same construction as verse 18, where no bearer of burdens bears another's. The disbelief is not transferred to God, to the community, or to the circumstances that produced it. It sits upon the one who holds it.

The source connects this to the vicegerency, and the connection is the verse's central insight: to disbelieve is to reject one's own office. The *khalīfah* is a representative of God on earth; his whole standing depends on acknowledging the One he represents. The disbeliever does not merely hold a false belief — he occupies a position whose definition he denies. It is the condition of an ambassador who denies the existence of the sovereign who sent him, and who thereby has no credentials, no authority, and no function.

This is why the verse says *fa-ʿalayhi kufruhu* rather than "God will punish him." The disbelief is itself the loss. It is not a crime awaiting a sentence; it is an abdication that takes effect immediately.

**The Threefold Escalation**

The verse then repeats its central claim three times, and the repetition is a rhetorical ascent.

First: *fa-man kafara fa-ʿalayhi kufruhu* — his disbelief is upon him. The statement of liability.

Second: *wa-lā yazīdu al-kāfirīna kufruhum ʿinda rabbihim illā maqtan* — "and the disbelief of the disbelievers increases them with their Lord in nothing but odium."

*Maqt* is a specific word. It is not anger generally (*ghaḍab*), nor punishment (*ʿadhāb*), nor displeasure (*sakhaṭ*). *Maqt* is the contempt and detestation felt toward something that has made itself vile. The Qur'an uses it of the pre-Islamic practice of marrying one's father's wife:

> *"And do not marry those women whom your fathers married, except what has already passed. Truly it is an indecency, and odious (*maqtan*), and an evil way."* (Qur'an 4:22)

The word denotes something that has become repulsive through its own nature, not merely through a penalty attached to it. To say that continued disbelief increases *maqt* is to say that the disbeliever is not merely accumulating a debt but becoming increasingly detestable in the estimation of the One whose representative he was appointed to be.

Third: *wa-lā yazīdu al-kāfirīna kufruhum illā khasārā* — "and the disbelief of the disbelievers increases them in nothing but loss."

*Khasār* is from the root *kh-s-r*, to lose in a transaction, to have one's capital diminish. It is commercial language, and its use here connects verse 39 to verse 29 of this sūrah: the believers hope for *a commerce that will never perish* (*tijārah lan tabūr*), and the disbelievers accumulate *khasār* — the exact opposite. One party's investment cannot be ruined; the other's cannot stop being ruined.

The escalation moves from liability (it is upon him), to relational degradation (odium with his Lord), to material diminishment (loss). Each is a different way of describing the same accumulation, and the triple statement leaves the reader no place to interpret the accumulation as neutral.

**The Grammar of Increase Without Gain**

The construction *lā yazīdu ... illā* — "increases nothing except" — appears repeatedly in the Qur'an and is one of its characteristic ironies. Increase is normally good. Here, increase produces only what is bad, and the more there is of the activity, the more there is of the outcome.

The same construction is used of the revelation's effect on those who reject it:

> *"And We send down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers, and it increases the wrongdoers in nothing but loss."* (Qur'an 17:82)

> *"And whenever a sūrah is sent down, there are some of them who say: 'Which of you has this increased in faith?' As for those who believe, it increases them in faith, and they rejoice. And as for those in whose hearts is a disease, it increases them in filth upon their filth, and they die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 9:124–125)

The same medicine produces health in one body and worsening in another, and the difference is in the body rather than in the medicine. This is the mechanism verse 42 will describe from the other side: *when a warner came to them, it increased them in nothing but aversion.*

**Stewardship and Its Refusal**

The concept of *khilāfah* has become central in contemporary Islamic thought precisely because of verses like this one, and its application to the natural world follows directly from the text.

The vicegerent is not the owner. Ownership remains with the One who appointed him, and the appointment is for a term. Everything the vicegerent does with the estate is done under an obligation to account for it, and the estate must be handed on. The Qur'an states the accountability in the same verse that states the delegation:

> *"...and raised some of you above others in degrees, that He may test you in what He has given you."* (Qur'an 6:165)

The test is in the *given* — in what has been placed in your hands. The person who consumes the estate as though he owned it has failed the office; the person who preserves it and improves it has discharged it. The Prophetic tradition states the principle in its most concrete form:

> *"If the Hour is established and in the hand of one of you is a seedling, then if he is able not to rise until he plants it, let him plant it."* (Aḥmad; the report is widely cited and its chain has been graded acceptable by a number of scholars)

A seedling planted at the end of the world produces no benefit to the planter. The instruction is therefore not about return but about office: the vicegerent cultivates because that is what a vicegerent does.

**The Loss as the Only Certain Outcome**

The verse's final word is *khasārā*, loss, and it is worth noting that the Qur'an treats loss as the default human condition rather than as a special punishment:

> *"By time! Truly mankind is in loss (*khusr*), except those who believe, and perform righteous deeds, and enjoin one another to truth, and enjoin one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

Sūrah al-ʿAṣr states that every human being is losing, simply by existing in time — every moment spent is capital consumed. The exceptions are those who meet the four conditions. Verse 39 of Fāṭir describes the case of the one who does not: his loss is not merely the default loss of time but an accumulating loss produced by the very activity he thinks is securing him.

That is the final form of the verse's warning. The disbeliever is not a person standing still while others advance. He is a person moving, expending, and arriving at a position worse than the one he left — *and the disbelief of the disbelievers increases them in nothing but loss.*

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:40

> **Say, "Have you considered your partners upon whom you call apart from God? Show me what they have created of the earth. Do they have a share in the heavens, or did We give them a book, such that they stand upon a clear proof from it?" Nay, the wrongdoers promise one another naught but delusion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Interrogation in Three Questions**

*Qul a-raʾaytum shurakāʾakum alladhīna tadʿūna min dūni Allāh* — "Say: Have you considered your partners whom you call upon apart from God?"

The verse is a formal challenge, and the Prophet ﷺ is instructed to deliver it. *A-raʾaytum* — literally "have you seen?" — is the Qur'an's standard formula for demanding that an unexamined belief be examined. It occurs more than thirty times, always addressed to something taken for granted:

> *"Say: Have you considered what you call upon apart from God? Show me what they have created in the earth, or do they have a share in the heavens? Bring me a book before this, or any trace of knowledge, if you are truthful."* (Qur'an 46:4)

The parallel in al-Aḥqāf is almost a duplicate, which establishes that this challenge is a settled argument rather than a contextual remark.

*Shurakāʾ* — partners — is used with deliberate irony. The word implies a partnership, and a partnership implies a shared enterprise with defined contributions. The verse asks the idolaters to specify the contribution. The answer, which the verse supplies by asking questions whose answers are known to be nothing, is that there is no enterprise and no contribution.

**The First Question: What Have They Created on Earth?**

*Arūnī mādhā khalaqū min al-arḍ* — "Show me what they have created of the earth." The imperative *arūnī* — "show me" — demands demonstration, not assertion. The object is *mā dhā khalaqū*, "what they have created," and the domain is specified: *min al-arḍ*, of the earth.

The limitation to the earth is precise and it is the minimum possible demand. The idolaters did not claim their gods created the heavens — 43:87 records that they attributed creation to God. The verse therefore does not ask them for something they never claimed. It asks them for the smallest possible share in the smallest possible domain: a single created thing on earth.

The answer, which the Qur'an supplies elsewhere, is that they cannot produce anything at all:

> *"O mankind! A parable has been struck, so listen to it. Truly those upon whom you call apart from God will never create a fly, even if they gathered together for it. And if the fly should rob them of aught, they could not rescue it from it. Feeble are the seeker and the sought!"* (Qur'an 22:73)

The fly is the smallest test case available, and the verse adds a second: not only can they not create one, they cannot recover from one what it takes. A deity who cannot create the smallest creature and cannot resist the smallest theft is not a partner in anything.

> *"And they have taken gods apart from Him, who create naught and are themselves created, and who have no power over harm or benefit to themselves, and no power over death, or life, or resurrection."* (Qur'an 25:3)

**The Second Question: Do They Have a Share in the Heavens?**

*Am lahum shirkun fī al-samāwāt* — "Or do they have a share in the heavens?" Al-Rāzī records two readings of how this clause connects, and both are cogent.

The first: it continues the question about the partners — do the idols have a share in God's powers over the heavens? The first question covered the earth; this covers the sky. Together they exhaust the created order.

The second: it connects forward to the following clause — do the idolaters have some access to the heavens by which they perceive the things they claim? On this reading the question is about the source of their knowledge rather than the object of their worship.

The second reading leads directly into the third question, and the sequence on that reading is epistemological: you have no access to heaven, and you have no book.

**The Third Question: Did We Give Them a Book?**

*Am ātaynāhum kitāban fa-hum ʿalā bayyinatin minh* — "Or did We give them a book, so that they stand upon a clear proof from it?"

*Bayyinah* is the same word used at verse 25 for the clear proofs the messengers brought. The verse is therefore making a comparison: the messengers came with *bayyināt*, *zubur*, and *al-kitāb al-munīr*; the partners have none of the three. The idolaters' claim stands on nothing corresponding to what the prophets' claim stands on.

The Qur'an poses the same question elsewhere with the same conclusion:

> *"Or did We give them a book before it, to which they hold fast? Nay, they say: 'We found our fathers upon a path, and we are upon their footprints guided.'"* (Qur'an 43:21–22)

> *"And We gave them not scriptures to study, nor did We send to them before thee any warner."* (Qur'an 34:44)

> *"Or is there for them a god apart from God? Glory be to God above what they associate!"* (Qur'an 52:43)

The answer in al-Zukhruf is the actual historical answer: not a book, but ancestral practice. *"We found our fathers upon a path"* — the claim of tradition without transmission.

**The Two Sources of Knowledge: ʿAql and Naql**

Al-Rāzī's reading, recorded in the source, organises the three questions into a complete epistemological case. Human knowledge arrives by two routes: *ʿaql* (intellect, demonstration) and *naql* (transmission, report). The verse examines both.

The first two questions address *ʿaql*: what have they created, and do they have a share in the heavens? These are empirical and rational questions, answerable by observation and inference. The answer is nothing observed and nothing inferred.

The third question addresses *naql*: did We give them a book? Is there a transmitted revelation supporting the claim? The answer is no book.

With both routes closed, the claim has no source. Al-Rāzī's formulation is that the idolaters have no way to substantiate their claims through intellect or transmission — which means their belief is not a poorly supported belief but an unsupported one, and the distinction matters. A poorly supported belief can be improved by better evidence; an unsupported belief has no relation to evidence at all.

This is the Qur'an's standard procedure with idolatry, and it is why the challenge is phrased as a demand for proof rather than a declaration of error:

> *"Say: Bring your proof, if you are truthful."* (Qur'an 2:111; 21:24; 27:64)

**"Nay, the Wrongdoers Promise One Another Nothing But Delusion"**

*Bal in yaʿidu al-ẓālimūna baʿḍuhum baʿḍan illā ghurūrā* — the verse identifies what actually sustains the belief once evidence is excluded. Not proof, but mutual promise. Not transmission from a divine source, but transmission among themselves.

*Ghurūr* is the same word as at verse 5 of this sūrah — delusion, the appearance of one thing where another is present. The circle closes: verse 5 warned *"let not the Deluder delude you concerning God"*; verse 40 shows the delusion in social operation, passing from one person to the next.

The mechanism deserves attention because it is the mechanism of every unfounded belief in every era. A claim originates with someone; others repeat it; the repetition is taken as corroboration; and the accumulated repetition is experienced as evidence. Nobody checks the source, because the source is not a source — it is the beginning of the circle. The Qur'an names this precisely at 43:22: *we found our fathers upon a path*.

The word *yaʿidu* — they promise one another — adds a further element: the belief is not merely repeated but *recommended*. It comes with assurances. The idols will protect you; the ancestors knew best; the practice is safe because it is old. Each assurance is a *ghurūr* passed from one person to another, and the verse's verdict is that the entire circulation contains no information.

**The Verse's Standing Challenge**

Read outside its original context, verse 40 is the Qur'an's statement of its own evidential standard, and it applies that standard to itself. The challenge to the idolaters is: show me creation, show me a share in the governance of the world, or show me a book.

The Qur'an claims all three for God and presents the third as its own evidence: creation is everywhere visible, governance is observable in the alternation of night and day and the sending of the winds, and the Book is in the reader's hand. The claim is therefore not exempt from the demand the verse makes; it is the only claim in the passage that meets it.

That is why the verse ends where it does — not with a declaration that the idolaters are wrong, but with a diagnosis of why they remain wrong. They have no evidence and they have one another. A belief maintained by mutual assurance against a demand for proof will survive indefinitely, and the verse is not surprised by this. It is describing it.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:41

> **Truly God maintains the heavens and the earth, lest they fall apart. And were they to fall apart, none would maintain them after Him. Truly He is Clement, Forgiving.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb of Holding**

*Inna Allāha yumsiku al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍa an tazūlā* — "Truly God holds the heavens and the earth, lest they depart."

*Yumsik* is from the root *ʾ-m-s-k*, to hold, to grasp, to retain, to keep from moving or falling. It is the same root the sūrah used at verse 2: *fa-lā mumsika lahā* — "none shall withhold it" — and *fa-lā mursila lahā* — "none shall release it." The sūrah has returned to its own vocabulary: the One who holds mercy open or withheld is the One who holds the cosmos in place.

*Tazūlā* is the dual subjunctive of *zāla*, from the root *z-w-l*, to decline, to depart, to be displaced, to vanish. *Zawāl* is the word for the sun's decline from its zenith at noon; *zāla* of a thing means it ceased to be in its place. The verse describes the heavens and the earth as having a tendency to *zawāl* — to slip from their position — which is held in check.

The claim is therefore not that God created the cosmos and left it running, but that He is holding it, presently and continuously, and that without the holding it would not continue.

**The Same Claim in Sūrah al-Ḥajj**

> *"Hast thou not seen that God has made subservient to you whatsoever is in the earth, and the ships that run upon the sea by His Command, and He holds the sky from falling upon the earth except by His Leave? Truly God is toward mankind Kind, Merciful."* (Qur'an 22:65)

*Yumsiku al-samāʾa an taqaʿa ʿalā al-arḍ illā bi-idhnih* — He holds the sky lest it fall upon the earth, except by His permission. The verb is the same; the object differs slightly. Fāṭir speaks of the heavens and the earth departing from their positions; al-Ḥajj speaks of the sky falling upon the earth. Both describe a stability that is maintained rather than intrinsic.

Sūrah al-Rūm states the principle in its most general form:

> *"And among His signs is that the heaven and the earth stand firm by His Command. Then, when He calls you with a single call from the earth — behold, you shall come forth."* (Qur'an 30:25)

*Bi-amrihi* — by His Command. The standing of the cosmos is described as an effect of a continuous command rather than a permanent structure. And the verse immediately connects this to resurrection: the same Command that holds the world in place will call the dead out of it.

**"None Would Maintain Them After Him"**

*Wa-laʾin zālatā in amsakahumā min aḥadin min baʿdih* — "and if they should depart, none would hold them after Him."

The construction is emphatic on both sides: the *lām* of oath in *laʾin*, and the *in* of negation in *in amsakahumā* — "no one at all would hold them." *Min aḥad* — "from anyone" — is the broadest possible negation: not no powerful being, not no god, but no one.

*Min baʿdih* — "after Him" — carries the same ambiguity noted at verse 2. It can mean after His holding ceases, or after Him in the sense of anyone acting independently of Him. Either reading produces the same conclusion: there is no second holder, no backup, no one who could catch the cosmos if the primary holding were released.

This is the metaphysical statement underlying the whole sūrah. Verse 15 declared that human beings are poor and God is Self-Sufficient. Verse 41 declares that the cosmos is poor and God is Self-Sufficient. The dependency is not specific to human beings; it is the condition of everything that exists.

**"Truly He Is Clement, Forgiving"**

*Innahu kāna ḥalīman ghafūrā* — and the two Names are the point of the verse's conclusion, not a formulaic ending.

*Al-Ḥalīm* is from the root *ḥ-l-m*, whose primary sense is forbearance — the withholding of a response one has the power and the right to make. It is not weakness or slowness; it is deliberate restraint. Al-Ḥalīm is the One who sees the offence, is fully able to respond, and does not respond immediately.

*Al-Ghafūr* is the One who covers — the Name discussed at verse 30.

The commentators — Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, as the source records — connect the pairing to the preceding clause with an insight that is the verse's most remarkable claim: God continues to uphold the heavens and the earth even while the disbelievers declare that others uphold them. The One who is denied is the One doing the holding, and He does not stop.

That is what *ḥalīm* means in practice. A person's continued existence, the continued stability of the ground under him, the continued operation of the sky above him — all of it is maintained by the Being he is presently insulting, and the maintenance is uninterrupted. The forbearance is not a general disposition but a specific, ongoing, physical act.

Sūrah al-Isrāʾ closes with the same two Names in the same context:

> *"The seven heavens and the earth and whatsoever is in them glorify Him. And there is nothing but glorifies Him with His praise, though you understand not their glorification. Truly He is Clement, Forgiving."* (Qur'an 17:44)

The parallel is exact — cosmological stability and universal glorification, closing with *ḥalīman ghafūrā*. The two verses should be read together as a single teaching.

**The Theology of Continuous Creation**

Verse 41 is one of the Qur'an's clearest statements of what the classical theologians called the continuous origination of the world (*tajdīd al-khalq*), and the doctrine is worth stating precisely because it is often misunderstood.

The alternative view — that God created the world and it now persists by its own nature — was held by philosophers who wished to make the cosmos eternal. The Qur'an rejects it by describing persistence as an act: *He holds*, not *He held*. The present tense of *yumsiku* is doing theological work.

The consequence is that there is no moment at which the world exists independently. Its existence at every instant is a fresh holding, and the holding is free — which means it could stop, and nothing in the world would prevent it. This is the same teaching as verse 16 and verse 17: *if He wills, He could remove you... that is not hard for God.*

Al-Ghazālī and the Ashʿarī theologians developed the point rigorously: what is possible in itself (*mumkin bi-dhātihī*) requires a cause at every moment of its existence, not only at its beginning. The verse states the same conclusion without the technical apparatus, in an image anyone can hold: something is being held up, and if the hand opened, it would fall.

**The Practical Meaning of Being Held**

The verse has an application to ordinary experience that requires no theology.

Everything a person considers stable — his health, his income, his family, his country, his own continuity — is being held. Not by him. The experience of sudden loss makes this visible: the person whose health fails in a week, whose business collapses in a month, whose country becomes unsafe in a year, discovers that the stability he assumed was structural was in fact maintained. It was held, and the holding could stop, and it did.

The verse is addressed to those for whom the holding has not stopped, which is everyone reading it. Its content is that the ground under you is not a floor but a hand.

Two responses are available. The first is anxiety: if the hand could open, nothing is secure. The second is trust: the hand has not opened, is not obliged to remain, and belongs to One described in the same verse as Clement and Forgiving. The verse's own ending selects the second. The Names that close it are not *Qahhār* and *Jabbār* but *Ḥalīm* and *Ghafūr* — the One who refrains and the One who covers.

The person who understands verse 41 does not become careless. He becomes grateful, and gratitude — as verse 35 will imply by placing the praise of Paradise immediately after the description of its bounty — is the correct response to being held.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:42

> **And they swore by God their most solemn oaths that, were a warner to come unto them, they would be more rightly guided than any of the communities. Yet when a warner came unto them, it increased them in naught but aversion,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Utmost of Their Oaths**

*Wa-aqsamū bi-llāhi jahda aymānihim* — "And they swore by God the utmost of their oaths."

*Aqsamū* is from the root *q-s-m*, to swear an oath, to take a solemn vow — the same root as *qasam*, the oath-formulas with which many sūrahs open (*wa-l-ʿaṣr*, *wa-l-shams*, *wa-l-layl*). *Jahd aymānihim* — literally "the effort of their oaths" — means the strongest oath they were capable of making, the one that exhausts the capacity of swearing.

The phrase indicates sincerity at the moment of swearing. These were not casual claims or tactical statements. The Quraysh swore by God, with maximum solemnity, that they would respond correctly if given the chance. The verse takes that oath at its stated value and then reports what happened.

**The Content of the Oath**

*La-in jāʾahum nadhīrun la-yakūnanna ahdā min iḥdā al-umam* — "if a warner came to them, they would surely be more rightly guided than any of the communities."

*Nadhīr* is the same word as verse 23 — *"Thou art naught but a warner."* *Ahdā* is the elative of *hādī*, more rightly guided; *iḥdā al-umam* is "any one of the communities."

The claim is comparative and superlative at once: not merely guided, but more guided than any other community. The oath thus commits them to outperforming the Jews, the Christians, and every previous people to whom a messenger was sent.

Al-Rāzī records the historical setting the source preserves: according to some accounts, the Makkans had criticised the Jews, claiming that if a messenger were sent to them they would not deny him as the previous communities denied theirs. Sūrah al-Anʿām records the same claim in God's own citation of it:

> *"Or lest you should say: 'If the Book had been sent down upon us, we would surely have been better guided than they.' Now there has come to you a clear proof from your Lord, a guidance and a mercy. So who does greater wrong than one who denies the signs of God and turns away from them?"* (Qur'an 6:157)

The parallel is close enough to establish that this is a specific historical claim by a specific people, not a generalisation.

**The Structure of the Verse: Two Clauses, One Reversal**

The verse is built as a perfect reversal. First clause: the oath about a hypothetical warner. Second clause: the arrival of the actual warner and the response.

*Fa-lammā jāʾahum nadhīrun mā zādahum illā nufūrā* — "then when a warner came to them, it increased them in nothing but aversion."

Note that the subject of the second clause is identical to the subject of the hypothetical in the first: *nadhīr*. The very thing they swore they wanted arrived, unchanged, and their response was the opposite of their oath.

**Nufūr: The Startle of a Frightened Animal**

The word chosen for their response is precise and unusual. *Nufūr* is from the root *n-f-r*, to be startled, to shy away, to bolt — the word used of a camel or horse that is frightened and runs off, and of people scattering in panic. The same root gives *nāfir*, one who flees, and *tanfīr*, the act of driving something away.

The choice describes a reflex rather than a decision. The idolaters did not consider the warner and reject him on grounds; they *bolted*. The response was at the level of a startled animal — immediate, involuntary, away.

This is a psychological observation of considerable accuracy, and the Qur'an makes it repeatedly about the effect of revelation on those who refuse it:

> *"So what is the matter with them, that they turn away from the Reminder, as though they were startled asses fleeing from a lion?"* (Qur'an 74:49–51)

The image in Sūrah al-Muddaththir is the same word family: startled wild asses fleeing a lion. The person who runs from truth is not described as reasoning; he is described as spooked.

**"It Increased Them in Nothing But Aversion"**

The construction *mā zādahum illā* — "it increased them in nothing except" — is the same one used at verse 39 of the disbeliever's loss and at 17:82 of the Qur'an's effect on the wrongdoer. The pattern is a law: revelation increases what is already there. In the receptive it increases faith; in the averse it increases aversion.

The Qur'an states this in several places and treats it as a regular feature rather than an accident:

> *"And when We expound in the Qur'an that which We expound in the Qur'an, that they might reflect, it increases them in nothing but aversion."* (cf. Qur'an 17:41)

> *"And when it is said to them: 'Prostrate to the Compassionate,' they say: 'And what is the Compassionate? Shall we prostrate to what thou commandest us?' And it increases them in aversion."* (Qur'an 25:60)

> *"And I have called them every time that Thou mightest forgive them, but they put their fingers in their ears and covered themselves with their garments, and persisted, and waxed very proud."* (Qur'an 71:7)

Noah's account in Sūrah Nūḥ is the same phenomenon across a longer period: the call repeated, the response hardening.

**The Psychology of the Conditional Promise**

Verse 42 describes a condition that is not confined to seventh-century Makkah, and its mechanism is worth setting out because it is one of the most reliable forms of self-deception available to human beings.

The conditional promise has the form: "If I had X, I would do Y." Its appeal is that it costs nothing. The person making it is credited with the virtue of Y without performing Y, and is never required to, because X is absent. The promise therefore functions as a moral asset acquired without payment.

When X arrives, the asset is called in. The person must now actually do Y — and discovers that he never wanted Y. What he wanted was the credit for Y under conditions in which Y was impossible. The arrival of X does not enable the good action; it exposes the absence of the intention.

This is why the Qur'an treats conditional promises with suspicion and warns against making them:

> *"And do not say of anything, 'I shall do that tomorrow,' except [with the saying], 'If God wills.'"* (Qur'an 18:23–24)

And why the verse's verdict is not that the Quraysh lied when they swore — they may well have believed their own oath — but that the oath was worthless as a predictor. Sincerity about a hypothetical is not evidence about an actual.

**Al-Rāzī on the Doubling of the Denial**

Al-Rāzī's structural observation, recorded in the source, completes the verse's placement in the sūrah. Verses 38 to 41 addressed the denial of Divine Oneness (*tawḥīd*); verse 42 addresses the denial of the Prophet. Before the messenger was sent, they denied only *tawḥīd*; after he was sent, they denied the messenger and therefore the revelation as well.

The escalation is a general law of rejection. A person who refuses the truth once must refuse it again when it returns, and each refusal adds to the difficulty of the next. The aversion of verse 42 is not the same aversion they would have felt before the oath; it is the aversion of people who have sworn to God and now must either keep the oath or be exposed as false. The stake has risen, and so has the flight.

**The Verse's Question to the Reader**

Every reader of this verse has made conditional promises about himself: if I had more time, I would pray properly; if my circumstances were different, I would give generously; if I met the right teacher, I would study; if things were calmer, I would be a better parent.

The verse reports that such promises are not evidence. The test is not what a person says he would do under different conditions but what he does under these. And the *nufūr* — the startle — is the sign to watch for: the moment when the condition is actually met, and the response is not action but avoidance, is the moment the oath reveals what it was.

The Quraysh swore by God the utmost of their oaths. Then the warner came.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:43

> **waxing arrogant upon the earth and plotting evil; yet evil plotting besets none but its authors. So do they await aught but the wont of those of old? Thou shalt find no alteration in the wont of God, and thou shalt find no change in the wont of God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Things the Aversion Produced**

Verse 42 ended with the aversion (*nufūr*). Verse 43 describes what grew out of it, and it names two things: arrogance and plotting.

*Istikbāran fī al-arḍ* — "waxing arrogant upon the earth." The word is the tenth verbal form of the root *k-b-r*, greatness. The basic form *kabura* means to be big; the tenth form *istakbara* means to consider oneself big — an act of self-assessment rather than a fact about size. *Istikbār* is therefore not merely being great but claiming greatness, and the claim is the sin.

The Qur'an treats *istikbār* as the primary human obstacle to faith, and it names its first instance in the unseen:

> *"And We said to the angels: 'Prostrate before Adam,' and they prostrated, except Iblīs. He refused and waxed arrogant (*istakbara*), and was among the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 2:34)

> *"He said: What prevented thee from prostrating when I commanded thee? He said: I am better than him. Thou createdst me from fire and createdst him from clay."* (Qur'an 7:12)

The first sin in the Qur'an's account of creation is a comparison followed by a refusal. Iblīs did not disbelieve that God existed or that the command came from Him; he declined to obey on grounds of his own superiority. Verse 43 of Fāṭir places the Quraysh in the same position, and the same word.

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī note the grammatical ambiguity of the accusative *istikbāran*: it can be read as a circumstantial state — "it increased them in nothing but aversion, [while they were] waxing arrogant" — or as a cause — "aversion due to arrogance." Some read it with the following noun as a triple object: "it increased them in nothing but delusion, arrogance, and evil plotting." All three readings produce the same picture: the flight of verse 42 was not neutral; it was proud.

**"Upon the Earth"**

*Fī al-arḍ* — "in the earth" — is not decorative. Arrogance is located geographically, and the Qur'an consistently pairs *istikbār* with *fī al-arḍ*:

> *"Those who wax arrogant in the earth without right."* (Qur'an 7:146; 40:56)

> *"Pharaoh waxed arrogant in the earth and made its people factions."* (Qur'an 28:4)

> *"That is the Abode of the Hereafter, which We grant to those who desire not arrogance in the earth, nor corruption. And the outcome belongs to the reverent."* (Qur'an 28:83)

The phrase *"arrogance in the earth"* describes a public, territorial, social phenomenon — not a private feeling of superiority. The *mutakabbir* of the Qur'an is one who behaves as though the earth were his: who occupies it without limit, takes from it without restraint, and treats its other inhabitants as subordinates. Pharaoh is the archetype, and his arrogance is described in terms of construction, faction-making, and the claim *"I am your lord most high"* (79:24).

**"And Plotting Evil"**

*Wa-makra al-sayyiʾ* — "and evil plotting." *Makr*, discussed at verse 10, is contrivance directed at an end the target does not see. The verse describes what the arrogant do with their position: they scheme.

Some commentators read the plotting as directed against the weaker ones — preventing them from following the Prophet — and others as their polytheism itself, and others as plotting against the Prophet ﷺ specifically. All three are historical facts about the Quraysh: they pressured the weak, they maintained the cult of the idols, and they plotted at Dār al-Nadwah to kill the Prophet on the eve of the Hijrah, an event the Qur'an records:

> *"And when those who disbelieved plotted against thee, to confine thee or to kill thee or to expel thee — they plot, and God plots; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 8:30)

**The Enclosure of the Plot**

*Wa-lā yaḥīqu al-makru al-sayyiʾu illā bi-ahlih* — "and evil plotting encloses none but its own people."

*Yaḥīqu* is from the root *ḥ-w-q*, to surround, to enclose, to encompass from every side — the word used of something that closes in. The verse states a law with the precision of a physical principle: the plot encloses the plotter. It does not merely fail; it returns and forms a circle around the one who made it.

The Qur'an states the same reflexivity in several forms:

> *"O mankind! Your tyranny is only against yourselves."* (Qur'an 10:23)

> *"Whosoever does righteousness, it is for his own soul; and whosoever does evil, it is against it. And thy Lord is not unjust to the servants."* (Qur'an 41:46)

> *"If you do good, you do good for yourselves; and if you do evil, it is for it."* (Qur'an 17:7)

The source correctly connects this to verse 18 of the same sūrah: no bearer of burdens bears the burden of another, and whoever purifies himself purifies himself for his own soul. The moral order of Fāṭir is closed and self-referential: every act returns to its agent.

The case of 8:30 is the historical demonstration. The plot at Dār al-Nadwah enclosed its authors: the Prophet ﷺ left, the plotters were defeated at Badr, and the scheme they made for him became the mechanism of their own destruction.

**"The Wont of Those of Old"**

*Fa-hal yanẓurūna illā sunnata al-awwalīn* — "Do they await anything but the wont of the former peoples?"

*Sunnah* is from the root *s-n-n*, to set a path, to establish a way, to smooth a surface. A *sunnah* is a beaten track, an established practice, a precedent that is followed because it has been followed. The word is used in Islamic terminology for the practice of the Prophet ﷺ, and in the Qur'an for the established way of God in dealing with peoples.

*Sunnat al-awwalīn* — the wont of the former peoples — is what happened to them, and the verse asks the Quraysh what they think they are waiting for. The answer is that they are waiting for the same thing, because they are doing the same thing.

The Qur'an references this wont repeatedly: 3:137; 4:26; 8:38; 15:13; 17:77; 18:55; 33:38, 62; 40:85; 48:23. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān states it with the instruction to study it:

Sūrah Āl ʿImrān states the same in almost the same words: the sunnah of God with those who passed away before, and no alteration in the sunnah of God (cf. Qur'an 3:137; 33:62; 48:23).

**No Alteration and No Change: Two Negations**

*Fa-lan tajida li-sunnati Allāhi tabdīlan wa-lan tajida li-sunnati Allāhi taḥwīlā* — "and thou shalt not find for the wont of God any alteration, and thou shalt not find for the wont of God any change."

The two nouns are near-synonyms, and the commentators distinguished them precisely. *Tabdīl* is substitution — the replacement of a thing with something other than it. *Taḥwīl* is transfer or conversion — the shifting of a thing from one place or person to another.
