Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Qurṭubī explain that the absence of *tabdīl* means God always recompenses the disbelievers with their just deserts — the law does not change its content; the absence of *taḥwīl* means no one can avert God's punishment by transferring the recompense due for one person onto another — the law does not change its target.

Together the two negations close every escape. The rule will not be amended, and it will not be applied to someone else.

Al-Rāzī notes the irony in the construction: as if God were saying to those who persist in disbelief, "You wish to follow the wont of those of old? Then God will bring a wont in which there is no alteration or change" — meaning their end will be like the end of those before them. The choice they have made is the choice of a precedent, and precedents complete themselves.

**A Doctrine of Historical Law**

Verse 43 is one of the Qur'an's clearest statements that the moral order operates as a law rather than as a series of discrete interventions. This is a substantial claim, and it distinguishes the Qur'anic view of history from both fatalism and moral randomness.

The law is not mechanical in the sense of being immediate — verse 45 will insist on the respite — but it is regular in the sense of being reliable. Arrogance upon the earth, combined with plotting, produces the same outcome in every generation. The verse invites the reader to verify this by observation, which is what verse 44 instructs: travel and look.

The theological consequence is that God's justice is knowable in advance. A person does not have to wait for the Day of Judgment to learn what arrogance and plotting produce; the record is available in the ruins. And the record does not vary.

**The Application: The Two Habits That Close the Circle**

Verse 43 names two behaviours and one mechanism, and the combination is diagnostic for any individual or institution.

The first behaviour is *istikbār fī al-arḍ*: occupying the earth as though one owned it. In personal terms this is the disposition of the person who treats other people as instruments, who regards rules as applying to others, and who experiences constraint as insult.

The second is *makr al-sayyiʾ*: concealed contrivance against others. Not open conflict, which has its own ethics, but hidden manoeuvre — the plan the target cannot see.

The mechanism is *yaḥīqu*: enclosure. The two behaviours together produce a structure that closes around their author. The arrogant person alienates those who would have warned him; the plotter destroys his own credibility and constructs a web in which every thread leads back to him. By the time the outcome arrives, there is no one left to intervene and no exit left to take.

That is the wont of those of old, and the verse's claim is that it is also the wont of those of now. *Thou shalt find no alteration in the wont of God, and thou shalt find no change in the wont of God.*

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:44

> **Have they not journeyed upon the earth and observed how those before them fared in the end, though they were greater than them in strength? Naught in the heavens or upon the earth can thwart God. Truly He is Knowing, Powerful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Travel**

*A-wa-lam yasīrū fī al-arḍ* — "Have they not travelled in the earth?" The verse opens with an interrogative particle doubled — *a-wa-lam*, "or have they not?" — which produces a tone of reproachful surprise. The question is not whether they have the information; it is why they have not gone to look at it.

The command to travel is one of the Qur'an's most repeated instructions, and the source lists its occurrences: 12:109; 30:9, 42; 40:21, 82; 47:10. Sūrah al-Rūm states it with the comparison fully drawn:

> *"Have they not travelled in the earth and seen how those before them fared in the end? They were greater than them in strength, and they cultivated the earth and built upon it more than these have built, and their messengers came to them with clear proofs. And God would not wrong them, but they were wronging themselves."* (Qur'an 30:9)

> *"And We settled them in the earth more firmly than We have settled you, and We sent down upon them the sky in abundance and made rivers flow through them. Then We destroyed them for their sins, and We raised after them another generation."* (Qur'an 6:6)

> *"Say: Travel in the earth, then see how the end of the deniers was."* (Qur'an 6:11)

The verb *sāra* — to travel, to journey, to set out on a road — is used for actual movement, not metaphorical investigation. The Qur'an is asking its audience to go somewhere.

**"And Observed How Those Before Them Fared in the End"**

*Fa-yanẓurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu alladhīna min qablihim* — "and let them see how the end of those before them was."

*ʿĀqibah* is from the root *ʿ-q-b*, heel, to follow at the heels; the *ʿāqibah* of a thing is what follows it, its eventual outcome, its consequence. The word is used throughout the Qur'an for the final state of a people, and its root implies sequence — the *ʿāqibah* is what comes after everything else, when nothing more can be added.

The verse directs attention specifically to the *end*, not to the career. The strength, the buildings, the power, the confidence — all of that is acknowledged in the verse's own admission that *"they were greater than them in strength."* What the reader is told to look at is what came after all of it.

Sūrah al-Ankabūt makes the observation concrete for the Quraysh, who passed the ruins on their trade route:

> *"And ʿĀd and Thamūd — it has become clear to you from their dwellings. Satan made their deeds seem fair to them and turned them from the path, though they were men of keen perception."* (Qur'an 29:38)

*"It has become clear to you from their dwellings"* — the evidence is architectural. The dwellings of Thamūd, carved into the sandstone at al-Ḥijr, were visible on the caravan route from Makkah to Syria, and the Quraysh passed them twice a year. Sūrah al-Ḥijr describes the same site:

> *"And the people of al-Ḥijr denied the messengers. And We gave them Our signs, but they were turning away from them. And they carved houses from the mountains, feeling secure."* (Qur'an 15:80–82)

The tradition records the Prophet's own instruction when the army passed through al-Ḥijr on the way to Tabūk:

> *"Do not enter upon these punished people except while weeping; and if you cannot weep, then do not enter upon them, lest what befell them befall you."* (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980)

He also forbade the use of the water from their wells, except the well of the she-camel, and instructed that dough made with it be given to the animals. The ruins were treated as an active site of warning, not as archaeology.

**"Though They Were Greater Than Them in Strength"**

*Wa-kānū akthara minhum quwwatan* — "and they were greater than them in strength." The comparison is deliberate and it runs in the direction that destroys the excuse. The Quraysh could not claim that the destroyed peoples were weak and therefore easily destroyed. They were stronger.

The Qur'an makes this comparison repeatedly and always with the same purpose:

> *"Have they not travelled in the earth and seen how those before them fared in the end? They were greater than them in strength..."* (Qur'an 40:82)

> *"And how many a generation We destroyed before them, who were greater in might than they, and they explored the lands. Was there any place of escape?"* (Qur'an 50:36)

The logic is a fortiori: if strength did not save those who had more of it, the absence of strength will not save those who have less. Every claim of security based on capacity is answered by the precedent of greater capacity.

**"Naught in the Heavens or Upon the Earth Can Thwart God"**

*Wa-mā kāna Allāhu li-yuʿjizahu min shayʾin fī al-samāwāti wa-lā fī al-arḍ* — "and God was not such that anything in the heavens or the earth could thwart Him."

*Yuʿjiz* is from the root *ʿ-j-z*, incapacity; the fourth form *aʿjaza* means to render incapable, to defeat, to escape from. A *muʿjizah* — a miracle — is so called because it defeats those who would counter it. To *yuʿjiz Allāh* would be to escape God, to frustrate Him, to place oneself beyond His reach.

The construction *mā kāna Allāhu li-* — "God was not such that..." — is one of strong impossibility: it is not in the nature of the case that God could be thwarted. *Min shayʾ* — "anything at all" — with the partitive *min* makes the negation total. And the domain is specified: in the heavens and in the earth. There is no third location.

The Qur'an states the same in several places:

> *"Know that you cannot thwart God, and that God disgraces the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 9:2)

> *"Or do those who commit evil deeds suppose that they can escape Us? Evil is what they judge."* (Qur'an 29:4)

> *"Say: There is for me no refuge from God, nor any protector apart from Him."* (cf. Qur'an 72:22)

Al-Rāzī reads the clause in context as a reaffirmation that those of old could not in any way escape the punishment merited by their aversion, arrogance, and plotting, despite their great material and physical strength. The verse thus completes the argument of verse 43: the wont of God has no *tabdīl* and no *taḥwīl*, and it has no escape.

**"Truly He Is Knowing, Powerful"**

*Innahu kāna ʿalīman qadīrā* — two Names closing the verse, and their pairing answers the two ways a person might imagine escaping.

*ʿAlīm*, the Knowing: you cannot escape by concealment. There is no hiding place, no secret route, no unrecorded exit. God's knowledge of the unseen of the heavens and the earth was established at verse 38, and His knowledge of what lies within breasts at the same verse.

*Qadīr*, the Powerful: you cannot escape by resistance. There is no strength sufficient, no fortification adequate, no coalition large enough.

The two together close the possibility of escape in both its forms — stealth and force. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the *kāna* of *innahu kāna* indicates a permanent attribute: He has always been Knowing and Powerful, and His being so is not contingent on the situation.

**Travel as a Religious Act**

The verse's instruction to travel has been taken seriously in the Islamic tradition as a form of knowledge. The classical literature on *al-riḥlah fī ṭalab al-ḥadīth* — travelling in search of ḥadīth — produced some of the most extensive travel in the pre-modern world, and the geographers, historians, and pilgrims who followed treated movement through the earth as an epistemic method.

The verse specifies what the traveller is to look at, and it is not scenery. *Kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu alladhīna min qablihim* — how the end of those before them was. The traveller is to examine outcomes. Where a civilisation flourished and is now empty, the question is what it did; where a city stands on the site of a destroyed one, the question is why the first one was destroyed.

This is history as a moral discipline rather than an antiquarian one, and it has a specific method: identify the behaviour, identify the outcome, and check whether the connection is regular. Verse 43 supplied the hypothesis — arrogance and plotting produce the wont of those of old. Verse 44 supplies the verification — go and look.

**The Reader Who Cannot Travel**

Not every reader can go to al-Ḥijr. The verse's requirement is nonetheless satisfiable, because the *ʿāqibah* of previous peoples is available in books, in ruins, in the record of every empire that believed itself permanent. The command to travel is the strongest form of an instruction that applies in weaker forms to everyone: examine the outcomes of the behaviour you are considering.

The person who has never looked at what happened to the powerful who were arrogant and plotted has not been informed; he has not looked. And the verse treats the failure to look as culpable rather than unfortunate: *"Have they not travelled in the earth?"* — with the particle of reproach.

The two Names that close the verse are the reason the looking matters. Nothing can thwart the Knowing, the Powerful. The ruins are not accidents. They are the wont.

---
## Sūrah Fāṭir 35:45

> **Were God to take mankind to task for that which they have earned, He would not leave a single creature upon the surface of the earth. But He grants them reprieve till a term appointed. And when their term comes, truly God sees His servants.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counterfactual That Opens the Last Verse**

*Wa-law yuʾākhidhu Allāhu al-nāsa bi-mā kasabū* — "And were God to take mankind to task for what they have earned."

The particle *law* introduces a counterfactual: something that is not the case, considered as if it were. The verse does not say God takes mankind to task for what they have earned. It says what would happen if He did — and the answer is the annihilation of everything living.

*Yuʾākhidh* is from the root *ʾ-kh-dh*, the same verb used at verse 26: *"Then I seized those who disbelieved."* The fourth form *ākhadha* means to hold someone to account for a thing, to exact from him, to take him to task — the same root produces *muʾākhadhah*, the calling to account, and *ukhuwwah* is unrelated. The verb is judicial: it denotes the enforcement of a liability.

*Bi-mā kasabū* — "for what they have earned." *Kasb* is from the root *k-s-b*, to acquire, to earn, to gain; it is the Qur'an's standard term for human action considered as producing an outcome. The same word appears in the final verse of Sūrah al-Baqarah:

> *"God tasks no soul beyond its capacity. It shall have what it has earned (*kasabat*) and be subject to what it has perpetrated (*iktasabat*)."* (Qur'an 2:286)

And in the verse that establishes the reflexivity of the moral order:

> *"O mankind! Your tyranny is only against yourselves."* (Qur'an 10:23)

Verse 45 of Fāṭir takes the principle to its limit: if the earned outcome were exacted in full and immediately, the sum of human earning would be a sum no world could carry.

**"He Would Not Leave a Single Creature Upon Its Surface"**

*Mā taraka ʿalā ẓahrihā min dābbah* — "He would not leave upon its back a single creature." *Ẓahr al-arḍ* — the back of the earth — is the surface, the inhabited top; the phrase is used in the Qur'an and in Arabic generally for the visible, occupied world. *Dābbah* is every creature that moves upon the ground, the same word used at verse 28 of this sūrah: *"and of mankind, beasts, and cattle."*

The scope of *dābbah* generated the exegetical discussion the source records. Al-Qurṭubī reads it as only human beings and jinn. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Qurṭubī also record the reading of human beings alone. The reading al-Qurṭubī considers most likely — and the one that gives the verse its greatest force — is that it refers to all living creatures, who would suffer from the sins of human beings, as they suffered in the flood of Noah.

That last reference is essential. The flood of Noah was a punishment for human sin that destroyed every living thing:

> *"And Noah said: 'My Lord, leave not upon the earth any dweller from among the disbelievers.'"* (Qur'an 71:26)

> *"Then We delivered him and those with him in the laden ship, and We drowned those who denied Our signs. Truly they were a blind people."* (Qur'an 7:64)

The precedent establishes that divine punishment of human beings has, in at least one recorded instance, extended to the whole of terrestrial life. Verse 45 says this would be the general rule if the reckoning were immediate.

**The Two Mechanisms of the General Destruction**

The commentators propose specific ways in which the destruction would occur, and the source records two. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī hold that it would be by withholding the rain, which would destroy all living things. Al-Rāzī adds that the elimination of the creatures of the earth would itself be part of the punishment for human beings, since creatures are a means of provision — and further, that the verse points to the integration of human life with the life of other creatures, so that the punishment human beings bring upon themselves cannot but affect other forms of life.

The first mechanism connects directly to verse 9 of this sūrah: God sends the winds and drives the cloud to a dead land and revives it. Withhold the wind and the cloud, and the revival does not occur; the land stays dead, and everything on it dies. The same hand that gives the rain could withhold it, and verse 2 established that *"whatsoever He withholds, none shall release thereafter."*

The second mechanism is the more far-reaching observation. Human sin is not contained within the human species. It affects the creatures, the land, the water, and the air — and the Qur'an states this as a general principle:

> *"Corruption has appeared on the land and in the sea because of what the hands of men have earned, that He may let them taste some of what they have done, that haply they may return."* (Qur'an 30:41)

*Fasād* — corruption, disorder, ruin — appears in the land and the sea as a consequence of human earning. The verse in al-Rūm describes a feedback mechanism: the corruption produced by human action returns to human beings as experience (*li-yudhīqahum baʿḍa alladhī ʿamilū*), with a stated purpose — *that haply they may return.*

Verse 45 of Fāṭir states the same doctrine at its extreme limit. The partial taste described in 30:41 is what actually happens; the full exacting is what verse 45 rules out.

**"But He Grants Them Reprieve Till a Term Appointed"**

*Wa-lākin yuʾakhkhiruhum ilā ajalin musammā* — "but He defers them to a term named."

*Yuʾakhkhir* is from the root *ʾ-kh-r*, to delay, to postpone, to put at the end. The same root gives *ākhirah*, the Hereafter — the deferred, the later. The verse thus contains the theological structure of the whole Islamic view of time: this life is the postponement, and the Hereafter is the term.

*Ajal musammā* — a named, appointed term — is the phrase used at verse 11 for the lifespan of the individual, at verse 13 for the courses of the sun and the moon, and throughout the Qur'an for communities:

> *"And if God were to take mankind to task for what they have earned, He would not leave upon its surface a single creature; but He grants them reprieve to a term appointed. And when their term comes — truly God is Seeing of His servants."* (Qur'an 35:45)

> *"And for every community there is a term. And when their term comes, they cannot delay it an hour, nor can they hasten it."* (Qur'an 7:34)

> *"And if God should take mankind to task for their wrongdoing, He would not leave upon it any creature, but He defers them to a term appointed. And when their term comes, they cannot delay it an hour nor hasten it."* (Qur'an 16:61)

The parallel in al-Naḥl is nearly verbatim, which confirms that this is a settled doctrine stated twice rather than a contextual remark.

**The Reprieve as Mercy and as Warning**

The commentators read the respite in two directions at once, and both are correct.

It is mercy, because it is the only reason anything is alive. Verse 45 states that immediate exacting would leave no creature. Every moment of continued existence is therefore a moment of withheld reckoning, and the withholding is *ḥilm* — the forbearance named at verse 41: *Truly He is Clement, Forgiving.*

It is also a warning, because the respite has a term and the term is named. The Qur'an is emphatic that the respite is not indefinite and that its misuse has consequences:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve suppose that the respite We grant them is good for them. We only grant them respite that they may increase in sin, and theirs shall be a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

And it records what the respite will produce at its end:

> *"And warn mankind of the Day when the punishment comes to them, and those who wronged will say: 'Our Lord! Grant us reprieve to a term nigh, that we may respond to Thy call and follow the messengers.' — 'Did you not swear aforetime that there would be no decline for you?'"* (Qur'an 14:44)

The request in 14:44 is the request of verse 37 of this sūrah, restated: *grant us reprieve to a term nigh*. The people of the Fire ask for exactly what verse 45 says everyone currently has — a postponement to a named term — and the answer is that they had it and used it.

**"And When Their Term Comes, Truly God Is Seeing of His Servants"**

*Fa-idhā jāʾa ajaluhum fa-inna Allāha kāna bi-ʿibādihi baṣīrā* — the sūrah's final sentence.

*Baṣīr* is the Name of penetrating sight, discussed at verse 31. *Bi-ʿibādihi* — of His servants. And the *kāna* of *inna Allāha kāna* indicates permanence: God has always been Seeing of His servants, and His seeing does not begin at the term.

The verse's ending is the sūrah's ending, and its choice is remarkable. Fāṭir could have closed with a threat, with a description of the Fire, with a demand for repentance. It closes with a statement about God's sight, and the statement is qualified by *ʿibād* — servants, the term of relationship rather than of judgment.

Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn ʿAjībah read the phrase as an allusion to God's requiting them for all that they have done. The seeing is the seeing of a reckoner. But the word chosen is not *ḥāsib* or *qāḍī*; it is *baṣīr*, and the persons seen are *ʿibād*.

**The Circle the Sūrah Closes**

Read against verse 1, the ending completes a structure. The sūrah opened with praise of the Originator of the heavens and the earth, Who appoints the angels as messengers, increasing creation as He will, Powerful over all things. It closes with the term of the creatures arriving, and God Seeing His servants.

Between the two: the mercy that is opened and withheld (2); the blessing of creation and provision (3); the denial of the messengers and the return of all affairs (4); the two deceivers (5–6); the two outcomes (7); the beautified evil (8); the winds and the resurrection (9); the ascent of the good word (10); the record of every life (11); the two seas (12); the subservience of the sun and the moon (13–14); the poverty of mankind and the sufficiency of God (15–17); the impossibility of bearing another's burden (18); the four pairs of unequals (19–22); the function of the warner (23–26); the colours of creation and the knowledge that produces awe (27–28); the commerce that never perishes (29–30); the truth of the Book (31); the three grades of its inheritors (32–35); the Fire and its dialogue (36–37); the knowledge of the unseen and of breasts (38); the vicegerency and its loss (39); the interrogation of the partners (40); the holding of the heavens and the earth (41); the oath and the aversion (42); the arrogance, the plotting, and the wont (43); the ruins to be visited (44); and the respite (45).

Every one of those themes is contained between the Originator and the Seeing.

**The Verse's Address to the Reader Standing Inside the Respite**

The final verse places every reader in a specific position, and the position is the whole of the practical teaching. You are inside the postponement. The exacting has not happened; the creatures are still on the surface of the earth; the rain is still falling; the term has not come.

Three things follow, and they are the three the verse names.

First, the continuation of the world is not its normal state but a withheld judgment. This is not a call to terror; it is a correction of the assumption that existence is owed. Nothing is owed. The respite is *faḍl*, and verse 35 used the same word for the Garden.

Second, the respite is the only opportunity. Verse 37 recorded what the people of the Fire ask for and do not receive. The named term is running, and it cannot be delayed by an hour nor hastened by an hour (7:34). What can be done in it is exactly what verses 29 and 32 describe: recite, pray, spend, purify, and be among the foremost by God's Leave.

Third, the seeing is already happening. *Fa-inna Allāha kāna bi-ʿibādihi baṣīrā* — God *has been* Seeing of His servants. The term's arrival does not begin the observation; it ends the postponement of the response. The person who waits for the term to take God's sight seriously has misunderstood the tense of the verb.

The sūrah that began with *"Praise be to God, Originator of the heavens and the earth"* ends with *"truly God sees His servants"* — and between the two sentences lies everything the reader has to do.

---

**[End of the commentary on Sūrah Fāṭir]**
